\newcommand\rmnum[1]{{\color{rmNumber}#1}}
% Evidence gaps (user, 2026-11-11): a claim that waits on a measurement is written for both outcomes.
% \evidencegap{state}{what decides it}{text if the evidence supports the claim}{text if it does not}
% state: pending prints both variants in a box and warns in the log; supported or unsupported prints
% only that variant, inline. When the evidence is in, change the word and fill every \tbd of the variant.
\newcommand{\evidencegap}[4]{%
  \ifstrequal{#1}{supported}{#3}{%
  \ifstrequal{#1}{unsupported}{#4}{%
    \PackageWarning{farm}{Evidence gap still pending}%
    \par\medskip\noindent
    \fcolorbox{orange!70!black}{orange!5}{\parbox{\dimexpr\linewidth-2\fboxsep-2\fboxrule\relax}{\small
      \textbf{Evidence pending.} #2\par\smallskip
      \textbf{If the evidence supports it:} #3\par\smallskip
      \textbf{If it does not:} #4}}%
    \par\medskip}}}
\newcommand{\tbd}[1]{\textcolor{orange!70!black}{[#1]}\PackageWarning{farm}{Value to fill: #1}}
\lstdefinelanguage{rmtext}{
  alsoletter={_}, sensitive=true,
  morekeywords={REWARD_MACHINE, STATES, INITIAL_STATE, FINAL_STATES, REJECTING_STATES, ACCEPTING_STATES,
    PENDING_PROMISES, DEFAULT_REWARD, HARD_FAILED_REWARD, SOFT_FAILED_REWARD, TRANSITION_FUNCTION, REWARD_FUNCTION},
  % digits, dots and minus signs are coloured; state names (u0 to u9) and arrows are not
  literate=*{0}{{\rmnum{0}}}1 {1}{{\rmnum{1}}}1 {2}{{\rmnum{2}}}1 {3}{{\rmnum{3}}}1 {4}{{\rmnum{4}}}1
    {5}{{\rmnum{5}}}1 {6}{{\rmnum{6}}}1 {7}{{\rmnum{7}}}1 {8}{{\rmnum{8}}}1 {9}{{\rmnum{9}}}1
    {.}{{\rmnum{.}}}1 {-}{{\rmnum{-}}}1 {->}{->}2
    {u0}{u0}2 {u1}{u1}2 {u2}{u2}2 {u3}{u3}2 {u4}{u4}2 {u5}{u5}2 {u6}{u6}2 {u7}{u7}2 {u8}{u8}2 {u9}{u9}2
}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%              RESUMS DEL TFG EN VALENCIA, CASTELLA I ANGLES                  %
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\begin{abstract}
La construcció automàtica de màquines de recompensa (\foreign{english}{Reward Machines}, RM) en l'aprenentatge per reforç s'ha explorat recentment amb grans models fundacionals, que generen RM directament a partir de descripcions de tasques en llenguatge natural. Tot i ser prometedors, aquests mètodes no oferixen garanties formals de la correcció de les RM i depenen d'una persona o d'un segon model per a revisar-les. En aquest treball proposem FARM (\foreign{english}{A Formal Approach to Reward Machine Generation in Reinforcement Learning}), una cadena de processament basada en la lògica temporal lineal (LTL) per a generar RM: un model de llenguatge traduïx primer les tasques en llenguatge natural a fórmules LTL, que després es compilen en RM amb eines de la teoria d'autòmats. Aquest enfocament produïx RM que, per construcció, estan alineades amb l'especificació LTL, si bé la traducció mateixa encara pot ser errònia. L'avaluem amb dos experiments: un construïx les RM i les comprova davant de la seua tasca sense entrenar cap agent, i l'altre hi entrena agents i jutja si aprenen la tasca. En un banc de proves de tasques amb estructura temporal, el nostre mètode basat en LTL produïx RM més fidels a l'especificació de la tasca que les d'una reconstrucció d'ARM-FM, un enfocament de l'estat de l'art basat en models fundacionals: sense editar a mà cap RM, FARM construïx el 99,6\,\% de les RM demanades i ARM-FM, el 57\,\%. En l'experiment d'entrenament, els agents entrenats amb les RM de FARM aprenen el 72\,\% de les tasques, enfront del 48\,\% amb les d'ARM-FM i del 38\,\% sense cap RM. Aquests resultats suggerixen que la síntesi formal de RM guiada per la lògica oferix una alternativa més fiable als mètodes de generació de recompenses purament neuronals.
\end{abstract}

\begin{abstract}[spanish]
La construcción automática de máquinas de recompensa (\foreign{english}{Reward Machines}, RM) en el aprendizaje por refuerzo se ha explorado recientemente con grandes modelos fundacionales, que generan RM directamente a partir de descripciones de tareas en lenguaje natural. Aunque prometedores, estos métodos carecen de garantías formales sobre la corrección de las RM y dependen de una persona o de un segundo modelo para revisarlas. En este trabajo proponemos FARM (\foreign{english}{A Formal Approach to Reward Machine Generation in Reinforcement Learning}), una cadena de procesamiento basada en la lógica temporal lineal (LTL) para generar RM: un modelo de lenguaje traduce primero las tareas en lenguaje natural a fórmulas LTL, que después se compilan en RM con herramientas de la teoría de autómatas. Este enfoque produce RM que, por construcción, están alineadas con la especificación LTL, si bien la propia traducción todavía puede ser errónea. Lo evaluamos con dos experimentos: uno construye las RM y las comprueba frente a su tarea sin entrenar ningún agente, y el otro entrena agentes con ellas y juzga si aprenden la tarea. En un banco de pruebas de tareas con estructura temporal, nuestro método basado en LTL produce RM más fieles a la especificación de la tarea que las de una reconstrucción de ARM-FM, un enfoque del estado del arte basado en modelos fundacionales: sin editar a mano ninguna RM, FARM construye el 99,6\,\% de las RM solicitadas y ARM-FM, el 57\,\%. En el experimento de entrenamiento, los agentes entrenados con las RM de FARM aprenden el 72\,\% de las tareas, frente al 48\,\% con las de ARM-FM y al 38\,\% sin ninguna RM. Estos resultados sugieren que la síntesis formal de RM guiada por la lógica ofrece una alternativa más fiable a los métodos de generación de recompensas puramente neuronales.
\end{abstract}

% Starts the English abstract on a page of its own (the Spanish one ends at the top of page iv).
\newpage
\begin{abstract}[english]
The automated construction of Reward Machines (RMs) in Reinforcement Learning has recently been explored using large foundation models, which generate RMs directly from natural language task descriptions. While promising, these methods lack formal guarantees of RM correctness and rely on a human or a second model to check the RMs. In this work, we propose FARM (A Formal Approach to Reward Machine Generation in Reinforcement Learning), a pipeline based on Linear Temporal Logic (LTL) for RM generation: natural language tasks are first translated by a language model into LTL formulas, which are then compiled into RMs using automata-theoretic tools. This approach produces RMs that, by construction, are aligned with the LTL specification, though the translation itself can still be wrong. We evaluate it with two experiments: one builds the RMs and checks them against their tasks without training an agent, and one trains agents on them and judges whether they learn the task. On a benchmark of temporally structured tasks, our LTL-based method produces RMs more faithful to the task specification than those of a rebuilt ARM-FM, a state-of-the-art foundation model approach: with no RM edited by hand, FARM builds 99.6\% of the RMs requested and ARM-FM 57\%. In the training experiment, agents trained on FARM's RMs learn 72\% of the tasks, against 48\% with ARM-FM's and 38\% with no RM. These results suggest that logic-guided formal RM synthesis offers a more reliable alternative to purely neural reward generation methods.
\end{abstract}

\chapter{Glossary}
\begin{description}
    \item[ARM-FM] Automated Reward Machines via Foundation Models: the method in which a foundation model writes an RM as text from a task in plain language. In this thesis it is also the method that trains on the RMs of its rebuilt version.
    \item[Clause] One template chosen by the generator, with the propositions it applies to, a priority, hard or soft, and one plain sentence that states its part of the task.
    \item[Compiler] Steps 2 to 4 of FARM. They build the RM from the chosen clauses with fixed tools and call no language model.
    \item[Critic] The second language model, different from the generator. It accepts the result or rejects it with comments, and it never edits the RM.
    \item[DFA] Deterministic finite automaton: an automaton with a finite set of states that reads a trace one step at a time and accepts or rejects it.
    \item[Experiment] One of the two parts of the evaluation. The generation experiment builds RMs and checks them against their tasks, and the training experiment trains agents on them and judges what they learn (Chapter~\ref{chap:evaluation}).
    \item[Final state] A state of the RM in which every hard clause is satisfied. Reaching it ends the episode, and only a task with a hard goal has one.
    \item[Generator] The language model that reads the task and writes it down: as clauses in FARM, as an RM in ARM-FM.
    \item[Goal and rule] A goal is a clause that asks for something to happen, so it stays unmet while nothing happens. Every other clause is a rule: it holds while nothing happens, and a later event may break it or make it wait for an answer.
    \item[Group] One of the eight groups into which the benchmark sorts its tasks, such as the base group, which holds the tasks of ARM-FM, or the shop (Section~\ref{sec:eval-cards}).
    \item[Hard and soft] The priority of a clause. A hard clause must hold for the task to count as done; a soft clause is a preference, and each break costs reward but never prevents finishing. Hard rules and soft rules are the hard and soft clauses that are not goals; where the goal is included, the text says hard clauses.
    \item[Judged episode] One evaluation episode played after training, with the agent taking its most likely action at every step, and scored by the judge.
    \item[Judge] The scorer of the evaluation. It reads each judged episode through the benchmark's own labelling and checks the clauses expected for the task, in the same way for every method.
    \item[Key level] One of the four MiniGrid levels of ARM-FM's tasks, \env{DoorKey}, \env{UnlockPickup}, \env{BlockedUnlockPickup} and \env{KeyCorridor}. In each, the agent must pick up a key to open a locked door.
    \item[Labelling function] The function that reads the environment at every step and returns the propositions that hold, which the RM follows.
    \item[LARM] Language-Aligned Reward Machine, the output of ARM-FM: an RM, the code of its labelling function and a plain-language description of each state.
    \item[Learned] A task counts as learned for a method when at least two of its runs pass, which a run does when 0.8 or more of its judged episodes succeed. A task with no goal and only soft rules is compared with an idle agent (Section~\ref{sec:eval-learned}). A task whose RM was not built counts as not learned.
    \item[LTL and LTLf] Linear Temporal Logic, a logic of properties over sequences of states. LTLf reads the same formulas over finite sequences, such as an episode.
    \item[Method] One of the ways of building RMs or of training that the evaluation compares, FARM and ARM-FM in the generation experiment, and these two and training with no RM (shown as ``no RM'' in tables and figures) in the training experiment.
    \item[Proposition] A fact about the environment that is true or false at each step, such as \texttt{has\_key}. The labelling function reports which propositions hold.
    \item[Re-arming and folding] Two ways in which FARM handles a soft rule. A re-armed soft rule watches for the next break after each one, so every break costs again; a folded soft Absence adds no state to the RM, and its break becomes a loop that costs reward.
    \item[Reward farming] An agent collecting reward again and again without doing the task, for example by going round a rewarding loop.
    \item[Reward Machine (RM)] A finite-state machine that reads which propositions hold at each step, moves between its states and pays a reward on each transition.
    \item[Rewarding loop] A cycle of transitions whose rewards add up to more than zero, so that an agent can collect reward by going round it without doing the task.
    \item[Rewording] The same task, said in other words.
    \item[Run] One instance of an experiment on a task. In the generation experiment it is one build of an RM for one wording of the task, and in the training experiment one training of an agent on the task from its own random start.
    \item[Scripted plan] A program of commands written in advance for a task: one solves the task, others each break a named rule, and an idle one only waits.
    \item[Scripted-plan check] Playing a task's scripted plans through a built RM, to read what each plan pays. No language model takes part.
    \item[Stage] One event still needed to finish the task. Every state of an RM that FARM builds owes a number of stages, and progress pays as that number falls.
    \item[Task] In the evaluation, a task of the benchmark, stored with the answers accepted for it, its rewordings and its scripted plans. A task trained again under a second setting counts as a task of its own.
    \item[Task schema] The clauses of a task with the propositions left open. Tasks of one schema get RMs of one shape, with the names exchanged.
    \item[Temperature] The setting of a language model that controls how random its output is. At zero, the model takes its most likely output at every step.
    \item[Template] One of the eight fixed patterns of IBM nl2ltl, taken from Declare, such as Existence, Precedence or Absence. Each has a fixed LTLf formula in which only the propositions change.
    \item[Thinking level] How much a model reasons before it answers, from none to max. FARM uses max by default.
    \item[Well] The one rejecting state of an RM that FARM builds, which an RM may lack. Entering it ends the episode. An edge leads to it when a step breaks a hard clause or, in a task with a hard goal, leaves the task impossible to finish, and each such edge carries its own price.
\end{description}

\mainmatter

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%                                 INTRODUCTION                                %
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\chapter{Introduction}
\label{chap:introduction}
\section{Motivation}
In reinforcement learning (RL) an agent learns to act by maximising the reward it receives, so the reward decides what the agent learns \cite{sutton2018reinforcement}. Writing that reward is hard. When the reward arrives only at the end of a long task, the agent gets almost no signal while it learns, and reward shaping adds intermediate reward to guide it \cite{ng1999policy}. Reward shaping improves learning, but people write and tune reward functions by hand, which takes time and needs expertise in both the task and RL.

A tool that builds the reward from a statement of the task would save time and open RL to people without that technical knowledge. Formal methods suit such a tool: a task stated in a formal language has one exact meaning, and fixed tools can turn it into a reward in the same way every time. Reward Machines (RMs), finite-state machines that pay reward as the steps of a task are completed, are a natural target, because they expose the structure of the task to the learner (Section~\ref{sec:sota-rm}).

Programming assistants built on large language models (LLMs) can help write code, so they could help with rewards as well. Recent work follows this line and lets an LLM write the RM from a task in plain language \cite{creus2026armfm} (Section~\ref{sec:sota-armfm}). A language model alone is not reliable, though. It can give different answers to the same input \cite{ouyang2025empirical} and state things that are false \cite{huang2025survey}. These approaches rely on a person or on a second model to check the RMs they generate.

A second motivation is to bring AI closer to what people want. A task as a person states it may hold more than a goal: an order of steps, rules that must not be broken and preferences to keep when possible. A reward that captures all of them carries the whole task, not only its goal. The aim is to build such rewards without a human expert, with RMs robust enough not to depend on the uncertainty of the language model. Something to clarify, that robustness is an aim of the method, not a property of every part of it. The model's reading of the task can still be wrong.

This thesis presents FARM, a formal approach to Reward Machine generation that rewards the agent's activities as they align with the user's specifications. The user specifies a task, an LLM transforms the task description into a formalisation of temporal patterns, and fixed tools then build the RM from that formalisation alone (Chapter~\ref{chap:farm}).

\section{Objectives}
The main objective is a fully automated generation of RMs from tasks stated in plain language, on which agents learn the tasks. In detail:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Capture the whole task as a person states it: the goal, the order of the steps, the rules that must not be broken and the preferences to keep when possible.
    \item Build the RM with fixed tools from a formal statement of the task, so that only the reading of the task is left to the language model.
    \item Need no human expert to check or repair the RMs.
    \item Verify the correctness of the method both qualitatively and empirically and compare the results against an approach that generates the RMs with an LLM \cite{creus2026armfm} and against training with no RM.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Expected impact}
The intended benefit is reward shaping and task definition open to people who are not experts in RL, and better learning through RMs that are built formally rather than written freely. The tool is meant for two groups of people, set apart by what they know. The first group knows RL, as the developers and researchers who train agents do. These people can write a reward, and the tool saves them the time of writing and tuning it by hand. The second group knows the task but not RL. These people cannot define the states and rewards of an RM, and the tool asks them only to state the task in plain language. The tool also brings AI closer to people, because a person can state a task in their own words, with its rules and preferences, and the agent then learns from a reward that carries all of them. These are the benefits the tool is meant to bring. The thesis includes no study with users, so they are not measured.

\section{Structure of the thesis}
The thesis starts with the concepts it builds on, where current methods stand, what they leave open and the approach it proposes (Chapter~\ref{chap:sota}). It then presents the new method, FARM, and its limits (Chapters~\ref{chap:farm} and~\ref{chap:limitations}), tests it and shows the results (Chapters~\ref{chap:evaluation} and~\ref{chap:results}). It ends with the conclusions and the open work (Chapters~\ref{chap:conclusions} and~\ref{chap:future-work}). A glossary precedes this chapter, and the appendices hold further examples and the implementation details.

\chapter{State of the art}
\label{chap:sota}
This chapter presents the work that this thesis builds on and where that work stands today. It first reviews the related work, and then examines what the current methods leave open, most of all the approach that generates RMs with an LLM. It ends by drawing the conclusions of the review and by proposing the approach of this thesis to meet the needs left open.

\section{Related work}
Four lines of work meet in this thesis. The first two concern the reward itself, with RMs and with ARM-FM, the approach that generates RMs with an LLM from a task in plain language. The other two concern how a task is stated, through temporal logic and through the hard and soft constraints of planning. The subsections below present them in this order.

\subsection{Reward Machines}
\label{sec:sota-rm}
In reinforcement learning (RL), an agent learns to act in a Markov decision process (MDP) by maximising the reward it receives \cite{sutton2018reinforcement}. Most methods treat the reward function as a black box. The agent observes the number returned after each step and nothing about how it is computed \cite{icarte2022reward}. In practice, however, a person writes that function by hand, so its structure is known and can be exploited to learn faster.

Sparse rewards pose a second problem. When the reward arrives only on completing a long task, the agent receives almost no signal while it learns. Reward shaping adds intermediate reward to guide it. Ng et al.~\cite{ng1999policy} prove that shaping with the difference of a potential function over states, $\gamma\Phi(s') - \Phi(s)$, leaves the optimal policy unchanged, and that no other form of shaping gives this guarantee in general.

RMs address both problems by exposing the structure of the reward to the learner. Toro Icarte et al.~\cite{toroicarte2018using} introduced them at the International Conference on Machine Learning in 2018 and gave a fuller account in the work published at the Journal of Artificial Intelligence Research \cite{icarte2022reward}. This thesis follows that work for the formalisation, counterfactual learning and reward shaping.

An RM is a finite-state machine, written $\langle U, u_0, F, \delta_u, \delta_r\rangle$ in the formulation of \cite{icarte2022reward}. $U$ is a finite set of states, $u_0 \in U$ the initial state and $F$ the set of terminal states, called final states in this thesis. The transition function $\delta_u$ takes the current state and the set of propositions that hold and returns the next state. The reward function $\delta_r$ assigns to each state a reward function over environment transitions. The formulation introduced in \cite{toroicarte2018using} \textbf{attaches the reward to the pair of states of a transition instead}, which is the convention of the RM text format used in this thesis (Listing~\ref{lst:armfm-doorkey}).

Propositions come from outside the RM. A labelling function $L$ maps each environment transition to the set of propositions that hold in it, for example ``the agent holds the key''. At every step the RM receives that set, and its transition function $\delta_u$ either keeps it in its current state or moves it to another one. The agent learns in the product of the MDP and the RM, whose states pair an environment state with an RM state. At each step the environment state changes with the agent's action, and the RM state changes with the propositions that the step makes true. In the product the reward is Markovian even when the task is not.

Exposing the structure of the reward function through the states and transitions of the RM enables dedicated learning methods, which exploit them at each learning step. Q-learning for RMs (QRM) \cite{toroicarte2018using} decomposes the RM and learns, off-policy, one sub-policy per RM state in parallel. Toro Icarte et al.~\cite{icarte2022reward} add three more methods:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Counterfactual experiences for RMs (CRM).} After every step, an off-policy learner receives one experience per RM state, the one the agent would have had if the RM had been in that state, so that one step updates every RM state.
    \item \textbf{Hierarchical RL for RMs (HRM).} The agent learns one sub-policy for each edge of the RM, and a higher-level policy that chooses which of them to follow from the current RM state.
    \item \textbf{Automated reward shaping.} The shaping potential is the negative optimal value of the RM state, $\Phi(s,u) = -v^{*}(u)$, computed by value iteration on the RM (Section~4.4 of the article, restricted to simple RMs, in which $\delta_r$ returns a number instead of a reward function).
\end{itemize}

Using RMs has two costs. The first is their design. Someone has to build the RM, and RMs do not by themselves solve the problem of specifying a reward that leads to the intended behaviour, although they may make temporally extended behaviours easier to specify \cite[p.~199]{icarte2022reward}. The second is the labelling function, which is assumed to exist and to be correct. The methods that learn RMs from experience all assume a correct labelling function, and learning both at once is one of the open problems listed in the article \cite[p.~201]{icarte2022reward}. Both costs motivate generating RMs automatically.

An RM is also a graph, a form that most people are not used to reading, and it becomes hard to follow once it has more than a few states and transitions. This motivates the generation of rewards that are readable and easily interpretable by humans.

\subsection{ARM-FM}
\label{sec:sota-armfm}
ARM-FM (Automated Reward Machines via Foundation Models) is the work of Creus Castanyer et al.~\cite{creus2026armfm}, published at the International Conference on Learning Representations in 2026. It replaces the hand-built RM proposed by Toro Icarte et al.~\cite{toroicarte2018using} with one generated by a foundation model (FM) from a natural-language task description. The output is a Language-Aligned Reward Machine (LARM), made of three parts: an RM, the Python code of its labelling functions, and a natural-language description of each RM state. A text embedding of the description of the current state is given to the policy together with the environment observation, so that the policy knows which sub-goal is active. In this thesis \textbf{ARM-FM is the method and LARM its output}.

The RM is produced by a generator model and reviewed by a critic model over several rounds of self-improvement. A human may optionally approve the result or give corrective feedback. The prompt given to the generator model asks it to write the RM as plain text in a fixed format. In turn, the critic prompt asks, among other checks, that regressions are penalised and that no unintentional loops exist, and the critic answers either \texttt{NO CHANGES NEEDED} or \texttt{CHANGES REQUIRED} \cite[pp.~26--27]{creus2026armfm}. The paper uses GPT-4o to generate all LARM components, except in one environment of its evaluation, where several open-source models are used \cite[p.~4]{creus2026armfm}.

The evaluation covers four environments: MiniGrid (\env{DoorKey} and three harder tasks), Craftium (a Minecraft-based environment where the agent mines a diamond after gathering wood, stone and iron), Meta-World manipulation tasks, and XLand-MiniGrid. On \env{DoorKey} the agent trained with the LARM outperforms the baselines of the paper. On the three harder MiniGrid tasks, the agent trained with the LARM is the only one of the compared agents that solves all three. On Craftium it learns where an agent without the LARM makes no progress. In these three comparisons, the agent trained with the LARM does better than the agents it is compared with. On XLand-MiniGrid a single policy trains on ten tasks at once and solves unseen combinations of known tasks without fine-tuning.

Listing~\ref{lst:armfm-doorkey} reproduces the \env{DoorKey} RM printed in the appendix of the paper, in the fixed text format that the generator writes.

\noindent\begin{minipage}{\linewidth}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=rm,caption={The \env{DoorKey} RM printed by ARM-FM (page 30 of the paper).},label={lst:armfm-doorkey}]
REWARD_MACHINE:
STATES: u0, u1, u2, u3
INITIAL_STATE: u0
TRANSITION_FUNCTION:
(u0, has_key) -> u1
(u0, else) -> u0
(u1, is_door_in_env_open) -> u2
(u1, not_has_key) -> u0
(u1, else) -> u1
(u2, at_goal) -> u3
(u2, else) -> u2
(u3, else) -> u3
REWARD_FUNCTION:
(u0, has_key, u1) -> 0.2
(u1, is_door_in_env_open, u2) -> 0.3
(u1, not_has_key, u0) -> -0.2
(u2, at_goal, u3) -> 1.0
\end{lstlisting}
\end{minipage}

The RM has four states and starts in u0. Taking the key moves it to u1 and pays $0.2$, opening the door then moves it to u2 and pays $0.3$, and reaching the goal moves it to u3 and pays $1.0$. Dropping the key before the door is open costs $0.2$ and sends the RM back to u0. Any other step, written \texttt{else}, keeps the RM in its state.

\subsection{Linear Temporal Logic}
\label{sec:sota-ltl}
Linear Temporal Logic (LTL) is a logic for stating properties of sequences of states, called traces. Pnueli \cite{pnueli1977temporal} proposed it to reason about programs. Its formulas are built from propositions, Boolean connectives and four temporal operators:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $\mathrm{X}\,\varphi$ (next): $\varphi$ holds at the next step.
    \item $\mathrm{F}\,\varphi$ (eventually): $\varphi$ holds at the current step or at a later one.
    \item $\mathrm{G}\,\varphi$ (always): $\varphi$ holds at every step from the current one on.
    \item $\varphi\ \mathrm{U}\ \psi$ (until): $\psi$ holds at some step from the current one on, and $\varphi$ holds at every step before it.
\end{itemize}
LTL is defined over infinite traces, whereas RL tasks in this work are defined over finite episodes. LTLf, LTL over finite traces \cite{degiacomo2013linear}, fits such episodes. It keeps the syntax and reads a formula over a trace of finite length, where next requires a following step to exist. LTLf is as expressive as first-order logic over finite traces, which is less than what regular expressions can describe.

For example, in the \env{DoorKey} task, where the agent takes a key, opens a door and reaches a goal, the propositions are \texttt{has\_key}, \texttt{door\_open} and \texttt{at\_goal}. 

\begin{itemize}
    \item  \textit{The agent reaches the goal at some point} is
    \[ \mathrm{F}\ \texttt{at\_goal}, \]
    \item \textit{the door is not open before the key is held} is
    \[ (\neg\,\texttt{door\_open}\ \mathrm{U}\ \texttt{has\_key}) \lor \mathrm{G}\,\neg\,\texttt{door\_open}. \]
\end{itemize}

For every LTLf formula, such as these two, there is a deterministic finite automaton (DFA) that accepts exactly the traces satisfying it \cite{degiacomo2013linear}, and tools exist that build this DFA from the formula.

On this basis, Camacho et al.~\cite{camacho2019ltl} bring LTLf to RL. They show that RMs can express reward specifications written in LTL, LTLf and other formal languages, translate a specification into an RM and combine several specifications with a product construction.\footnote{The product of two graphs has one state for each pair of states, one from each graph, and follows both graphs at once. At each step, each graph moves along its own edge, and the product moves to the pair of their new states.}

LTL formulas can also be built from a catalogue of fixed patterns. For instance, \texttt{Declare} is a language to specify business processes declaratively. It defines a catalogue of templates, such as Existence, Response or Precedence, each with a meaning in LTL. Pesic and van der Aalst introduced it under the name ConDec \cite{pesic2006declarative}.

\subsection{Hard and soft constraints in planning}
\label{sec:sota-preferences}
In automated planning, a plan is a sequence of actions that takes a system from an initial state to a goal, and the Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) is the standard language to describe such problems. PDDL3 \cite{gerevini2006preferences} adds two elements to it. The first element is the state trajectory constraints, which are conditions on the whole sequence of states that the plan goes through, not only on the last one. They are written with operators such as \texttt{always}, \texttt{sometime}, \texttt{at-most-once} and \texttt{sometime-before}, close to those of LTL. The second element is the preferences, which are soft constraints. A valid plan must satisfy every hard constraint, while each preference it breaks only adds a cost to the plan. Goals and trajectory constraints can both be stated as preferences, so a condition over time can be either required or only preferred.

\section{Critique of the state of the art}
\label{sec:critique}
The methods reviewed above leave several problems open. The four problems that this section examines concern ARM-FM, the method that this thesis compares against. 

The tasks that ARM-FM is evaluated on are narrow, its generation is neither consistent nor structured, its RMs partially depend on a human to verify them, and its exact outputs cannot be reproduced.

\subsection{Narrow task variety}
The tasks that ARM-FM evaluates are simple in what they ask: most are a list of steps that ends in one goal. 

For instance, in \env{DoorKey} the agent ``finds a key'', ``unlocks a door'' and ``reaches the goal''; in \env{BlockedUnlockPickup} it ``moves a ball out of the way'', ``takes a key'', ``unlocks a door'' and ``picks up a box''; in Craftium it gathers ``wood'', ``stone'', ``iron'' and ``diamond'' in this order; etc. Only its MiniGrid tasks are long and hard to explore, as the paper stresses \cite[pp.~5, 14]{creus2026armfm}, but what they ask is still such a list. 

Each RM the paper shows is a chain of steps the agent has to follow, beyond that, no task states a rule that must hold throughout the episode, and none separates what must happen from what is only preferable. 

\subsection{Inconsistent and unstructured generation of RMs}
In ARM-FM a language model writes the whole RM with just a few guides, and two things together make this a problem. First, a language model does not return the same output on every run, even with its temperature set to zero, as Ouyang et al.~\cite{ouyang2025empirical} measured for ChatGPT. Second, almost nothing constrains what the model writes. The RM is plain text in a format described by the prompt, and the model chooses every reward value freely. The same task can therefore yield a different RM on every run, and nothing limits how different those RMs can be.

Free reward values are a risk, because an agent learns to collect as much reward as it can, whether or not that is what the task asks for. If the rewards around a cycle of the RM add up to more than zero, the agent can go round it again and again and earn reward on every lap without getting any closer to finishing the task. This is called reward farming, and such a cycle is a rewarding loop. ARM-FM's own guarantee, say that an agent which maximises the reward of the RM also solves the original task, but this holds only if the RM has no rewarding loop that allows farming \cite[p.~21]{creus2026armfm}. 

Nothing in ARM-FM enforces this condition. The paper describes no automatic check of it and leaves it to the critic, whose prompt asks the model to look for unintentional loops \cite[p.~27]{creus2026armfm}. The critic is itself a language model, and language models can state things that are false \cite{huang2025survey}, so its approval is \textbf{not a robust check}. The condition does fail in the paper itself. The paper states that its RMs have no rewarding loop \cite[p.~21]{creus2026armfm}, yet the Meta-World RM it prints has a cycle of three transitions that leaves $+0.20$ on every lap \cite[p.~32]{creus2026armfm}.

\subsection{Verification by a human or another model}
ARM-FM states the limitation itself: human verification during RM generation is a trade-off that presupposes access to human verifiers. The authors add that such verifiers are not strictly required, although they can improve the output, and leave reducing this dependence by exploiting the automaton structure, for example through formal verification, as future work \cite[pp.~9--10]{creus2026armfm}. 

In practice, 3 of the 7 settings that the paper reports needed a human to correct or extend the RM \cite[p.~20]{creus2026armfm}. In two of them, the RM that the model generated was missing something the task needed. In one MiniGrid task it had no transition for the agent dropping the key after picking it up, and a human had to ask the model to add it. In another its rewards were too sparse, and a human had to advise the model to define intermediate rewards, such as one for crossing a door. In both, the model wrote the change only after a person had pointed out what was missing, which implies the need of an expert reviewing the process.

\subsection{Reproducibility}
The ARM-FM paper releases no code. Instead, its appendices print the generator and critic prompts, six RMs, the labelling functions of four MiniGrid tasks and the hyperparameters. A public repository of the first author \cite{armfm_code}, which the paper does not reference, adds prompts, generated RMs, labelling code and a training script for four MiniGrid tasks only. This material allows the method to be rebuilt but not its exact outputs, so the ARM-FM baseline of this thesis is a reconstruction whose results may differ from the original ones. The authors did not answer the contact attempts made for further material.

\section{Conclusions and proposed approach}
\label{sec:proposal}
Now that the review is presented, two ideas stand out as worth keeping: an RM gives the reward a structure that the learner can use, and ARM-FM lets a language model write the RM, so that no one has to build it by hand. 

Now, ARM-FM is not yet the final solution. As Section~\ref{sec:critique} shows, three problems in how it writes the RM are still. This thesis takes each problem as an aim.

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Narrow tasks.} The tasks of ARM-FM are lists of steps that end in one goal. The first aim is to capture the whole task, with its order, rules and preferences.
    \item \textbf{Human verification.} A person or a second model has to check each RM. The second aim is an RM that needs no expert.
    \item \textbf{Free generation.} The model writes a different RM on every run, with nothing to constrain it. The third aim is that tasks of the same kind get the same kind of RM.
\end{itemize}

To meet these aims, this thesis uses the other two lines of work reviewed: LTLf and the hard and soft constraints of planning. The three subsections below present one aim each, and Chapter~\ref{chap:farm} presents the method built for them.

\subsection{Broader tasks, with rules and preferences}
The tasks that ARM-FM is evaluated on are lists of steps that end in one goal (Section~\ref{sec:critique}). A person may want to tell the agent more than that, because a task is rarely a single chain of steps. 

A person says, in plain language, what to do, in which order, what to do in response to an event and what must never happen. Thus, the first aim is a reward that captures the whole of such a task. This matters because an RL agent pursues whatever its reward pays for, so the reward is the way a person tells the agent what to achieve \cite{sutton2018reinforcement}. 

More broadly, AI alignment can be brought into the table, given that it aims to make AI systems behave in line with human intentions and values \cite{ji2025alignment}. Again, alignment is a reward that captures the whole task as the person states it.

Capturing the whole task means catching these \textbf{relations of order}, \textbf{response and prohibition}, together with the person's \textbf{preferences}, in a \textbf{shape from which a reward can be built}. That needs a translator from human intent into a language that encodes such relations exactly, a part that a foundation model can play.

It also means keeping apart what must hold and what is only preferred. Each statement of the task is either hard or soft, where a hard statement must hold for the task to count as done, while a soft statement is a preference, which the agent may give up at a cost. This split between constraints and preferences comes from planning (Section~\ref{sec:sota-preferences}), and the aim is to carry it into RMs.

\subsection{RMs without a human expert}
ARM-FM relies on a human or on a second model to verify its RMs, and its authors name human verification as a trade-off of their approach (Section~\ref{sec:critique}). As we stated, the second aim is generation without a human expert. A person states the task, and no one who knows RMs or reinforcement learning has to check or repair the output RM.

That is possible only if an RM is safe to train on without being reviewed. The properties that make it safe, such as having no rewarding loop, should therefore \textbf{follow from how the RM is built}, and not from a reviewer who may miss them.

\subsection{Same kind of task, same kind of RM}
\label{sec:schemas}
A language model that writes an RM freely may write a different one on every run (Section~\ref{sec:critique}). Since that is a problem inherent to LLMs, the third aim tries to attack it by making tasks of the same kind receive the same kind of RM, so that the result is predictable.

Two tasks are of the same kind when they share a \texttt{schema}. For example, \textit{gather wood, then stone, then iron} and \textit{attend the client, prepare the order, then serve it} share the schema ``first $a$, then $b$, then $c$''. Their RMs should then have the same shape, with only the objects exchanged.

The one thing that can still vary is how the language model reads the task, so this is an aim and not a guarantee. Tasks of the same kind get the same kind of RM only when the model reads them into the same schema. Chapter~\ref{chap:results} measures the closest case, whether the rewordings of one task get the same RM (Section~\ref{sec:res-clauses}).

\chapter{FARM}
\label{chap:farm}
% \section{Introduction and motivation}
This chapter describes the process by which FARM (A Formal Approach to Reward Machine Generation) turns a task written in plain language into an RM. More implementation details, with the list of what FARM adds to the tools it builds on, are in Appendix~\ref{app:implementation}.
% Each aim of Section~\ref{sec:proposal} rests on one tool:
% \begin{itemize}
%     \item A language model reads the task and the environment description, from which captures the whole task as the person states it, with its goal, the order of its steps, its rules and its preferences.
%     \item The Declare templates of nl2ltl, an IBM tool that goes from natural language to LTLf, guide the LLM. It picks a template and fills it to form a clause, and the template writes the formula.
%     \item LTLf encodes these statements as formulas over finite traces (Section~\ref{sec:sota-ltl}).
%     \item The Formal Language--Automaton Transmogrifier (FL-AT) and MONA, two tools for logic and automata, convert each formula into an automaton and combine the automata into one product.
%     \item FARM's post-process builds the RM from the chosen clauses alone.
%     \item A second language model, the critic, reviews the clauses against the task.
% \end{itemize}
% Together these tools form the pipeline of FARM, whose five steps use them in this order.

\section{Global overview of the pipeline}
\label{sec:overview}
FARM is a \textbf{framework for generating Reward Machines from natural-language tasks}, which extends from the existing tools and methodologies and is based on the approach used by ARM-FM. The framework consists of five main steps, which can be summarised as follows.

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Step 1: task interpretation and formalisation.} A language model reads, understands and decomposes the task into a set of what are called \textit{clauses}. Clauses are short statements that express a single aspect of the task that must be satisfied, each with a hard or soft label. Since the ones used by FARM follow a predefined structure, they are a closed set, which is easy to translate each via mapping into a formal representation, in this case LTLf formulas.
    \item \textbf{Step 2: formalisation to automata.} Each formula is compiled into an automaton (DFA), using a formal second-order method based on automata decision procedures. The resulting automaton accepts exactly, those traces that satisfy the corresponding formula.
    \item \textbf{Step 3: the product.} Then, all the automata are combined into a single automaton, using the product construction, a standard method in automata theory that walks through the states of each automaton and combines them. The resulting product automaton accepts exactly those traces that satisfy all the formulas, and therefore all the clauses.
    \item \textbf{Step 4: the post-process.} At this point, the product automaton is not yet a reliable reward machine. Thus, FARM applies a post-processing step where adds final states, simplifies parts of the resulting product, and assigns rewards on the transitions to ensure proper reward shaping. 
    \item \textbf{Step 5: the critic.} Given that the first step of this pipeline relies on an LLM, a second reviews that the final RM does match the original task demanded. If it doesn't, the RM is rejected and the critic provides feedback so the generator of the first step can try again. This critic gets to judge the clauses more that the final RM, as those are what the first model has chosen.
\end{itemize}

To clarify further what FARM does in comparison to ARM-FM (Section~\ref{sec:sota-armfm}), it can be stated that out of the different innovations that ARM-FM introduced: LLM based RM machine generation, LLM based labeling function generation, RM state descrition and representation; FARM focuses on the first one, the generation of RMs from natural language tasks, and does not address the other three.


Notice that Steps 2 to 4 form the \textbf{compiler}. They call no language model and read only the LTLf formulas and convert them into an RM. They form the deterministic part of the pipeline, therefore making the two language models of Steps 1 and 5 the only source of variability. 


% It does so in five steps: task interpretation and formalizaton (Step~1), that form is used to get its automata representation (Step~2), the automatas are combined into a single automaton i.e. the RM (Step~3), the post-process adjusts and verifies the RM (Step~4) and the critic gives a last review (Step~5). Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline} draws the pipeline.

\subsection{Running examples}
\label{sec:running-examples}
To make the process easy to follow, three tasks in the RL \env{DoorKey} environment used on the base paper (ARM-FM), run through the chapter as examples of what each step accomplishes. These are the tasks:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \runex{Sequence}: \textit{Pick up the key, then open the door, then reach the goal.}
    \item \runex{Dropped key}: \textit{Reach the goal, and if you drop the key, pick it up right away.}
    \item \runex{Careful key}: \textit{Reach the goal, ideally without ever losing the key.}
\end{itemize}

The environment provides four events (that can be call propositions more formally): \texttt{has\_key}, \texttt{door\_open}, \texttt{at\_goal} and \texttt{key\_lost}. These events will be used by the Generator to convert each tasks into the corresponding clauses.

Now, we present all the steps in more detail, and each ends with the part of the examples it needs. Appendix~\ref{app:running} gives the three RMs in full.

\section{Step 1 · Task interpretation and formalisation}
\label{sec:step1}
In the first step a language model, the \textbf{generator}, reads the task and the environment description and writes the task as clauses (Figure~\ref{fig:step1}). A \textbf{clause} names one of eight \textbf{templates}, the \textbf{propositions} it applies to and a \textbf{priority, hard or soft}, and states its part of the task in one plain sentence. The answer is restricted to this form, with at most 25 clauses, and only the template, the propositions and the priority reach the compiler. The template of each clause then writes its LTLf formula.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[farm, node distance=7mm,
    field/.style={shape=farm slant, draw=orange!60!black, fill=orange!40, align=center, text width=1.9cm, minimum height=4mm, inner sep=1pt}]
\node[farm text, text width=2.1cm, minimum height=7.5mm] (task) at (0,0.55) {Task};
\node[farm text, text width=2.1cm, minimum height=7.5mm] (env) at (0,-0.55) {Environment description};
\node[farm llm, farm step=1.1, text width=2cm] (gen) at (3.3,0) {Language model};
% one clause: a stacked card titled Clause that holds its three fields
\node[font=\scriptsize\bfseries] (cltitle) at (6.2,0.66) {Clause};
\node[field] (f1) at (6.2,0.24) {Template};
\node[field] (f2) at (6.2,-0.2) {Propositions};
\node[field] (f3) at (6.2,-0.64) {Hard or soft};
\begin{scope}[on background layer]
\node[farm items, shape=rectangle, text width=, fit=(cltitle) (f1) (f2) (f3), inner sep=3pt] (cl) {};
\end{scope}
\node[farm tool, farm step=1.2, text width=2cm, right=of cl] (ibm) {IBM templates};
\node[farm items, text width=1.8cm, right=of ibm] (ltl) {LTLf formula};
\draw[farm flow] (task.east) -- (gen.west);
\draw[farm flow] (env.east) -- (gen.west);
\draw[farm flow] (gen.east) -- (gen.east -| cl.west);
\draw[farm flow] (cl) -- (ibm);
\draw[farm flow] (ibm) -- (ltl);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption[Step 1 of FARM]{Step 1. The language model chooses the clauses, each a template with its propositions and a priority, and the template of each clause writes its LTLf formula.}
\label{fig:step1}
\end{figure}

FARM assumes that the task it receives can be done. Everything the task speaks of is a proposition that the environment description declares and that can become true in the environment, and the clauses that state the task do not contradict each other. Section~\ref{sec:lim-anomalies} describes what happens to a task that breaks this assumption, and letting the generator refuse such a task is future work (Section~\ref{sec:fw-refusal}).

\subsection{IBM nl2ltl}
IBM's nl2ltl \cite{fuggitti2023nl2ltl} goes from natural language to LTLf through templates. A language model chooses which templates to use and which propositions fill them, and each template maps to a fixed LTLf formula in which only the propositions change. 

All the work therefore lies in the choice. A template is a fixed pattern with holes, and the model only says \textbf{which pattern to use and what goes in the holes}. The choice is still hard, since the model has to understand the task, its order and the dependencies between its steps, rules and goals. 

FARM keeps the template classes of nl2ltl, improves its performance with prompting, and replaces its engine (Appendix~\ref{app:implementation}) so other LLMs can be used. FARM's prompts were tuned on a set of tasks kept apart from the ones the evaluation scores (Chapter~\ref{chap:evaluation}), and Appendix~\ref{app:res-prompts} gives the prompt versions and their scores.

\subsection{Templates}
Each clause that the generator writes uses one of the templates of nl2ltl, fixed patterns with holes. Table~\ref{tab:templates} lists the eight templates with their LTLf definitions in IBM's library, and its last column gives the kind of each, goal or rule, which the next subsection explains.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{4.6cm}l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Template} & \textbf{LTLf} & \textbf{Reads as} & \textbf{Kind} \\ \midrule
Existence$(a)$ & $\mathrm{F}\,a$ & $a$ happens at some point & goal \\
ExistenceTwo$(a)$ & $\mathrm{F}\,(a \land \mathrm{X}\,\mathrm{F}\,a)$ & $a$ happens at least twice & goal \\
Absence$(a)$ & $\neg\,\mathrm{F}\,a$ & $a$ never happens & rule \\
RespondedExistence$(a,b)$ & $\mathrm{F}\,a \rightarrow \mathrm{F}\,b$ & if $a$ ever happens, $b$ happens too & rule \\
Response$(a,b)$ & $\mathrm{G}\,(a \rightarrow \mathrm{F}\,b)$ & every $a$ is answered by $b$ at the same step or later & rule \\
Precedence$(a,b)$ & $(\neg b\ \mathrm{U}\ a) \lor \mathrm{G}\,\neg b$ & $b$ cannot happen before $a$ & rule \\
ChainResponse$(a,b)$ & $\mathrm{G}\,(a \rightarrow \mathrm{X}\,b)$ & every $a$ is followed by $b$ at the next step & rule \\
NotCoExistence$(a,b)$ & $\mathrm{F}\,a \rightarrow \neg\,\mathrm{F}\,b$ & if $a$ ever happens, $b$ never does & rule \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The eight Declare templates of IBM nl2ltl with their LTLf definition and kind.}
\label{tab:templates}
\end{table}

The formulas use four operators (Section~\ref{sec:sota-ltl}): $\textbf{F}\,a$ means that $a$ holds at some step from now on, $\textbf{G}\,a$ that $a$ holds at every step from now on, $\textbf{X}\,a$ that $a$ holds at the next step, and $a\ \textbf{U}\ b$ that $b$ holds at some step and $a$ holds at every step before it.

Response and Precedence are easy to confuse, and both allow the same step. Response$(a,b)$ lets $b$ appear without $a$, but \textbf{after} an $a$ a $b$ must follow, at the same step or later. Precedence$(a,b)$ lets $a$ appear without $b$, but a $b$ needs an $a$ \textbf{before} it or at the same step.

\subsection{Goals and rules}
A clause is a \textbf{goal} if it asks for something to happen, and a \textbf{rule} if it only limits what may happen. One test tells them apart. After a step in which \textbf{nothing happens}, that is, no proposition holds, a goal is not satisfied yet, while a rule is. 

For example, Existence and ExistenceTwo are goals since by doing nothing, they cannot be satisfied. The other six templates are rules, Precedence included. They hold while nothing happens, and a later event may break them or make them wait for an answer. For example, if $b$ happens before $a$, Precedence$(a,b)$ is broken, but if nothing happens, it still holds.

The compiler applies the test to the DFA of each clause, so it needs no list of templates. The difference shapes Step~4. Only a hard goal gives the RM a final state, and the RM pays the agent as it gets closer to that state.

\subsection{The generator prompt}
IBM's default prompt is a few-shot list that offers five of the eight templates, with examples from business applications such as Slack or Salesforce, no definitions and no description of the environment. \textbf{FARM's prompt offers all eight}, adding Absence, Precedence and NotCoExistence. It gives the model the catalogue with:

\begin{itemize}
    \item The meaning, kind and formula of each template;
    \item A table from typical wording to template;
    \item Worked examples;
    \item The instructions for hard and soft;
    \item The \textbf{environment description} in Markdown;
    \item The \textbf{task};
    \item On a retry, the comments of the critic.
\end{itemize}

The environment description is a Markdown file written once for each environment, in plain words and with no code. It says what the environment contains and what the agent can do in it, what ends an episode and what can and cannot happen, and it lists the propositions with the meaning of each. It also gives one solved episode step by step and a few example tasks. The propositions come from this file, so the same LLM prompt serves any environment.
    
Three instructions shape the answer. 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Every step the task asks for needs a goal of its own, a hard Existence. A rule holds while nothing happens, so without a goal nothing makes the agent take the step. Only a step that a hard Precedence already puts before a later step needs none, because the Precedence makes it come first. A task with a single step therefore gets one goal, and an ordered task gets one for its last step.
    \item When the task states an order, the order stays as a Precedence even if the environment already forces it, so the RM follows the instruction and not one layout of the environment.
    \item When the task states no order, none is added. Appendix~\ref{app:implementation} gives the prompt text and the form of an ordered task.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Hard and soft clauses}
Each clause carries a \textbf{priority that the model chooses for that clause alone}. Clauses are hard by default. The model marks a clause soft only when the task uses a preference cue such as ``if possible'', ``ideally'' or ``try to'', and the cue covers the phrase it qualifies, not the whole sentence or the task. 

The priority never changes the formula. A hard clause must hold for the task to be completed. A soft clause is a preference. Breaking it costs reward but never prevents finishing (Step~4). IBM's templates have no such notion, since \textit{never touch the lava} and \textit{if possible, never touch the lava} are the same Absence clause, so the priority has to come from the prompt. For FARM, the first would be a hard Absence and the second a soft one.

\subsection{Task schemas}
\label{sec:task-schemas}
The compiler reads three things of each clause: its template, its priority and the slot each proposition fills. It never reads what a proposition means. Two tasks whose clauses differ only in their propositions therefore get the same RM, with the names exchanged. The clauses with the propositions left open form a \textbf{task schema}.

Table~\ref{tab:schemas} gives two tasks of the schema of the \runex{sequence} example: \textit{gather wood, then stone, then iron} and \textit{attend the client, prepare the order, then serve it}. Both are a hard Existence of the last step and one hard Precedence for each pair of consecutive steps, so both would get the \runex{sequence} RM of Listing~\ref{lst:farm-example1} with the propositions renamed.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.2cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Schema clause} & \textbf{Gather wood, then stone, then iron} & \textbf{Attend the client, prepare the order, then serve it} \\ \midrule
Existence$(c)$ & Existence(\texttt{has\_iron}) \newline $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{has\_iron}$ & Existence(\texttt{served}) \newline $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{served}$ \\
Precedence$(a,b)$ & Precedence(\texttt{has\_wood}, \texttt{has\_stone}) \newline $(\neg\texttt{has\_stone}\ \mathrm{U}\ \texttt{has\_wood}) \lor \mathrm{G}\,\neg\texttt{has\_stone}$ & Precedence(\texttt{attended}, \texttt{prepared}) \newline $(\neg\texttt{prepared}\ \mathrm{U}\ \texttt{attended}) \lor \mathrm{G}\,\neg\texttt{prepared}$ \\
Precedence$(b,c)$ & Precedence(\texttt{has\_stone}, \texttt{has\_iron}) \newline $(\neg\texttt{has\_iron}\ \mathrm{U}\ \texttt{has\_stone}) \lor \mathrm{G}\,\neg\texttt{has\_iron}$ & Precedence(\texttt{prepared}, \texttt{served}) \newline $(\neg\texttt{served}\ \mathrm{U}\ \texttt{prepared}) \lor \mathrm{G}\,\neg\texttt{served}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\caption{Two tasks of one schema. All clauses are hard. The propositions are illustrative.}
\label{tab:schemas}
\end{table}

The priority belongs to the schema. The \runex{careful-key} task with a hard Absence instead of a soft one gives a different RM. With a soft clause, the price of a hard break also depends on the step limit, the discount and the number of soft rules that can break in one step (Section~\ref{sec:well-charge}), so two such tasks share an RM only under the same settings. In every case, two tasks of the same shape share an RM only when the generator gives them the same clauses.

\subsection{Running examples: from task to formulas}
\label{sec:run-step1}
Table~\ref{tab:running} gives the clauses that the generator chose for each running example and the LTLf formula that the template of each clause wrote. The \runex{sequence} becomes a hard Existence of its last step and two hard Precedence rules, one for each pair of consecutive steps, and, because the last Existence is hard, the order makes the first two steps happen too. The \runex{dropped key} joins the goal with a ChainResponse rule, the template for ``right away''. In the \runex{careful key} the word ``ideally'' makes the Absence of a lost key soft, and its formula is the same as that of a hard Absence.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{5.8cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Clauses} & \textbf{LTLf} \\ \midrule
\multicolumn{2}{@{}l@{}}{\runex{Sequence}: \textit{Pick up the key, then open the door, then reach the goal}} \\
Existence(\texttt{at\_goal}) & $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{at\_goal}$ \\
Precedence(\texttt{has\_key}, \texttt{door\_open}) & $(\neg\texttt{door\_open}\ \mathrm{U}\ \texttt{has\_key}) \lor \mathrm{G}\,\neg\texttt{door\_open}$ \\
Precedence(\texttt{door\_open}, \texttt{at\_goal}) & $(\neg\texttt{at\_goal}\ \mathrm{U}\ \texttt{door\_open}) \lor \mathrm{G}\,\neg\texttt{at\_goal}$ \\ \addlinespace
\multicolumn{2}{@{}l@{}}{\runex{Dropped key}: \textit{Reach the goal, and if you drop the key, pick it up right away}} \\
Existence(\texttt{at\_goal}) & $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{at\_goal}$ \\
ChainResponse(\texttt{key\_lost}, \texttt{has\_key}) & $\mathrm{G}\,(\texttt{key\_lost} \rightarrow \mathrm{X}\,\texttt{has\_key})$ \\ \addlinespace
\multicolumn{2}{@{}l@{}}{\runex{Careful key}: \textit{Reach the goal, ideally without ever losing the key}} \\
Existence(\texttt{at\_goal}) & $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{at\_goal}$ \\
Absence(\texttt{key\_lost}), soft & $\neg\,\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{key\_lost}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\caption[Clauses and LTLf formulas of the running examples]{The clauses and LTLf formulas of the running examples after Step~1. All clauses are hard unless marked soft.}
\label{tab:running}
\end{table}

\section{Step 2 · LTLf to DFA}
\label{sec:step2}
The second step, the first of the compiler, turns the LTLf formula of every clause into a deterministic finite automaton (DFA) that accepts exactly the traces satisfying it (Section~\ref{sec:sota-ltl}). FL-AT translates the formula for MONA, and MONA returns the automaton (Figure~\ref{fig:step2}).

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[farm, node distance=7mm]
\node[farm items, text width=1.8cm] (ltl) {LTLf formula};
\node[farm tool, farm step=2, text width=2cm, right=of ltl] (mona) {MONA};
\node[farm items, text width=1.8cm, right=of mona] (dfa) {DFA};
\draw[farm flow] (ltl) -- (mona);
\draw[farm flow] (mona) -- (dfa);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption[Step 2 of FARM]{Step 2. Each LTLf formula becomes one DFA.}
\label{fig:step2}
\end{figure}

\subsection{FL-AT and MONA}
FL-AT, the Formal Language--Automaton Transmogrifier \cite{transmogrifier2020}, writes each LTLf formula in monadic second-order logic over finite strings, the input language of MONA. In that encoding a temporal operator becomes a quantifier over the positions of the trace, and a proposition becomes the set of positions where it holds. MONA \cite{henriksen1995mona} decides a formula of this logic by building the smallest DFA for it. Such a DFA always exists, because the formulas of this logic describe exactly the regular languages (the Büchi--Elgot--Trakhtenbrot theorem \cite{buchi1960weak,elgot1961decision,trakhtenbrot1966finite}).

FL-AT runs MONA as a separate process and reads back the automaton that MONA prints: its initial state, its accepting states and its transitions, each with a guard over the propositions. The reading is strict, and a malformed automaton stops the build (Appendix~\ref{app:implementation}). FARM changes two things in FL-AT, which now drops MONA's first state when that state is redundant (see next at Section~\ref{sec:redundant}) and returns, for every state of the product built in Step~3, the clause states it combines.

\subsection{Redundant states in MONA's automata}
\label{sec:redundant}
MONA returns the smallest automaton for each formula, but two of its states do no work in an RM. Figure~\ref{fig:mona-stages} in Appendix~\ref{app:mona} shows both for the clause Absence(\texttt{key\_lost}), $\neg\,\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{key\_lost}$, but they are described here.

\paragraph{A pass-through first state.} MONA's automaton can begin with a state that every input leaves for the same next state. In panel~(a), S0 moves to S1 whatever happens. FARM removes such a state and starts the automaton at its successor, which gives panel~(b). To be safe, it removes the state only when it is not accepting, has one successor, is never entered again and has transitions that cover every input exactly once. Otherwise the automaton stays as MONA made it.

\paragraph{A start for a trace with no steps.} In a rule clause, MONA keeps a separate start for the moment before the first step. In panel~(b), S1 is that start. It moves exactly like S2, the state that a step without \texttt{key\_lost} leads to, and differs from S2 only in that it does not accept, because the automaton does not accept a trace with no steps. An episode always has at least one step, so the difference never matters, and Step~4 merges S1 into S2 (Section~\ref{sec:start-merge}), which gives panel~(c).

\subsection{Running examples: from formulas to DFAs}
\label{sec:run-step2}
The three running examples have seven clauses but only four templates, and clauses of the same template give automata of the same shape. Figure~\ref{fig:run-dfas} therefore draws one automaton per template. 

A double circle marks an accepting state, where the clause holds for the steps taken so far. A state whose only edge is a loop marked $\top$, which stands for any input, is never left, so the clause is met or broken there for good. S1 is always the start. In panels~(b) to~(d) it is the ``no step yet'' start of Section~\ref{sec:redundant}, which moves like S2, so these three can be read from S2.

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Existence, panel~(a).} The automaton waits in S1 until the agent is at the goal. It then moves to S2 and stays there, with the clause met for good.
    \item \textbf{Absence, panel~(b).} The automaton stays in S2 while the key is kept. The first loss of the key sends it to S3, where the clause is broken for good.
    \item \textbf{Precedence, panel~(c).} The automaton stays in S2 while the agent has neither picked up the key nor opened the door. Picking up the key sends it to S3, where the clause is met for good, whatever happens to the door afterwards. Opening the door without the key sends it to S4, where the clause is broken for good.
    \item \textbf{ChainResponse, panel~(d).} The automaton stays in S2 while the key is not lost. A lost key sends it to S3, where the key must be back at the next step. With the key back it returns to S2. Otherwise it falls to S4, where the clause is broken for good.
\end{itemize}

\section{Step 3 · Multiply DFAs into one automaton}
\label{sec:step3}
Still inside the compiler, the third step combines the automata of all clauses into one (Figure~\ref{fig:step3}). FL-AT builds their synchronous product, the construction that Camacho et al.~\cite{camacho2019ltl} use for combined specifications. A product state holds one state of each clause DFA, and every step moves all of them at once.

\node[farm rm, text width=2.9cm, right=of flat] (pm) {Product automaton};

\label{fig:step3}

\subsection{The product of the clause DFAs}
The idea of the product is to run the clause automata side by side. Each automaton follows its own clause, and after every step each one is in one of its states. A state of the product records where all of them are at that moment. In the \runex{careful key} it is a pair, written as (S1, S2) for the state of Existence(\texttt{at\_goal}) first and the state of Absence(\texttt{key\_lost}) second.

A step of the agent moves every automaton at once, each along the edge that the events of the step choose in it. An edge of the product is therefore one edge of each automaton, taken together in the same step. Its guard asks for all of their guards to hold, so it joins them with \textit{and}, and it leads to the product state made of their destinations. When two of these guards contradict, as when one asks for \texttt{door\_open} and the other for $\neg\,\texttt{door\_open}$, no step can take both edges, and the product has no such edge.

FL-AT builds the product from its start state, which holds the start state of every automaton. It adds the edges out of that state, then the edges out of every new state they reach, until no new state appears. The product therefore contains only the combinations of states that some trace can reach.

The \runex{careful key} example shows each part. Both automata start in S1, so the product starts in p0 = (S1, S1). In S1, Existence has two edges: $\neg\,\texttt{at\_goal}$ stays in S1 and \texttt{at\_goal} goes to S2. Absence has two as well: $\neg\,\texttt{key\_lost}$ goes to S2 and \texttt{key\_lost} to S3. One edge from each gives four combinations, so p0 has four edges. A step that reaches the goal and keeps the key, for instance, takes \texttt{at\_goal} in Existence and $\neg\,\texttt{key\_lost}$ in Absence, so its product edge has the guard $\texttt{at\_goal} \land \neg\,\texttt{key\_lost}$ and leads to p3 = (S2, S2). The other three edges lead to p1 = (S1, S2), where nothing has happened yet, p2 = (S1, S3), where the key is lost, and p4 = (S2, S3), where the goal is reached and the key lost. No guards contradict here, since the two clauses share no proposition. The edges out of p1 to p4 reach no new pair, so the product has the five states of Table~\ref{tab:run-product}. The two automata could form $2 \times 3 = 6$ pairs, but (S2, S1) never occurs, because Absence leaves S1 on the first step and never comes back.

Step~4 changes the automata of soft clauses before the product (Section~\ref{sec:recurrent}). The table and Figure~\ref{fig:run-product3} show the product of the automata as MONA returns them, which is also the product of the task with a hard Absence.

\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{State} & \textbf{Clause states} & \textbf{Meaning} & \textbf{FL-AT reward} \\ \midrule
p0 & (S1, S1) & Start & -- \\
p1 & (S1, S2) & No goal yet, key kept & 1 \\
p2 & (S1, S3) & No goal yet, key lost & 0 \\
p3 & (S2, S2) & Goal, key kept & 2 \\
p4 & (S2, S3) & Goal, key lost & 1 \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption[Product states of the \runex{careful key}]{The product states of the \runex{careful key} after Step~3. The clause states give the state of Existence(\texttt{at\_goal}) first and that of Absence(\texttt{key\_lost}) second. The reward is the one that FL-AT pays on every edge into the state.}

\subsection{Discarded features of FL-AT's product}
\label{sec:flat-discard}
FL-AT also sets the rewards of the product and marks its final states, and FARM replaces both. FARM needs rewards that pay for each step of progress, never pay for going round a loop, and price a broken hard rule apart from a broken preference, and it needs final states that only the hard clauses decide. FL-AT's rewards give none of these.

FL-AT pays each edge one unit for every clause whose DFA accepts after the step. This rewards the agent before the task is done, a simple form of shaping, but it falls short in four ways.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{It pays for staying, not for progress.} A clause pays on every step it spends accepting, not once when it is met, so a loop through an accepting state keeps paying, and a rule that holds from the start pays the agent for doing nothing. Such a reward is not potential-based shaping, so it can change which behaviour is best \cite{ng1999policy}. The agent may earn more by staying than by finishing. FARM pays for the change in the stages still owed, where a stage is one event still needed (Section~\ref{sec:reward-assignment}), so every loop adds up to zero, and its loop check refuses any RM with a rewarding loop (Section~\ref{sec:step4}).
    \item \textbf{Progress that completes no clause pays nothing.} A clause pays only once its DFA accepts. In the \runex{sequence} example, taking the key pays the same as a step in which nothing happens, because the Precedence of key and door accepts both before and after it. FARM pays $+1$ for each stage a step closes.
    \item \textbf{A break has no price of its own.} Each clause is worth one unit, whatever it states. A broken clause just stops paying, one unit less on that step and on every later one, so the cost of a break depends on the steps left, not on what was broken, and an early break costs more than a late one. Nothing ends the episode either, so after a break the agent still collects the units of the other clauses. FARM gives each break a price of its own, paid at the step it happens, and ends the episode when the task can no longer be completed (Step~4).
    \item \textbf{The final states follow from the rewards.} FL-AT marks as final the destinations of the edges with the largest reward. A broken preference then stops the task from finishing, and in a task made only of rules every clause already accepts after a step in which nothing happens, so that step would finish the task. FARM sets the final states itself (Section~\ref{sec:final-well}).
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[farm graph]
\input{images/farm/product-ex3}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption[Product of the \runex{careful key}]{The \runex{careful-key} product as FL-AT builds it, with the rewards of FL-AT. Each edge is labelled \textit{propositions / reward}.}
\label{fig:run-product3}
\end{figure}

The \runex{careful key} of Figure~\ref{fig:run-product3} shows points 1, 3 and 4; point 2 needs a task with more steps, such as the \runex{sequence}. The loop on p1 pays 1 at every step in which nothing happens, because the Absence clause accepts (point 1). If p3 ended the episode, an agent that idles in p1 until the step limit would earn more than one that reaches the goal early. Losing the key leads to p2, which pays nothing until the goal, so the loss costs more the earlier it happens (point 3). The largest reward is 2, so p3 is the only final state, and p4, the goal reached with the key lost, is not, although the key is only a preference (point 4).

FARM keeps the states and edges of the product, sets the final states from the hard clauses and sets the reward of every edge itself (Step~4).

\label{sec:run-step3}
The \runex{careful-key} product result is the one of Table~\ref{tab:run-product} and Figure~\ref{fig:run-product3}. The \runex{dropped-key} product has seven states and is drawn in Appendix~\ref{app:running} (Figure~\ref{fig:run-product2}). The \runex{sequence} product has ten states, out of the $2 \times 4 \times 4 = 32$ combinations of states its three automata could form, and Figure~\ref{fig:run-product1} draws it in simplified form. Its two Precedence clauses share \texttt{door\_open}, so pairings that need \texttt{door\_open} and $\neg\,\texttt{door\_open}$ at once give no edge. Five of the ten states break an order. Step~4 sends every edge into them to the well and makes p1 the start in place of p0 (Section~\ref{sec:start-merge}), which leaves the five states of Listing~\ref{lst:farm-example1}.

\section{Step 4 · Post-process}
\label{sec:step4}
The fourth step, the last of the compiler, is FARM's own and turns the product into the RM (Figure~\ref{fig:step4}). It prepares the clause automata \textbf{before} FL-AT multiplies them, and \textbf{after} the product it sets the final states, the well and the reward of every edge. Hard clauses decide which states are final and which edges go to the well; soft clauses only change what the RM pays.
