\subsection{Clause states}
Every state of a clause DFA gets one of three labels. It is \textbf{satisfied} when the DFA accepts there, that is, when the trace so far meets the clause. It is \textbf{pending} when the DFA does not accept yet but can still reach an accepting state, and \textbf{violated} when it can no longer reach one. The labels are exact, computed by reachability on the DFA of each clause, and since each product state of Step~3 records where every clause automaton is, it knows the label of each of its clauses.

\subsection{Re-arming soft rules}
\label{sec:recurrent}
An LTLf formula gives one verdict per trace, so a broken soft rule would be penalised \textbf{only once} per episode however often it is broken again. FARM \textbf{re-arms} a soft rule instead: 

\keyidea{The violated state of its DFA drops its own edges and takes those of the start, so the rule watches for the next break, and every new break is penalised again.} 

This only applies to the four templates whose DFA has a violated state: Absence, Precedence, ChainResponse and NotCoExistence. The other four, Existence, ExistenceTwo, RespondedExistence and Response, have no violated state, but a soft one still moves progress while it is pending. For example, the event that sets off a soft Response can cost $-1$ at once and its answer pay the $+1$ back, and finishing with the clause still pending costs $-5$.

\paragraph{Folding soft Absence} A soft Absence is \textbf{folded} as well. In the product, the states in which it is violated merge into the matching states in which it is satisfied, and the break becomes a mark on the edge. The clause then adds no state to the RM, and its break is a loop that pays $-1$ (Section~\ref{sec:run-step4}). Re-arming and folding are settings, both on by default. With re-arming off, a soft rule is penalised only once per episode.

To accomplish this, FARM uses no table of template conversions. It computes reachability on the DFA, a deterministic process. This means that the affected templates are not hard-coded, and any new templates added by other developers would be processed automatically by the same algorithm.

\subsection{Final states and the well}
\label{sec:final-well}
FARM drops the final states that FL-AT marks on the product (Section~\ref{sec:flat-discard}), so the RM still has to say where an episode ends. Two kinds of state end it: the well, entered when the task can no longer be completed, and the final states, reached when it is done. Only the hard clauses decide which states these are.

\keyidea{A product state in which a hard clause is violated can never complete the task, so every edge into such a state goes to one shared \textbf{well}, a rejecting state that ends the episode.}

\noindent When the task has a hard goal, the edges into states from which no final state can be reached go to the well too. The final RM can work with one well, because the price of a break sits on the edge into it, so each edge can pay its own price (Section~\ref{sec:well-charge}).

A \textbf{final state} is a state in which every hard clause is satisfied, and only a task with a hard goal has one. The edges out of a final state are dropped, so reaching it ends the episode, and the saved RM merges all final states into one.

\paragraph{Soft clauses} They never affect the final condition. A preference broken on the way still lets the task finish, at a cost. In the \runex{careful key} the goal is the only hard clause, so the goal is final with or without the key (Section~\ref{sec:run-step4}). By contrast, a hard clause that is only pending, such as a Response still waiting for its answer, blocks finishing but does not end the episode.

\paragraph{Unreachable states} States that the start cannot reach are pruned. In a task with a hard goal, if no reachable edge enters a final state, the clauses conflict and the build fails. A rule-only task compiles with no final state.

\subsection{Merging the start state}
\label{sec:start-merge}
MONA gives each rule a ``no step yet'' start (Section~\ref{sec:redundant}), and the product inherits it as a start that no edge re-enters. Its \textbf{idle successor} is the state it moves to on a step in which no proposition holds. The idle successor becomes the start, and the old start is dropped, when three conditions hold: no edge re-enters the start, the idle successor is neither the start, the well nor a final state, and both states move to the same state on every input. Otherwise the product is unchanged.

The merge makes the RM smaller. It is safe because no template of the catalogue is broken by a step in which nothing happens, so the start and its idle successor get the same rewards.

\subsection{Reward assignment}
\label{sec:reward-assignment}
With the final states and the well in place, every edge of the RM still needs a reward. FARM sets these rewards itself, in place of FL-AT's one unit per accepting clause (Section~\ref{sec:flat-discard}), so that the agent is paid for progress rather than for staying, and every break has a price of its own.

Every reward serves the order between five ways an episode can end up. Among the episodes that end up one way, the best is the one that collects the most reward.

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Finished.} The best episode that reaches the final state.
    \item \textbf{Started.} The best episode that makes progress without finishing. It ends closer to the final state than the start, with fewer events still needed to finish, and totals a net reward of zero or more.
    \item \textbf{Not started.} The episode in which nothing happens. The agent produces no event, either because it does not move or because its movements do not influence the environment.
    \item \textbf{Soft break.} The episode that is penalised the most for breaking soft rules, at worst, a break at every step.
    \item \textbf{Hard break.} The episode that breaks a hard rule at the lowest price, the edge into the well with the highest reward.
\end{itemize}

An RM must pay them in this order:

\keyidea{\centering\upshape\bfseries\boldmath finished $>$ started $>$ not started $\geq$ soft break $>$ hard break}
\noindent Not started may tie with a soft break. Every reward of this section is set to keep this order, and they all follow one principle. Getting closer to finishing the task or a rule \textbf{pays}. Stepping back from it, breaking a rule, or getting a new thing to do \textbf{costs}. A new thing to do can come from the agent, which triggered a rule, or from the environment, which changed so that the instructions now ask for a reaction.

Table~\ref{tab:rewards}, at the end of the section, gives every value. To clarify the rewards with an example, Appendix~\ref{sec:rewards-example} follows them step by step on one task, the \runex{dropped key}: what each state still owes, what each step pays and what five example episodes total.

\subsubsection{Progress}
Each step is paid first for its progress, measured by how much less it leaves to do. Progress is what puts the started episode above the not started one. 

A \textbf{stage} is one event still needed, and every state owes a number of stages $\Phi(u)$. With a final state, $\Phi(u)$ counts the fewest events still needed to finish plus, for each soft clause (a soft goal included), the most events its own automaton may still need; without one, every clause counts its own events (Appendix~\ref{app:implementation}). A step from $u$ to $u'$ pays
\[ r_{\mathrm{progress}}(u,u') = \Phi(u) - \Phi(u'), \]
\gain{one unit} for each stage it closes and \loss{minus one} for each stage it adds. This is potential-based shaping \cite{ng1999policy} with the potential $-\Phi$ and no discount, so around any loop the progress adds up to zero. The learner does discount its rewards, so the guarantee that shaping leaves the best behaviour unchanged holds only approximately. A final state owes nothing, so the finishing edge pays every stage still owed. 

Two exceptions apply: a step that breaks a soft rule pays no progress for that rule, and an edge into the well takes progress back (see \emph{Taking progress back} below).

\subsubsection{Completion and negative rewards}
Besides progress, a few events have a fixed reward. Completion puts the finished episode at the top of the order, and the negative rewards keep the soft break no better than the not started episode. 

\begin{itemize}
    \item Completion pays \gain{$+10$} on top of the progress of the finishing step. 
    \item Each new break of a re-armed soft rule is penalised again, \loss{$-1$} per break (Section~\ref{sec:recurrent}), so a repeated break is never free. 
    \item A soft clause still pending when the episode finishes is penalised with \loss{$-5$} once, on the finishing edge, so a preference that was never met costs reward without blocking the end. 
    \item A soft break costs \loss{$-1$}, a tenth of completion, so one preference weighs less than finishing the task.
    \item A hard break costs \loss{$-10$} when the task has no soft clause. When the task also has soft clauses, the price grows with the step limit, about \loss{$-96$} for 300 steps, so that a hard break still costs more than breaking soft rules at every step and stays at the bottom of the order (see \emph{The price of a hard break} below).
\end{itemize}


\subsubsection{The price of a hard break}
\phantomsection\label{sec:well-charge}
A hard break ends the episode in the well, and its price must keep the hard break at the bottom of the order. In a task \textbf{without} a soft clause, an edge into the well pays the flat price of Table~\ref{tab:rewards}, \loss{$-10$}. \textbf{With} a soft clause in the task, a hard break must cost more than breaking soft rules at every step until the episode ends; otherwise an agent facing many soft breaks could prefer the well. The edge into the well then pays
\[ -\left(\max\!\left(10,\; N c\,\frac{1-\gamma^{T}}{1-\gamma}\right) + 1\right), \]
where $c$ is the price of a soft break ($1$), $N$ the number of soft rules that can break in one step (a setting, $1$ by default), $\gamma$ the discount ($0.99$) and $T$ the step limit of the environment. The fraction is the discounted number of steps in an episode of $T$ steps. 

With a step limit of 300 steps, the discount $0.99$, one soft rule per step and a soft-break price of $1$, the price is about \loss{$-96$} ($-96.096$), which is the discounted cost of breaking a soft rule at every one of the 300 steps, plus one. The price grows with the step limit and is never milder than \loss{$-11$}. The order holds only if no more than $N$ soft rules can break in one step, and the compiler does not check $N$ against the task. 

\paragraph{Taking progress back}
Progress pays as the episode gets closer to finishing, so an episode that breaks a hard rule late has already been paid for the stages it closed. Without a correction, the same break would cost less the later it came, and the hard break could climb up the order.

\keyidea{The edge into the well takes back the progress the episode made, so a hard break late in the episode costs no less than one at the first step.}
\noindent A state is \textbf{ahead} of the start when it owes fewer stages than the start. The edge into the well takes \loss{one unit} for each stage by which the state it leaves is ahead, and nothing when that state is not ahead. The negative rewards of the soft rules paid on the way are not taken back.

In the \runex{sequence} RM (Listing~\ref{lst:farm-example1}), the edge from u0 into the well pays \loss{$-10$}, and the edge from u1, one stage ahead of the start, pays \loss{$-11$}. An episode in which the agent picks up the key and then reaches the goal with the door closed therefore totals \loss{$-10$}, the same as a break at the first step.

\subsubsection{Checks}
The rewards are set to keep the order of the start of this section, and two checks look at each built RM to catch one that does not pay as it should.

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Loop check.} It searches the RM for a loop whose rewards add up to more than zero, counting every step without discount. An agent could circle such a loop for ever and earn more than by finishing, which is known as reward farming. It runs on every build and refuses the RM if it finds one.
    \item \textbf{Order check.} It checks that the RM pays the five episodes in the order of the start of this section. It runs when FARM prepares an RM for training, because it needs the step limit of the environment, and a failed order only gives a warning.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Rule-only RMs}
\phantomsection\label{sec:rule-only}
Some tasks only set rules, such as \textit{never touch the lava}, and ask for nothing to happen. A task with no hard goal has no final state. Only the well or the environment ends the episode, and the compiler warns that the task has no goal. 

Without a final state, progress has no goal to aim at. It then counts every clause on its own and aims at the states where no clause is pending. A step pays progress only if such a state can still be reached after it. If none can, the rules can no longer all be met, and there is nothing left to get closer to.

\label{sec:checks}\paragraph{Rule triggers} A rule that waits for an event, such as a Response, owes a stage once its trigger happens, whether the agent or the environment caused it. The trigger costs \loss{one unit} \textbf{per stage at once}, and the reaction that the rule asks for pays it back. A trigger that is answered therefore totals 0, the same as no trigger, but it lets no other choice to the agent but to follow the rule for maximising its reward.

The task \card{shop-siren}, \textit{Keep the shop quiet: whenever the siren rings, turn it off}, shows these rewards at work with one hard rule, Response(\texttt{siren\_ringing}, \texttt{switch\_toggled}). In the shop environment (Section~\ref{sec:eval-cards}), a siren also starts ringing by itself at random steps. The proposition \texttt{siren\_ringing} holds while the siren rings, and \texttt{switch\_toggled} holds at the step the agent turns it off. The task has no goal and asks for none of this work, so the agent has nothing else to do. Only an event of the environment, the siren starting, gives it something to do. 

Its RM has two states and no final state (Figure~\ref{fig:rule-only-rm}), and no well either, since the siren can still be turned off at any later step. A step in which the siren rings moves the RM from u0, where nothing is owed, to u1 for \loss{$-1$}, the negative reward of one stage. Turning the siren off pays the \gain{$+1$} back on the way to u0. A siren left ringing keeps the \loss{$-1$}. With the discount, the \gain{$+1$} is worth more the sooner it comes, so the agent does best by turning the siren off as soon as it rings.

Table~\ref{tab:rewards} gathers every reward of this section with its default value. The default values were found by trials on many tasks, always within the order that opens this section.

\textbf{What happens} & \textbf{What it pays} \\ \midrule

The episode reaches the final state &\gain{$+10$} \\

\subsection{Running examples: from the product to the RM}
\label{sec:run-step4}
Figure~\ref{fig:ex3-rm} draws the \runex{careful-key} RM with every transition labelled \textit{propositions / reward}.

The larger RMs of the thesis are drawn in a simplified form, in which transitions between two states that pay the same share one arrow, labelled with their reward and their number in brackets, and transitions with different rewards may also share one, labelled \textit{mixed}. In both forms, an arrow without a source marks the start and a double circle the final state. Implicit stays are not drawn. When no transition of a state matches, the RM stays in that state and pays 0.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[farm graph, node distance=6cm,
    rm gain/.style={draw=green!50!black, text=green!40!black},
    rm cost/.style={draw=red!70!black, text=red!60!black}]
\node[state, initial] (u0) {u0};
\node[state, accepting, right=of u0] (u1) {u1};
\path[->]
    (u0) edge[rm cost, loop above] node[above left] {$\neg\,\texttt{at\_goal} \land \texttt{key\_lost}$ / $-1$} ()
    (u0) edge[rm gain, bend left=25] node[above] {$\texttt{at\_goal} \land \neg\,\texttt{key\_lost}$ / $+11$} (u1)
    (u0) edge[rm gain, bend right=25] node[below] {$\texttt{at\_goal} \land \texttt{key\_lost}$ / $+10$} (u1);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption[RM of the \runex{careful key}]{The RM of the \runex{careful key} after Step~4.}
\label{fig:ex3-rm}
\end{figure}

The task, \textit{Reach the goal, ideally without ever losing the key}, has a hard Existence(\texttt{at\_goal}), $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{at\_goal}$, and a soft Absence(\texttt{key\_lost}), $\neg\,\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{key\_lost}$. The five-state product of Figure~\ref{fig:run-product3} becomes two states, and each part of Step~4 does its share:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Folding soft Absence} (Section~\ref{sec:recurrent}). The fold leaves out the lost-key states p2 and p4 of the product. A loss becomes a mark on the edge, and the RM stays where it was.
    \item \textbf{Final states and the well} (Section~\ref{sec:final-well}). p3 becomes the final state u1. The key is soft, so no state leads to a well, and a lost key never blocks finishing.
    \item \textbf{Merging the start state} (Section~\ref{sec:start-merge}). The start p0 merges with its idle successor p1 into u0.
    \item \textbf{Reward assignment} (Section~\ref{sec:reward-assignment}). Of the three transitions, losing the key before the goal costs \loss{$-1$} and stays in u0, so every new loss is penalised again. Reaching the goal pays \gain{$+11$}, the \gain{$+10$} of completion plus \gain{one} stage of progress, or \gain{$+10$} when the key is lost in the same step, the \loss{$-1$} of the break taken from the $+11$. Idle steps pay nothing.
\end{itemize}

The \runex{sequence} RM has five states and eleven transitions (Listing~\ref{lst:farm-example1}). Its states follow the order of the task, and every edge that breaks the order goes to the well. The \runex{dropped-key} RM has five states and ten transitions, and Appendix~\ref{sec:rewards-example} follows what it pays. Appendix~\ref{app:running} draws both.

\section{Step 5 · The critic}
\label{sec:step5}
The fifth step reviews the generator's work before the RM leaves FARM. A second language model, the \textbf{critic}, reads the task, the clauses and the RM built from them, and accepts the result or rejects it with comments; on a rejection the generator tries again (Figure~\ref{fig:step5}). The critic never edits the RM.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[farm, node distance=7mm]
\node[farm text, text width=2.6cm, minimum height=7.5mm] (env) at (0,2.0) {Environment description};
\node[farm text, text width=2.6cm, minimum height=7.5mm] (task) at (0,1.0) {Task};
\node[farm items, text width=2.6cm] (cl) at (0,0) {Clauses};
\node[farm rm, text width=2.6cm] (rm) at (0,-1.0) {RM};
\node[farm text, text width=2.6cm, minimum height=7.5mm] (clues) at (0,-2.0) {Compiler clues and warnings};
\node[farm llm, farm step=5, text width=1.6cm] (crit) at (3.6,0) {Critic};
\node[farm final, text width=2.9cm, anchor=west] (ok) at ($(crit.east)+(18mm,5mm)$) {Final RM};
\node[farm text, text width=2.9cm, anchor=west] (back) at ($(crit.east)+(18mm,-5mm)$) {Comments to Step 1};
\draw[farm flow] (env.east) -- (crit.west);
\draw[farm flow] (task.east) -- (crit.west);
\draw[farm flow] (cl.east) -- (crit.west);
\draw[farm flow] (rm.east) -- (crit.west);
\draw[farm flow] (clues.east) -- (crit.west);
\draw[farm flow] (crit.east) -- node[above, sloped] {Accepted} (ok.west);
\draw[farm flow, dashed] (crit.east) -- node[below, sloped] {Rejected} (back.west);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption[Step 5 of FARM]{Step 5. The critic accepts the RM or sends comments back to the generator.}
\label{fig:step5}
\end{figure}

Like the generator's, the critic's prompt was tuned on that separate set of tasks, never on the tasks the evaluation scores (Appendix~\ref{app:res-prompts}).

\subsection{What the critic reads and checks}
Only the choice of clauses varies from run to run, because the compiler turns the same clauses into the same RM every time. One review at the end therefore covers the whole pipeline, and it looks at the clauses.

\keyidea{The critic judges whether the clauses say what the task says (if they are the right selection). It reads the RM only to see what the clauses mean in practice, and does not judge its shape or its rewards, which follow from the clauses.}
\noindent The critic reads six inputs:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The environment description and the task, the same two that the generator read;
    \item The clauses and the RM compiled from them, which shows what the clauses do in practice. An empty set of final states, for instance, shows that a hard goal is missing;
    \item The \textbf{compiler clues}, which the compiler computes exactly from the clauses and the RM: each rule in plain words, the hard clauses that the others already imply, and the events that every way to finish includes;
    \item The \textbf{warnings}, which flag a task with no goal and a soft clause that changes no reward.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Measured on clause sets with a known defect, the compiler clues raise the share of defects that the critic flags at its first call from 30\% (74 of 246) to 56\% (23 of 41). Appendix~\ref{app:res-prompts} gives the measurement.

\noindent A clause passes when it:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Comes from a sentence of the task;
    \item Uses the right template and argument order;
    \item Carries the priority that its wording asks for;
    \item Uses the declared propositions.
\end{itemize}

\noindent The critic also rejects a clause whose event can never occur in the environment and a hard Existence that the other clauses already imply. It does not judge the topology of the RM, its number of states or the size of its rewards, and it treats the warnings, such as a task with no goal, as valid output. Its verdict is either \texttt{NO CHANGES NEEDED} or a list of changes that starts with \texttt{CHANGES REQUIRED}.

\subsection{Fixing mistakes}
A rejection starts a new attempt. The generator's prompt then carries the history of the task's earlier attempts: for each, the clauses it proposed, the RM compiled from them when one was built, and why the attempt failed, the comments of the critic or the error of the compiler. The generator proposes new clauses, from which the compiler rebuilds the RM (Appendix~\ref{app:implementation}). The critic sees none of this history and judges every attempt afresh. One stored case shows such a repair.

\textbf{Task.} \textit{Try to move the ball off the door before you take the key. Pick up the box in the other room.}

\textbf{First answer.} Two clauses: a hard Existence(\texttt{target\_picked\_up}) and a soft Precedence(\texttt{blocker\_moved}, \texttt{has\_door\_key}).

\textbf{Critic.} It asked for a soft Existence(\texttt{blocker\_moved}) as well, ``so the preferred move is expressed''.

\textbf{Accepted answer.} The second answer, with three clauses:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Existence(\texttt{target\_picked\_up}), hard: $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{target\_picked\_up}$;
    \item Existence(\texttt{blocker\_moved}), soft: $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{blocker\_moved}$;
    \item Precedence(\texttt{blocker\_moved}, \texttt{has\_door\_key}), soft:\[(\neg\texttt{has\_door\_key}\ \mathrm{U}\ \texttt{blocker\_moved}) \lor \mathrm{G}\,\neg\texttt{has\_door\_key}\]
\end{itemize}
The second answer adds the soft Existence that the critic asked for.

\subsection{Different LLMs for generation and critique and bias}
A review helps only when it does not come from the model that wrote the clauses, to avoid any inherited model bias. Generator and critic are therefore configured separately, and the build refuses a critic that resolves to the same model as the generator. FARM's \textbf{default pair} is \texttt{deepseek-v4.1-flash} as generator and \texttt{glm-5.3-flash} as critic.

\noindent To further this ``no-bias'', the critic also sees none of the earlier attempts.

\subsection{Clauses and environment facts}
The critic reads the environment description, but it judges the clauses against the task, not against the layout of one environment.

\keyidea{The RM matches what the task says, not what one environment happens to force.}
\noindent The critic rejects a clause set that drops an order the task states, even when the environment already forces that order, and it never asks for an order the task does not state. As a result, an RM can hold edges that the environment never takes.

The \runex{sequence} RM (Listing~\ref{lst:farm-example1}) has edges out of u0 on an open door without the key and on the goal before the door, while in \env{DoorKey} only the key opens the locked door. These edges never fire, so \textbf{they pay nothing in training}. Chapter~\ref{chap:limitations} discusses what the compiler cannot know about the environment.

\subsection{Things to keep in mind}
Two points bound what this step can promise. Both models can be wrong, and how often depends on their thinking level.

\paragraph{LLMs can hallucinate} As stated earlier, the critic and the generator are LLMs, thus they can hallucinate or make mistakes \cite{huang2025survey}. The compiler builds the RM exactly from the chosen clauses and adds nothing they do not state, so a wrong RM always comes from a wrong clause set. By this idea, if any mistake is made, both LLMs are at fault, thus, \textbf{the generator proposed something wrong and the critic missed it}.

\paragraph{Thinking level of the models} Both models can think before they answer. Thinking levels go from none to low, medium, high or max. The default is max and Chapter~\ref{chap:results} shows how the share of correct RMs changes with the level. Now, that comes at a price, since thinking longer means more tokens and therefore more cost.

\subsection{Running examples: the verdict of the critic}
\label{sec:run-step5}
The critic accepted the three RMs of Step~4 at the first attempt, each time with the verdict \texttt{NO CHANGES NEEDED}. The generator therefore did not try again, and the RMs of Step~4 are the final output of FARM.

\section{The output format}
\label{sec:output-format}
The RM is what FARM hands over to training. FARM writes it in the plain-text format of ARM-FM (Listing~\ref{lst:armfm-doorkey}), whose training it reuses, and extends that format; Appendix~\ref{app:implementation} lists the differences.

\subsection{The fields}
Listing~\ref{lst:farm-example1} shows the \runex{sequence} RM in this format.

\noindent\begin{minipage}{\linewidth}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=rm,caption={The RM that FARM builds for the \runex{sequence} example.},label={lst:farm-example1}]
REWARD_MACHINE:
STATES: u0, u1, u2, u3, u4
INITIAL_STATE: u0
FINAL_STATES: u4
REJECTING_STATES: u2
DEFAULT_REWARD: 0
TRANSITION_FUNCTION:
(u0, !at_goal&!door_open&has_key) -> u1
(u0, !at_goal&door_open&!has_key) -> u2
(u0, !at_goal&door_open&has_key) -> u3
(u0, at_goal&!door_open&!has_key) -> u2
(u0, at_goal&!door_open&has_key) -> u2
(u0, at_goal&door_open&!has_key) -> u2
(u0, at_goal&door_open&has_key) -> u4
(u1, !at_goal&door_open) -> u3
(u1, at_goal&!door_open) -> u2
(u1, at_goal&door_open) -> u4
(u3, at_goal) -> u4
REWARD_FUNCTION:
(u0, !at_goal&!door_open&has_key, u1) -> 1
(u0, !at_goal&door_open&!has_key, u2) -> -10
(u0, !at_goal&door_open&has_key, u3) -> 2
(u0, at_goal&!door_open&!has_key, u2) -> -10
(u0, at_goal&!door_open&has_key, u2) -> -10
(u0, at_goal&door_open&!has_key, u2) -> -10
(u0, at_goal&door_open&has_key, u4) -> 13
(u1, !at_goal&door_open, u3) -> 1
(u1, at_goal&!door_open, u2) -> -11
(u1, at_goal&door_open, u4) -> 12
(u3, at_goal, u4) -> 11
\end{lstlisting}
\end{minipage}

\noindent After the \texttt{REWARD\_MACHINE:} header come seven fields:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \texttt{STATES}: every state.
    \item \texttt{INITIAL\_STATE}: the start.
    \item \texttt{FINAL\_STATES}: the final state, at most one; empty when the task has no hard goal.
    \item \texttt{REJECTING\_\allowbreak STATES}: the well, left out when there is none.
    \item \texttt{DEFAULT\_REWARD}: the reward of a step that matches no transition. The RM then stays in its state and pays 0.
    \item \texttt{TRANSITION\_\allowbreak FUNCTION}: one line per transition, \texttt{(source, event) -> destination}, where the event combines propositions with \texttt{!} for ``not'' and \texttt{\&} for ``and''.
    \item \texttt{REWARD\_FUNCTION}: the reward of each transition, \texttt{(source, event, destination) -> reward}, leaving out the transitions that pay 0.
\end{itemize}

In the listing, u0 is the start, u1 holds the key, u3 holds the key with the door open, u4 is the final state and u2 the well. Each stage closed pays \gain{$+1$} and the goal adds \gain{$+10$}, so doing all three steps at once pays \gain{$+13$}. Every way to break the order costs \loss{$-10$}, or \loss{$-11$} from u1, which is one stage ahead of the start (Section~\ref{sec:reward-assignment}, \emph{Taking progress back}).

\subsection{The RM in training}
Two more rules decide how the RM meets training: when an episode ends, and which reward the agent is trained on. An episode ends when:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The RM enters its \textbf{final} state;
    \item The RM enters the \textbf{rejecting well};
    \item The environment reaches its \textbf{own end};
    \item The \textbf{step limit} runs out.
\end{itemize}


\noindent The setting \texttt{reward\_source} picks the reward:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \texttt{rm}, the default: the reward of the RM alone, plus a flat time-limit penalty (default \loss{$-5$}) on the last step of an episode that times out.
    \item \texttt{both}: the reward of the RM plus the reward of the environment.
    \item \texttt{env}: the reward of the environment alone, with no RM.
\end{itemize}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%                                 LIMITATIONS                                 %
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\chapter{Limitations}
\label{chap:limitations}

A limit, in this chapter, is something FARM does not do or does not guarantee today. FARM's compiler knows the task only through the clauses the generator chooses, while it does not know the environment at all. The generator and the critic know the environment only through its description.

Each limit below is given with a case, so that a person knows what to avoid when writing a task or describing an environment. The first two limits concern the RMs the compiler builds, the next four what a task can state and how the generator reads it, and the last one the tasks that RMs are not meant for.

\section{States the environment rules out}
\label{sec:lim-impossible}
An RM can hold states that no run in its environment ever visits. The compiler prunes the states that no path from the start reaches (Section~\ref{sec:step4}), but it knows nothing of the environment, so it keeps the states and transitions that only the environment rules out.

This follows from a choice of the generator prompt. An order that the task states stays in the RM even when the environment already forces it, so the RM follows the instruction and not one layout of the environment (Section~\ref{sec:step1}). The \runex{sequence} RM of Listing~\ref{lst:farm-example1} shows the result. It has transitions out of u0 on an open door without the key and on the goal before the door, while in \env{DoorKey} only the key opens the locked door (Section~\ref{sec:step5}). Such a transition never fires, and a state that only such transitions enter is never visited. These states and transitions make the RM bigger and harder to read, but they \textbf{do not make training worse}.

FARM does not cut them, because a cut needs facts about the environment that are certain. The constraints an environment description states rest on reading the code of the environment and on random episodes, which can show a constraint false but never prove it. A wrong cut would also fail silently: if the environment did take a cut transition, the RM would have no edge for it, so it would stay in its state and pay nothing.

\section{RMs grow with the clauses}
\label{sec:lim-size}
An RM built by FARM is the product of one automaton per clause, so it can grow exponentially with the number of clauses. On the tasks of the shop, an environment of the evaluation's benchmark of tasks (Chapter~\ref{chap:evaluation}), where the agent takes orders, cooks and serves (Table~\ref{tab:eval-suites}), each added rule that waits for an answer nearly doubles the states (Table~\ref{tab:res-shop}). 

On the trained tasks where both FARM and ARM-FM built an RM, FARM's RMs are larger than ARM-FM's (Table~\ref{tab:res-size}), and the largest RM, that of a shop task with six hard rules, has more than ten thousand transitions (Section~\ref{sec:res-shop}). An RM of that size still follows its clauses exactly, but it is hard for a person to read. Section~\ref{sec:fw-size} takes it up.

\section{A rule alone does not make the agent act}
\label{sec:lim-rules}
Whether an agent has a reason to act depends on the kind of clause its task holds. Of the eight templates, only Existence and ExistenceTwo ask for something to happen. The other six are rules, which hold while nothing happens (Section~\ref{sec:step1}).

A task made of rules alone is therefore kept by an agent that does nothing, unless the environment sets the rules off by itself or produces some event the agent reacts to. Apart from such an environment, only a goal makes the agent act.

\keyidea{A person who wants the agent to act has to state a goal, or make the environment cause the agent to react.}

This is intended, since a rule says what must not happen or how to answer an event, not that the agent must begin anything. It limits what such a task can test. The benchmark leaves out of training the tasks with no goal that an agent doing nothing passes (Section~\ref{sec:eval-cards}), and it adds environments that keep setting the rules off: the zombie lanes, where zombies enter three lanes by themselves, and the shop, which for example rings a siren the agent has to turn off.


\section{Ambiguous wording}
\label{sec:lim-ambiguous}
Everyday language is ambiguous. A sentence can read two ways, and the generator may take either.

The task \textit{Try not to let zombies through both the top lane and the side lanes} can mean 
\begin{itemize}
    \item ``not the two together'', a soft NotCoExistence(\texttt{top\_\allowbreak breach}, \texttt{side\_\allowbreak breach}), $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{top\_breach} \rightarrow \neg\,\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{side\_breach}$, 
    \item or ``neither one nor the other'', a soft Absence(\texttt{top\_\allowbreak breach}), $\neg\,\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{top\_breach}$, and a soft Absence(\texttt{side\_\allowbreak breach}), $\neg\,\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{side\_breach}$. 
\end{itemize}

\noindent FARM's generator takes the second reading, which is one of the clause sets that the benchmark accepts as an answer for this task.

\keyidea{The compiler builds the reading the generator takes, so the wording decides the RM.}
\noindent In the evaluation (Chapter~\ref{chap:evaluation}), the few clause sets that differed from the first of the answers the benchmark accepts for their task were, all but one, judged afterwards to be fair readings of their wording (Section~\ref{sec:res-built}). They are cases, not a measure of how often the generator errs. The guide to task wording in the repository of this thesis (\texttt{docs/LTLF\_TASK\_LANGUAGE.md}) lists the readings found and how to write each one.

\section{Anomalies in the task}
\label{sec:lim-anomalies}
An anomaly is a task that breaks the assumption of Section~\ref{sec:step1}, that the task can be done. It is a fault in the task itself, which no choice of clauses can fix. The generator's answer is a list of one to 25 clauses with no field for a refusal, so an anomaly is caught only when a later check rejects the answer, and when every attempt is rejected the task fails with no RM (Section~\ref{sec:step5} and Appendix~\ref{app:implementation}).

Each of the three kinds of anomaly is caught only in part:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{An event that cannot occur}: the critic rejects a clause whose event can never occur. It checks each event alone, so a task whose events cannot happen the way it asks still gets an RM.
    \item \textbf{A fact the environment does not declare}: a clause that names an undeclared fact is rejected. The generator can instead use the nearest declared facts and drop part of the task.
    \item \textbf{Clauses that contradict each other}: the compiler rejects hard clauses that leave no way to reach a hard goal. A task without a hard goal is not checked, so its rules can contradict each other and it still builds. Soft clauses that conflict build too.
\end{itemize}

\section{Conditions the templates cannot state}
\label{sec:lim-conditions}
Some conditions cannot be written as clauses. A template argument is always one declared fact, never a negated condition or a combination of facts. All the clauses of a task also hold together, so the generator cannot join them with ``or'' or make one optional.

For example, a condition such as \textit{serve no food while the siren rings} joins two facts, so it works only if the environment declares one event that carries both, \texttt{served\_\allowbreak while\_\allowbreak ringing}.

\section{Tasks that RMs are not meant for}
\label{sec:lim-quantities}
The limits above are things FARM does not do today. This last section is about tasks it is not meant to do, because they do not fit an RM at all. They are of three kinds:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{As much or as little as possible}: a clause is met or broken over a whole run, so it cannot ask for as many items as possible or for the shortest path. The templates that count stop at a fixed number and LTLf cannot express the unbounded counting. An RM that paid every time something happens would need a rewarding loop, which the loop check refuses (Section~\ref{sec:step4}). 
    
    A reward paid for each event (such as \texttt{kill\_zombie}) is simple to write in the environment instead if needed.
    \item \textbf{Behaviour no one can put into words}: something the user cannot define with words, such as a movement that looks natural, gives no clauses to write. It needs a reward learnt from human feedback \cite{christiano2017deep}.
    \item \textbf{Behaviour close to single moves}: a task such as \textit{Stay close to the obstacles on your way} can be stated only with a fact declared for it or with clauses over single moves. The RM then works at the level of moves, not at the level of the steps of the task, where it is meant to work, so such a task is out of scope.
\end{itemize}

% \begin{table}[H]
% \centering
% \footnotesize
% \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
% \begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{4.8cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
% \toprule
% \textbf{Limit} & \textbf{Can later work remove it?} \\ \midrule
% States the world rules out (Section~\ref{sec:lim-impossible}) & Possibly, with facts about the world that can be trusted. These states do not make training worse. \\
% Machines grow with the clauses (Section~\ref{sec:lim-size}) & Open: how the size grows is the first thing to measure (Section~\ref{sec:fw-size}). \\
% A rule alone does not make the agent act (Section~\ref{sec:lim-rules}) & No. It follows from the design: a rule holds while nothing happens, and only a goal asks for action. \\
% Ambiguous wording (Section~\ref{sec:lim-ambiguous}) & In part: the guide to task wording gives a wording for each reading found, and a task in plain language can still read two ways. \\
% A task that cannot be done (Section~\ref{sec:lim-anomalies}) & In part: a refusal field would let the model say so (Section~\ref{sec:fw-refusal}). \\
% A fact the environment does not declare (Section~\ref{sec:lim-anomalies}) & In part: only declared facts are allowed, by design, and that stays; a refusal field would let the model report the missing fact (Section~\ref{sec:fw-refusal}). \\
% Clauses that contradict each other (Section~\ref{sec:lim-anomalies}) & No. Hard rules that leave the goal unreachable cannot make one machine; conflicting preferences still build, each with its price. \\
% Conditions the templates cannot state (Section~\ref{sec:lim-conditions}) & In part: a model that writes the LTLf itself could state them (Section~\ref{sec:fw-ltlf}). \\
% Tasks the machines are not meant for (Section~\ref{sec:lim-quantities}) & No, by design: a clause is met or broken over a whole run. The environment can pay a repeated event itself, with a reward any developer can write, and a behaviour no one can put into words needs a reward learnt from human feedback. \\
% \bottomrule
% \end{tabularx}

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%                            EVALUATION METHODOLOGY                           %
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\chapter{Evaluation methodology}
\label{chap:evaluation}
The evaluation tests two claims, each with its own experiment. The \textbf{generation experiment} tests the first, that FARM builds a right RM from a task written in plain language and the \textbf{training experiment} tests the second, that an agent trained on such an RM learns the task. 

For the first FARM and a rebuilt ARM-FM build RMs for the same tasks, and each RM is checked against its task with no agent trained (Section~\ref{sec:eval-machines}). For the second, agents train on the RMs of FARM and of ARM-FM plus with no RM (only on the environment's own reward) as a baseline. A judge checks what they do against the task to asses the result of the runs (Section~\ref{sec:eval-learning}). 

Smaller side experiments also train agents on the tasks of the ARM-FM paper, the shop and the zombie lanes under change (Section~\ref{sec:eval-armfm-paper}). Two smaller \textbf{studies} evaluate empirically how the thinking levels of the LLMs vary across LLMs of different providers (Section~\ref{sec:eval-machines}).

For the terminology used, mention that we have:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Tasks}: a task is one plain-language instruction, with its environment and its expected clauses. A task can be trained or not, and it can be repeated under different settings. If trained, it will come with the expected agent behaviour.
    \item \textbf{Experiments}: a set of tasks used to test a claim. The evaluation has two experiments, one for generation and one for training.
    \item \textbf{Runs}: a run is one instance execution of a task of one experiment. Either just build an RM, or rain an agent with or without an RM. 
\end{itemize}

\section{The benchmark tasks}
\label{sec:eval-cards}
The benchmark gathers the tasks of both experiments. Each of its tasks states what counts as a right RM and what an agent must do to pass. Together, all the tasks cover both the lists of steps that ARM-FM was built for and the rules and events that FARM adds. This section describes what a task holds, how the tasks are grouped and which of them are trained. A task holds  

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{text}: the plain-language instruction that defines the task.
    \item \textbf{environment}: the environment in which the task is executed.
    \item \textbf{markdown description}: a markdown description of the environment.
    \item \textbf{goal}: whether the task has a goal.
    \item \textbf{Rewordings}: to show whether the generation of a RM depends on the wording and validate robustness, a 'rewording' says the same task in other words.
    \item \textbf{answers accepted}: we found out that some tasks can have multiple valid interpretations, and therefore solutions. This is a set of lists of clauses, each list being a valid solution to the task.
    \item \textbf{scripted plans}: a task holds three programs of commands written in advance for its environment (see Section~\ref{sec:eval-plan-check}). One the plan, solves the task, other breaks a named rule, and the last 'idle' plan only waits. Playing these plans checks an RM before any agent trains on it.  
\end{itemize}

The benchmark holds 126 tasks in the eight \textbf{groups} of Table~\ref{tab:eval-suites}, each made for one kind of task. The base group holds the tasks of ARM-FM, as a baseline. Each is a list of steps in one of four MiniGrid levels, small variations of one idea in which the agent must pick up a key to open a locked door. The thesis calls them the \textbf{key levels}. At the start of every episode, a key level places its walls, door, key and other objects anew, and this arrangement is the \textbf{layout} of the episode. The other groups try different rules and events where most of them use four small environments made for this thesis: the \env{service room}, the \env{zombie lanes}, \env{KeyGift} and the \env{shop}, in which things happen by themselves and the agent has to react. Appendix~\ref{app:eval-cards} shows one whole task as an example.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lr>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Group} & \textbf{Tasks} & \textbf{Environment} & \textbf{What the tasks test} \\ \midrule
Base & 7 & Four MiniGrid key levels & ARM-FM's MiniGrid tasks \\
Pilot & 2 & Two key levels, layout never changes & Two base tasks, fewer training steps \\
Complex & 6 & Two key levels and the service room & A long sequence, rules, goals and events \\
Templates & 80 & Service room, zombie lanes, key levels & Eight Declare templates, hard or soft \\
Box-carry & 14 & Service room, holding a falling box & Rules triggered by events, not the agent \\
Forcing & 4 & Zombie lanes and \env{KeyGift} & Rules the environment keeps triggering \\
Forced start & 2 & Key levels with a step already done & Tasks whose first step is already done \\
Shop & 11 & The shop & Many rules merged in one task \\ \midrule
All & 126 & & \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\caption{The eight groups of the benchmark.}
\label{tab:eval-suites}
\end{table}

Training needs one more property of a task, because an if and \textbf{idle agent}, plays like the idle plan, and the task passes, we cannot show whether an agent learned it. 

\keyidea{A task that an idle agent passes tests nothing in training.}

An idle agent passes 18 tasks of the benchmark, 17 of the \textbf{templates group} and one of the \textbf{complex group}. None of them has a goal, and an idle agent keeps all their hard rules. They are therefore not trained, and only their RMs are scored. A task with only soft rules stays in training when the idle agent breaks one of its rules or leaves one unmet. The \textbf{forcing group} and the \text{shop} exist for this same reason, their environments keep triggering the rules, so an agent that waits fails.

Out of the 126 tasks of Table~\ref{tab:eval-suites} two are the same, leaving 124 different ones. The two pilot tasks repeat two base tasks with the same layout in every episode. The training experiment trains 53 of these 124. Of the other 71, 18 tasks an idle agent passes and the other 53 tasks not trained, are only scored in the generation experiment (Section~\ref{sec:eval-machines}). Most of these 53 are variants of a trained task, with the same rule in another environment, size or layout, so they do nos who anything meaningful at training.

Seven of the 53 trained tasks train twice, each time under a second setting. The two \textbf{pilot} tasks train with the same layout in every episode, and five \textbf{shop} tasks train with the environment's reward added to the RM's. Each repeat counts as a task of its own, so the training experiment has 53 + 7 = 60 tasks. Eleven of them are \textbf{step-by-step tasks}, the kind ARM-FM was built for, which name every step in order, and the other 49 are new kinds of task. Appendix~\ref{app:eval-cards} gives the details of the groups and of the 60 trained tasks.

\paragraph{Tasks IDs} Appendix~\ref{app:cards} gives the text of every task named in the thesis because the tables name tasks by ID. An ID starts with its group, as \texttt{tpl-} for templates or \texttt{cx-} for complex. A template task then names its template, adds \texttt{soft} for a soft rule, a number that tells apart tasks of the same kind, and \texttt{goal} or \texttt{nogoal}, as in \card{tpl-absence-soft-1-goal}. A \texttt{\textasciitilde p} with a number marks a rewording such as \cardrow{tpl-precedence-1-goal~p3} . 

\section{Qualitative evaluation of the Reward Machines}
\label{sec:eval-machines}
The generation experiment tests the first claim, that FARM builds a right RM from a task in plain language with no human intervention. It sets FARM beside ARM-FM, the method that FARM extends, and it trains no agent. 

This section first presents the two methods and how each builds its RMs. It then presents what the experiment measures on them: whether an RM is built, whether it is right, whether every wording of a task gives the same RM and how large the RMs are.

\subsection{The two methods}
\label{sec:eval-arms} We compare the ARM-FM as the baseline to our FARM method. The ARM-FM method is a reconstruction, built from the appendices of its paper and, for the tasks in MiniGrid, the first author's public repository \cite{armfm_code}, without access to the official ARM-FM code. Parts that neither source documents are reconstructed. The rebuilt baseline may therefore differ from the original, and the results compare FARM with this reconstruction. 

Between the two methods, only the way the RM is built changes (which is the goal of this work). In FARM, the generator chooses clauses and the compiler builds the RM from them (Chapter~\ref{chap:farm}). In ARM-FM, the generator writes the RM itself as text. The rest is shared. 

Each method builds one RM for the text of every task and one for each of its rewordings. Then, a second phase writes the labelling function, and every RM state gets a plain-language description. Both methods use the default pair of models, \texttt{deepseek-v4.1-flash} as generator and \texttt{glm-5.3-flash} as critic. Others are tested on the thinking-level study (Section~\ref{sec:eval-built}).


\subsection{What is measured}
Three things are measured on the RMs of both methods, and a fourth compares their sizes. The first three are whether an RM is built, whether it is right (only with FARM) and whether every wording of a task gives the same RM. The fourth is the number of states and transitions of an RM.


\subsubsection{Whether an RM is built}
\label{sec:eval-built}The first measure is \textbf{how many RMs each method builds without human intervention}. For this to be accounted as such, the generated RM has to be right, and appropriate for that task. This is, pass its critic (see Section~\ref{sec:step5} for FARM's), its method's own checks (see Section~\ref{sec:checks} for FARM's) and run the plans check of that task (see next). If all holds, the generated RM is counted as built. Though is not yet said if that RM alone could make an agent learn the task.

\label{sec:eval-plan-check}\paragraph{Scripted-plan check} The \textbf{scripted-plan check} looks at what an RM pays rather than at what it states. It plays each scripted plan of the task through the RM, with the method's own labelling function reading every step. An RM passes when 
\begin{itemize}
    \item the solving plan does not enter the well and ends in a final state if there is one,
    \item when no plan that breaks a hard rule reaches a final state or ends in a well,
    \item and when every plan that breaks only soft rules is penalised,
    \item it does not end before the agent acts,
    \item and an RM with no final state must meet three more conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:eval-machines}).
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Whether an RM is right}
The second measure is whether an RM is right. A FARM RM is \textbf{right} when its chosen clauses match one of the task's accepted answers, exactly or by an equivalent formula. ARM-FM stores no clauses, so its RMs are not scored this way. 

In other words, given that the answers of FARM are not open, we can reliably define a test set for 'how it is should generate'. The same cannot be said for ARM-FM since its machine can get any shape.

\subsubsection{Whether an RM is robust}
The third measure uses the rewordings and asks whether every wording of a task gives the same RM. We check if the build machines on tasks with at least two built wordings, the RMs are identical, have the same size without being identical, or differ in size.

\subsubsection{Size of the RMs}
\label{sec:eval-size}The last measure is size, the number of states and of transitions of an RM. The sizes of FARM's and ARM-FM's RMs are compared on the trained tasks where both methods built an RM, and each method's sizes are summarised by their median.

\subsection{Two smaller studies}
Two smaller studies look at the language models behind FARM with the goal of observing the performance of different models against our tasks. Both run only FARM's generation, with no ARM-FM and no training, on 46 texts chosen where the generator had made mistakes before. Each text runs three times in each setting, all timed, and as said before, a run is right when its final clauses match an accepted answer of the task.

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{thinking-level study} changes how much the models reason before they answer, from level none to max (Section~\ref{sec:step5}). It measures how the share of right RMs and the time per RM change with the level.
    \item \textbf{study of other language models} replaces the default pair with one model from another maker, such as Anthropic's, OpenAI's or an open-weight one. 
\end{itemize}

\section{Empirical evaluation in a real environment}
\label{sec:eval-learning}
The training experiment tests the second claim, that an agent learns a task from its RM. Agents train on the 60 tasks of Section~\ref{sec:eval-cards} with three methods (\texttt{FARM}, \texttt{ARM-FM}, and \texttt{no RM}), and one judge decides for every method whether the trained agents do what the task asks. This section presents how the agents train, when a task counts as learned and which counts the results report. Appendix~\ref{app:eval-learning} gives the exact rules.

\subsection{How the agents train}
\label{sec:eval-training}The three methods differ in the reward the agent trains on. In FARM and ARM-FM, the agent trains on the reward of the method's RM, which can also be added to the environment's own reward. Training with no RM takes the environment's reward alone and serves as a baseline.

% NOTE-OPEN: cite PPO (Schulman et al. 2017) and MiniGrid; no keys in bib-keys.md.
All three methods train the same learner, Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), a reinforcement learning algorithm. Every task trains under the setting of its group. Most tasks share one setting, and the pilot and shop tasks have their own (Table~\ref{tab:eval-training}). In the FARM and ARM-FM methods, the RM follows the method's own labelling function, and the agent sees the environment's observation together with its current RM state. Most of their tasks train on the RM's reward alone. On the five repeated shop tasks, FARM's agents train on the RM's reward plus the environment's, and ARM-FM trains no shop task (Section~\ref{sec:res-react}). A side test adds the environment's reward to FARM's RM in the same way on the zombie lanes (Section~\ref{sec:eval-armfm-paper}).

% PICK RB24 (accepted by the author, 23 November, about 01:10)
With no RM, the reward depends only on the environment. Several tasks, even if they ask different things, for a no-RM agent are the same. They share the environment, the layout and every hyperparameter match. Of the 60 tasks to train, this method does 19 trainings because the other 41 are repeats of these 19.

\paragraph{Note:} To avoid the influence of randomness, each method trains three runs per task, with different random seeds. Each run is a full training but with the same RM and settings.

\subsection{When a task is learned}
\label{sec:eval-learned}After training, each run plays a set of \textbf{judged episodes}. A \textbf{judge} scores each of them by reading it through the benchmark's reference labelling, written with each environment, and checking the task's expected clauses on the trace. The judge is the same for every method and never uses a method's labelling function or RM, so a wrong RM cannot turn a failed episode into a pass. First of all:

\keyidea{A task whose RM was not built counts as not learned for that method.}

\paragraph{Success} A judged episode \textbf{succeeds} when every hard clause of the task holds, the goal included when the task has one. It fails when the method's RM, not the environment, ends it in a final state before the agent could act (Appendix~\ref{app:eval-learning}). Note that \textit{soft rules never decide success}. Each break of a soft rule counts instead in the \textbf{soft breaks per episode}. The \textbf{judged success} of a run is the share of its judged episodes that succeed, and the run \textbf{passes} at 0.8 or more.

\keyidea{A task counts as learned for a method when at least two out of three runs pass.}

\paragraph{Success on soft rules}  This rule does not work for a task with no goal and only soft rules, since with no hard rule every judged episode succeeds. The runs of such a task are compared instead with an idle agent on the same layouts, through their soft breaks and their soft rules \textbf{left unmet}, those still pending when the episode ends. 


\subsection{What the results count}
The results count the tasks that each method learns, over the 60 tasks and over four kinds of subset. They split the tasks by:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{kind}: step-by-step or new, 
    \item \textbf{goal}: whether the task has a goal or not, 
    \item \textbf{what}: what the task asks, 
    \item \textbf{group}: those listed at \ref{tab:eval-suites}.
\end{itemize} 
% PICK H2H
A last count pairs FARM and ARM-FM task by task. The \textbf{head-to-head} count sorts each of the 60 tasks by which of the two methods learn it, both, FARM only, ARM-FM only or neither.

\subsection{Further comparisons}
% NOTE-OPEN: cite XLand-MiniGrid; no key in bib-keys.md.
\label{sec:eval-armfm-paper}Smaller experiments go beyond the 60 tasks. Each changes one thing, the tasks, the environment or the training, to find where the methods reach their limits. They are side tests with fewer tasks and runs, so they are not counted with the 60, and Sections~\ref{sec:res-learning} and~\ref{sec:res-react} give their results.

\paragraph{The tasks of the ARM-FM paper} The first trains all three methods on tasks of the ARM-FM paper~\cite{creus2026armfm}. They are \env{DoorKey} from MiniGrid, \env{UnlockToUnlock} from BabyAI and tasks of XLand-MiniGrid, a second grid world. In XLand-MiniGrid, one agent can learn several tasks together. Each of its tasks therefore trains on its own and also in a mix of three or five tasks, where one agent learns them all.

\paragraph{The shop} This is one of the environments created just for this thesis. The shop is the hardest group of the benchmark, since it puts several complex rules together while the agent has to serve food to its customers. Several experiments are run on this group only to get an agent to learn it, as FARM's RMs for it are already right.

\paragraph{The zombie lanes} This is another of the environments created for this thesis. The agent has to react to the zombies and keep them from crossing the lanes, guarding one lane before the others. Three experiments change the setup. They make the environment smaller, add obstacles that force the agent to guard one lane first, and add the environment's own reward to FARM's RM.

\paragraph{Other limits} Three last experiments each test one limit: three times the training on two tasks, other room sizes for \env{KeyCorridor}, and training the ARM-FM RMs that were not built because they fail a check.

\section{Hardware}
Almost all experiments have been run on the same machine: a PC with an AMD Ryzen 9 9900X CPU, 64 GB of RAM and an NVIDIA GeForce RTX 5070 GPU with 12 GB of VRAM. Those that not, were ran on the cluster provided by the university. 

The private LLMs are accessed through their providers' APIs, and the open-weight ones either on the machine or at the cluster. 

All the code, tasks and results can be found at the repository of this thesis [REFERENCIA a https://github.com/MiquelGomezCorral/TFM-schema-rm-rl]

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%                                   RESULTS                                   %
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\chapter{Results}
\label{chap:results}

The two experiments of Chapter~\ref{chap:evaluation} test two claims, that FARM builds a right RM from a task in plain language and that an agent trained on that RM learns the task. Section~\ref{sec:res-comparison} compares the RMs that FARM and the rebuilt ARM-FM build for the same tasks, with no agent trained. Section~\ref{sec:res-thinking} sets other thinking levels and other language models beside the default pair. Section~\ref{sec:res-learning} trains agents, first on the tasks of the ARM-FM paper and then on the 60 tasks of the benchmark. Section~\ref{sec:res-react} ends in environments made for this thesis, a falling box, the zombie lanes and the shop, where something happens by itself and the agent must react. Tasks are named by their ID (Section~\ref{sec:eval-cards}), and Appendix~\ref{app:cards} gives the text of each task named.

\section{The generated RMs}
\label{sec:res-comparison}

The generation experiment asks FARM and the rebuilt ARM-FM for the same 266 RMs, one for the text of each of 111 tasks and one for each of the 155 rewordings of 52 of them. The 111 are the 126 tasks of Table~\ref{tab:eval-suites} without 15 that get no RM in this experiment. Ten of the 15 repeat another task in a changed environment and reuse its RM, and the other five are extra tasks of the further comparisons, a soft version of a box-carry task and four shop tasks. Both methods use the default pair of models (Section~\ref{sec:eval-arms}), and FARM builds its RMs at thinking level max.

\subsection{RMs built and right}
\label{sec:res-built}
\label{sec:res-farm-machines}

An agent can train with an RM only once the RM is built, so the first count is how many RMs each method builds with no human intervention (Section~\ref{sec:eval-built}). For FARM, a second count asks whether each built RM is right, that is, whether its clauses match an accepted answer of its task.

\keyidea{No RM was edited by hand. FARM builds 265 of the 266 RMs asked (99.6\%, 95\% Wilson interval 97.9\% to 99.9\%), and the rebuilt ARM-FM builds 152 (57\%, 51\% to 63\%).}

\noindent Figure~\ref{fig:res-built} shows both counts. FARM builds the RM of every task's text, and its one miss is a rewording. ARM-FM leaves 114 RMs unbuilt, which Section~\ref{sec:res-armfm-machines} explains.

\begin{figure}[!htb]
\centering
\input{images/results/fig-res-built.tex}
\caption[RMs built per method]{FARM builds 265 of its 266 RMs and ARM-FM 152. Each bar splits the 266 RMs asked into those built at the first round, those built after a further round and those not built. The whisker under each bar is the 95\% Wilson interval of the built share.}
\label{fig:res-built}
\end{figure}

Every RM that FARM builds is also right, so 265 of the 266 are right (99.6\%, 97.9\% to 99.9\%). Eight of them, on six template tasks, read an ambiguous sentence of their task in a second fair way, which was then added to the task's accepted answers. A model also reviewed every RM that FARM built against its task, and none is wrong in its rules (Appendix~\ref{app:eval-machines}). ARM-FM stores no clauses, so whether its RMs are right is not measured.

The one RM that FARM does not build is that of \cardid{shop-boil~p2}, a rewording of the shop task \card{shop-boil}. When the pot boils over, it asks the agent to put its load down, turn the stove down and pick the load up again. The shop environment has no event for putting food down, so this step has no exact rule. The generator added a seventh clause for it, a hard Response(\texttt{stove\_fixed}, \texttt{holding\_food}), which asks the agent to hold food again after every fix of the stove. The task also asks the agent to turn off the siren whenever it rings. With the seventh clause, the plan that ignores the siren pays no less than the solving plan. The scripted-plan check does not allow this for an RM with no final state (Appendix~\ref{app:eval-machines}), so it refuses the RM. The one miss is therefore a single extra rule, taken from a wording that the environment cannot state exactly, and the check stops it before any agent trains on it.

\paragraph{Rounds of generator and critic} Each build passes two phases, the RM and its labelling function. In each phase the critic accepts the generator's answer or sends it back (Section~\ref{sec:step5} for FARM's). A round is one answer of the generator, so a build takes at least two rounds, one per phase, and most RMs of both methods need no more. Of FARM's 265 built RMs, 262 pass both phases at the first round, and the other 3 need a second round after one rejection by the critic. Of ARM-FM's 152, 132 are built at the first round of each phase, 19 after a rejection by its critic and 1 after an invalid first answer. On average a built RM takes 2.01 rounds with FARM and 2.16 with ARM-FM (Appendix~\ref{app:res-counts}).

For both methods, then, the two phases usually pass at their first round, and no RM is lost to the critic. Every RM left unbuilt passed both phases and failed later, at the scripted-plan check.

\subsection{Why ARM-FM's RMs are not built}
\label{sec:res-armfm-machines}

ARM-FM builds its RMs in another way. Its generator writes each RM as text, and no compiler builds it from clauses. This subsection shows why 114 of these RMs are not built, and why so many of them come from tasks with no goal.

All 114 pass both phases and then fail the scripted-plan check (Section~\ref{sec:eval-plan-check}). Table~\ref{tab:res-armfm-failures} sorts them by the first problem the check finds, and one problem stands out. In half of them the start state is final, so every episode ends before the agent acts. In the next three rows, breaking a soft rule costs nothing, breaking a hard rule still reaches a final state, or the solving plan ends in no final state. The last row holds one RM whose labelling function crashes.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{First problem in the scripted-plan check} & \textbf{RMs not built} & \textbf{of them, on the 60 trained tasks} \\ \midrule
Start state final & 57 & 18 \\
Soft break costs nothing & 27 & 6 \\
Hard break reaches a final state & 20 & 3 \\
Solving plan ends in no final state & 9 & 0 \\
Labelling function crashes & 1 & 0 \\ \midrule
All & 114 & 27 \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption[Why ARM-FM's RMs were not built]{Half of ARM-FM's 114 RMs not built end every episode at the start. Each line of the table counts the RMs whose first problem in the scripted-plan check is of that kind, out of the 114 not built and out of the 60 tasks trained. Appendix~\ref{app:res-armfm-examples} gives one task for each row.}
\label{tab:res-armfm-failures}
\end{table}

A start state marked final comes only with tasks that have no goal.

\keyidea{ARM-FM makes the start state final on 57 of the 135 RMs it wrote for tasks with no goal, and on none of the 131 for tasks with a goal. On these 57 RMs every episode ends before the agent acts.}

\noindent The 57 include all 16 RMs of the forcing group and 23 of the 24 RMs of the shop, and the last shop RM fails the check in another way, so ARM-FM builds no RM in either group. On \card{keygift-hard}, for example, a task that asks the agent to open the door whenever it holds the key, every episode ends at step 0.

The cause lies in what ARM-FM expects. Every task in its prompts ends on a goal that pays \gain{$+1$}. Its format has no final states (Listing~\ref{lst:armfm-doorkey}). The rebuilt ARM-FM, however, ends an episode when the RM enters a final state, as FARM does, so it asks the model to name them. A task with no goal has no goal state to name, so the model often marked the start state final. FARM, by contrast, states such tasks with rule templates such as Absence, and their RMs have no final state (Section~\ref{sec:rule-only}).

A task whose RM was not built counts as not learned for its method (Section~\ref{sec:eval-learned}). For ARM-FM, the 57 RMs above are such losses, all on tasks with no goal, and in the shop and the forcing group ARM-FM has no RM to train at all.

\subsection{The RMs side by side}
\label{sec:res-machines}

The built RMs of the two methods also differ in what they pay for and in their size. A FARM RM comes from the product of the automata of the chosen clauses and keeps hard and soft rules apart (Section~\ref{sec:reward-assignment}). Each step of progress pays \gain{$+1$}, and the finish pays \gain{$+10$} more. A hard break enters the well and ends the episode. The edge into the well costs \loss{$-10$}, or more when the task also has soft clauses (Section~\ref{sec:well-charge}), and a soft break costs \loss{$-1$}. An ARM-FM RM is a chain of steps through the level. It pays a fraction of a unit for each step, \gain{$+0.05$} to \gain{$+0.4$} over its 152 built RMs, and usually \gain{$+1$} for the last. Some of its RMs also penalise an event, such as the loss of the key, and none of them stores clauses or marks a rule as hard or soft.

Three tasks show the RMs side by side, each chosen for one difference between them:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{\env{DoorKey}} (\card{base-doorkey}). \textit{Use the key to open the door, then reach the goal.} One of ARM-FM's own tasks.
    \item \textbf{Blocked box} (\card{tpl-not-co-existence-soft-1-goal}). \textit{Pick up the box in the other room, but try not to both drop the key while the door is still closed and open the door.} A task that names fewer steps than its level needs.
    \item \textbf{Box carry} (\card{cx-box-carry}). \textit{Carry the box to the exit, and whenever the box falls, pick it up again.} A task whose environment repeats one event, the box falling, and whose ARM-FM RM pays each pick-up after a fall.
\end{itemize}
Blocked box sits on the \env{BlockedUnlockPickup} level, where moving the ball, taking the key and opening the door all come before the box, yet the task names only the box as a goal. Table~\ref{tab:res-ex-clauses} first gives the clauses FARM chose for each task. On Blocked box these clauses are a fair reading of the task, not the answer it lists first. Figure~\ref{fig:res-ex-machines} then draws both RMs of each task.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{5.4cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Task} & \textbf{Clauses chosen by FARM} & \textbf{LTLf} \\ \midrule
\env{DoorKey} & Precedence(\texttt{has\_key}, \texttt{door\_open}) & $(\neg\texttt{door\_open}\ \mathrm{U}\ \texttt{has\_key}) \lor \mathrm{G}\,\neg\texttt{door\_open}$ \\
  & Precedence(\texttt{door\_open}, \texttt{at\_goal}) & $(\neg\texttt{at\_goal}\ \mathrm{U}\ \texttt{door\_open}) \lor \mathrm{G}\,\neg\texttt{at\_goal}$ \\
  & Existence(\texttt{at\_goal}) & $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{at\_goal}$ \\ \midrule
Blocked box & Existence(\texttt{target\_picked\_up}) & $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{target\_picked\_up}$ \\
  & Absence(\texttt{key\_lost}), soft & $\neg\,\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{key\_lost}$ \\ \midrule
Box carry & Existence(\texttt{at\_exit}) & $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{at\_exit}$ \\
  & Response(\texttt{at\_exit}, \texttt{holding\_box}) & $\mathrm{G}\,(\texttt{at\_exit} \rightarrow \mathrm{F}\,\texttt{holding\_box})$ \\
  & Response(\texttt{box\_fell}, \texttt{holding\_box}) & $\mathrm{G}\,(\texttt{box\_fell} \rightarrow \mathrm{F}\,\texttt{holding\_box})$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabularx}

\label{tab:res-ex-clauses}
\end{table}

\begin{figure}[!t]
\centering
\input{images/results/fig-res-ex-machines.tex}

Three drawings show every transition, labelled \textit{propositions / reward} as in Figure~\ref{fig:ex3-rm}. The others show one arrow per pair of states, with its reward or \textit{mixed}, and $(\times n)$ for $n > 1$ transitions. Each task shows one point beyond the states and rewards of Figure~\ref{fig:res-ex-machines}:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{\env{DoorKey}.} FARM's RM enters u1 once the key has been acquired, and a later drop of the key does not undo it. A broken order enters the well, u2, and never leaves it. From u1 that break costs \loss{$-11$}, because the key has already paid \gain{$+1$} (Section~\ref{sec:reward-assignment}, \emph{Taking progress back}). ARM-FM's RM has no state for a broken order.
    \item \textbf{Blocked box.} ARM-FM's RM pays each step of the level on the way to the box, although the task asks only for the box. FARM's RM pays only the box, and it penalises a lost key, as its soft rule asks.
    \item \textbf{Box carry.} Both RMs follow the box as it falls and is picked up again, between u0 and u1. In FARM's RM that cycle pays 0. In ARM-FM's RM, however, each pick-up after a fall pays \gain{$+0.1$}, so the cycle pays every time the box falls. It is a rewarding loop, and FARM's compiler accepts no RM with one (Section~\ref{sec:step4}).
\end{itemize}

Blocked box shows what ARM-FM does on the \env{BlockedUnlockPickup} level. It writes the level's steps into its RM even when the task names only some of them. FARM's compiler, in contrast, adds nothing beyond the chosen clauses. The generator can still choose a requirement that the task does not spell out, and the task then accepts it as a fair reading (Section~\ref{sec:res-clauses}).

Box carry shows a rewarding loop, and it is not the only one. Such a loop appears in 24 of ARM-FM's 152 built RMs and in none of FARM's 265, since FARM's compiler refuses it. Three of the 24 were trained, all on tasks with a falling box. Their loop pays only when the environment drops the box, at most twice per episode, and no agent collected more than these falls pay.

Size is the last difference, the number of states and transitions of an RM (Section~\ref{sec:eval-size}), and FARM's RMs are the larger ones. Sizes are compared over the 33 trained tasks where both methods built an RM, with 31 distinct pairs of RMs because three box-carry tasks share one pair. On them, FARM's median RM has 5 states and 11 transitions, and ARM-FM's 4 states and 8 transitions. The gap does not hold on every task, since FARM's RM has more states on 17 of the 33, fewer on 9 and as many on 7. The 10 step-by-step tasks among them have the same medians as the 33. Table~\ref{tab:res-size} gives the sizes on ARM-FM's four own tasks and on Box carry. The gap is widest in the transitions of the two longer levels, 33 against 12 on \env{BlockedUnlockPickup} and 26 against 11 on \env{KeyCorridor}. Making the RMs smaller is work still to do (Chapters~\ref{chap:limitations} and~\ref{chap:future-work}).
