\subsection{Consistency and robustness}
\label{sec:res-clauses}

A language model reads each task, so an RM built from plain language could depend on how the task is worded, or on chance from one run to the next. Two results answer this question together. The rewordings test the wording, and repeated runs of one wording test chance.

Rewording a task \textbf{seldom changes} FARM's RM. On 47 of the 52 tasks with rewordings, FARM gives the same RM in every wording (Table~\ref{tab:res-rewording}). ARM-FM does so on 3 of the 29 tasks where it built at least two wordings, and gives RMs of different sizes on 19 of them. On the other 23 tasks it built fewer than two, so there is nothing to compare. FARM's RMs are also right about as often on the rewordings as on the texts, with all 111 texts and 154 of the 155 rewordings giving a right RM.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Tasks with rewordings} & \textbf{FARM} & \textbf{ARM-FM} \\ \midrule
Wordings built & 206 of 207 & 106 of 207 \\
Every wording built & 51 of 52 & 21 of 52 \\
At least two wordings built & 52 of 52 & 29 of 52 \\
\quad Same RM & 47 of 52 & 3 of 29 \\
\quad Same size, not identical & 2 of 52 & 7 of 29 \\
\quad Different sizes & 3 of 52 & 19 of 29 \\
Fewer than two built & 0 of 52 & 23 of 52 \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption[RMs across rewordings]{FARM gives the same RM across a task's wordings on 47 of the 52 tasks with rewordings, ARM-FM on 3 of the 29 tasks where it built at least two wordings. Each count is given out of its total. The first row counts the 207 wordings of these tasks, their texts included, the indented rows split the tasks with at least two built wordings, and the other rows count the 52 tasks.}
\label{tab:res-rewording}
\end{table}

Where FARM's RM does change, the wording leads the generator to another accepted answer, as on tasks that forbid doing both of two things or that set steps in order. A change of this kind needs a task that accepts more than one answer, and 15 tasks do. Each rewording ran once, so these changes rest on one run per wording.

Chance is measured in the thinking-level study, where each text runs three times at each level. Eleven of its tasks accept more than one answer, and these show which answer each run takes. A set is the three runs of one such task at one level, and it counts when at least two of its runs are right. In 51 of the 52 sets that count, the right runs choose the same answer (98\%, 95\% Wilson interval 90\% to 100\%). From low to max, the thinking level does not change the choice either (Appendix~\ref{app:res-choice}).

\keyidea{Where a rewording changes FARM's RM, it changes to another accepted answer. Each rewording ran once, and repeated runs of one wording choose the same answer in 51 of the 52 sets.}

\section{Language models and thinking levels}
\label{sec:res-thinking}
\label{sec:res-models}

The two smaller studies of Section~\ref{sec:eval-machines} compare the default pair at thinking level max, the setting of Section~\ref{sec:res-comparison}, with other thinking levels and other language models, over 138 runs per setting (46 texts, three runs each). The prompts were tuned with the default pair (Sections~\ref{sec:step1} and~\ref{sec:step5}), and every other model uses them unchanged and plays both roles, generator and critic. The build normally refuses a critic that is the same model as the generator (Section~\ref{sec:step5}), and the study of other models allows it so that one model is scored on the whole pipeline. \tbd{the 46 texts were also part of the prompt tuning: rerun on texts kept apart pending} The time of a run is the time to build one RM, covering the generator, the critic, the compiler and every further round after a rejection. Times compare only within one of three groups, the models called through a provider, those on the university's cluster and those on the PC used for this thesis. Figure~\ref{fig:res-thinking} therefore plots only the models called through a provider against their time. Figure~\ref{fig:res-open-models} orders the open-weight models, those of the cluster and of the PC, by the memory that their weights take, and Table~\ref{tab:res-llms} gives every model.

\begin{figure}[!htb]
\centering
\input{images/results/fig-res-thinking.tex}
\caption[Right RMs per model and thinking level]{Each point is a model, or the default pair, at one thinking level. The axes give its median seconds per RM, on a log scale, and its share of right RMs, with the 95\% Wilson interval. A point is filled, on the grey Pareto frontier, when no other point is better on one axis and at least as good on the other. Many intervals overlap, so the frontier ranks point estimates only.}
\label{fig:res-thinking}
\end{figure}

Each maker names its own thinking levels, so one level name need not mean the same reasoning in two models. On a model with only an on and off switch, high is thinking on. The times also depend on how each model is run. Those of Haiku 5.5 and GPT-6 Luna include a new command-line process per call. The models on the PC run with compressed weights, in the Q4 format and in Q5 for Llama 3.1 8B. About a third of gpt-oss-20b's weights do not fit on the GPU and stay in the PC's memory. The PC has one GPU with 12 GB of memory, and the GPUs used on the cluster have 48 GB each. gpt-oss-120b runs on two of them, and the other three models of the cluster on one.

With the default pair, right RMs rise from 105 of 138 at none to all 138 at high. Max, the level of the generation experiment, adds no right RM and raises the median time from 20 to 88 seconds. Of the other models, only Haiku 5.5 and Qwen3.8 27B get all 138 right, both at max. Among the models called through a provider, three settings come within one right RM of the default pair's 138. In median time per RM, Haiku 5.5 gets 137 right in 14 seconds at high and 138 in 111 seconds at max, and GPT-6 Luna gets 137 in 32 seconds at max. The default pair at high gets all 138 right in a median of 20 seconds, and none of the other settings called through a provider gets as many right in as little time. The intervals of Haiku 5.5, and of GPT-6 Luna at high and max, overlap those of the default pair from low to max, so this ranking rests on the point estimates (Figure~\ref{fig:res-thinking}).

Figure~\ref{fig:res-open-models} sets the open-weight models by what they need to run. The three whose weights fit the PC's 12 GB GPU get at most 111 of 138 right, and gpt-oss-20b, whose weights do not fit it, gets 92 at its best level, high. The three models of the cluster that fit one 48 GB GPU get 132 to 138 right at their best level. More memory does not always give more right RMs, since gpt-oss-120b, which needs two such GPUs, gets 126 at its best level. With thinking off, no open-weight model gets more than 109 right, whatever its size, and Llama 3.1 8B, which has no thinking mode, gets 7.

\section{Learning with the RMs}
\label{sec:res-learning}

The two sections before look at how each method and each language model builds its RMs, with no agent trained. The RMs of Section~\ref{sec:res-built} are built to train agents, and this section asks whether an agent trained on each method's reward learns its task. One judge scores every method in the same way, with the learned rule of Section~\ref{sec:eval-learned}. The section starts from the tasks of the ARM-FM paper, which check that the rebuilt ARM-FM works as its paper reports. It then trains the 60 tasks of the benchmark, and it ends with the tasks that FARM does not learn even though its RM is right.

\subsection{The tasks of the ARM-FM paper}
\label{sec:res-armfm-paper}

The first comparison checks the rebuilt ARM-FM on tasks from its own paper~\cite{creus2026armfm}, and all three methods train on them. Two of them are \env{DoorKey} from MiniGrid and \env{UnlockToUnlock} from BabyAI, a set of MiniGrid levels with language missions.
% NOTE-OPEN: cite BabyAI; no key in the bibliography.
In \env{DoorKey} the agent takes a key, opens a door and reaches a goal, at three sizes and on a layout that is new every episode or fixed. In \env{UnlockToUnlock} it opens two locked doors in turn and picks up a ball. The other tasks come from XLand-MiniGrid, where each task asks the agent to turn objects into others, once or twice, and to end next to the last object or holding it. The paper's task X1 trains alone, and its tasks X1 to X5 train in mixes of three or five tasks, in which one agent learns them all. A last check, which the paper calls zero-shot, trains one agent on two tasks, ZA and ZB, and judges it on a third, ZC, on which it never trained.

The count covers 16 tasks. They are the five \env{DoorKey} tasks, \env{UnlockToUnlock}, X1 and ZC trained alone, and the eight tasks of the two mixes. A task of a mix is judged on its own, and its three runs are the three runs of the mix. The zero-shot check judges ZC a second time, so it stays outside the 16. FARM and ARM-FM build all their RMs with no hand edit, and Appendix~\ref{app:res-armfm-paper} lists the tasks.

The paper uses another learner and another measure. On MiniGrid and BabyAI it trains a Deep Q-Network (DQN) for 10M steps and plots the episode reward over the first 300k, and on XLand-MiniGrid it trains Rainbow DQN, a variant of DQN, for 5M steps and reports the success rate. This thesis trains PPO, for 1.5M steps on the three small \env{DoorKey} tasks and for 5M on the rest, and reports judged success. The paper's column in Table~\ref{tab:res-armfm-paper} holds values read from its figures. They serve as a reference for the rebuild, and the comparison does not test whether the two results are equal.

X1 alone & $\approx$0.88 & 0.77 & 0.85 & 0.75 \\
ZC alone & -- & 0.77 & 0.82 & 0.69 \\
Mix of 3 tasks & $\approx$0.80 & 0.52 to 0.61 & 0.80 to 0.81 & 0.14 to 0.22 \\
Mix of 5 tasks & $\approx$0.96 & 0.09 to 0.31 & 0.61 to 0.80 & 0.01 to 0.08 \\

\caption[The tasks of the ARM-FM paper]{The paper's values ($\approx$) are read by eye from its figures. The other columns give the mean judged success over three runs, and over eight for FARM and for ARM-FM on \env{DoorKey} 16$\times$16 fixed. On a fixed layout a run is judged on one episode, so it scores 0 or 1. A mix gives the range over its tasks, the paper one value. -- marks a task the paper does not report.}
\label{tab:res-armfm-paper}
\end{table}

On MiniGrid and BabyAI the rebuild reaches the level its paper reports and learns all six tasks. A task counts as learned by its runs that pass, not by its mean (Section~\ref{sec:eval-learned}). On \env{DoorKey} 16$\times$16 with a fixed layout, 6 of the 8 runs pass for FARM and for ARM-FM, which is the 0.75 of the table. Training with no RM learns only the three small \env{DoorKey} tasks, which are at 1.00 for every method and so show nothing about the RM. \env{DoorKey} 16$\times$16 with new layouts is the one row that differs, in the rebuild's favour, at 0.89 against about 0.03. The paper's own run of 10M steps stays near that value, so the number of steps does not explain the difference.

On XLand-MiniGrid the tasks and the total of 5M steps are the same as the paper's. The learner and the measure differ, and the paper gives neither the size of its grid nor how a mix shares the steps. There the rebuild falls short of its paper once several tasks train at once, and it is clearly below the paper in the 5-task mix, at 0.09 to 0.31 against about 0.96. The runs do not show why. FARM is at or above the rebuild on every row. By the learned rule of Section~\ref{sec:eval-learned}, it learns 11 of the 16 tasks, against 6 for the rebuild and 3 for training with no RM. It learns 2 of the 3 tasks of the 3-task mix and 1 of the 5 of the 5-task mix, so it does not solve the mixes. On the zero-shot check the agents of both RM methods transfer, FARM's at 0.69 and ARM-FM's at 0.58, against 0.02 for training with no RM. No method passes that check by the learned rule, and the check does not rank the two.

The baseline holds on \env{DoorKey} and \env{UnlockToUnlock}, and on the XLand-MiniGrid mixes FARM's lead is a lead over this rebuild, not over the paper's results.

\subsection{The 60 tasks}
\label{sec:res-60}

The main training covers the 60 tasks of the benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:eval-cards}). Each method trains on the RMs it built for the tasks' texts in Section~\ref{sec:res-built}, and a task whose RM was not built counts as not learned.

\keyidea{FARM learns 43 of the 60 tasks trained (72\%, 95\% Wilson interval 59\% to 81\%), ARM-FM 29 (48\%, 36\% to 61\%) and training with no RM 23 (38\%, 27\% to 51\%).}

\noindent Figure~\ref{fig:res-learned} shows these counts. ARM-FM's count includes, as not learned, the 27 tasks where its RM was not built, most of them tasks with no goal and the shop (Section~\ref{sec:res-armfm-machines}). Table~\ref{tab:res-groups} then splits the counts by kind of task and by group, and FARM learns at least as many tasks as ARM-FM in every subset except the tasks with a goal.

\end{figure}

FARM's lead does not rest on the new kinds of task alone. The 11 step-by-step tasks are the kind ARM-FM was built for, and FARM learns all 11 (100\%, 95\% Wilson interval 74\% to 100\%), ARM-FM 8 (73\%, 43\% to 90\%). The same holds on the 6 tasks with the text of the ARM-FM paper, where FARM learns all 6 (100\%, 61\% to 100\%) and ARM-FM 5 (83\%, 44\% to 97\%). The one task ARM-FM misses there is \env{KeyCorridor}.

The gap widens on the 49 tasks of new kinds, and most of it comes from RMs that ARM-FM did not build. On the 15 tasks with no goal outside the shop, FARM learns 14 (93\%, 70\% to 99\%) and ARM-FM 4 (27\%, 11\% to 52\%), since its RM was not built on 11 of them. ARM-FM also builds no RM for any of the 4 tasks of the forcing environments or the 11 shop tasks, so it learns none of them. FARM learns 3 of the shop tasks and training with no RM 1, none of them with a serving rule, and Section~\ref{sec:res-shop} returns to the shop.

Tasks with a goal turn the order around. On these 23 tasks ARM-FM learns 17 (74\%, 54\% to 87\%) and FARM 15 (65\%, 45\% to 81\%), and the two intervals overlap.

Split by clause instead, FARM is ahead on both halves of the 49. It learns 16 of the 29 tasks with hard rules but no order and no soft rule, against 13 for ARM-FM, and 16 of the 20 tasks with at least one soft rule, against 8.

A side test bounds this part of the gap. Of ARM-FM's 27 RMs not built, which all fail the scripted-plan check, 9 still let the agent act. Trained anyway, 7 of these 9 are learned, which would raise ARM-FM from 29 to 36 of the 60 tasks, still below FARM's 43.

% PICK H2H: from here to END PICK H2H, cut as one block.
Counts per method do not say whether the two methods learn the same tasks. The tasks that only one of FARM and ARM-FM learns show where the two differ, and Table~\ref{tab:res-h2h} sorts the 60 tasks by which of the two learn them. Of the 21 tasks that only FARM learns, ARM-FM's RM was not built on 18, and on the other 3 its agents trained without learning. The 10 tasks that neither learns are the 8 shop tasks with a serving rule (Section~\ref{sec:res-shop}), the forced-start task and one box-carry task (Appendix~\ref{app:res-boxcarry}).

\begin{table}[!htb]
\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
\toprule
 & \textbf{ARM-FM learns} & \textbf{ARM-FM does not} & \textbf{All} \\ \midrule
\textbf{FARM learns} & 22 & 21 & 43 \\
\textbf{FARM does not} & 7 & 10 & 17 \\ \midrule
\textbf{All} & 29 & 31 & 60 \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption[Head-to-head of FARM and ARM-FM]{FARM learns 21 tasks that ARM-FM does not, and ARM-FM 7 that FARM does not. Each cell counts the tasks of the 60 that FARM and ARM-FM each learn or not.}
\label{tab:res-h2h}
\end{table}

The 7 tasks that only ARM-FM learns share one level and one shape. Each sits on the \env{BlockedUnlockPickup} level and asks for the box, with one rule beside it. Three steps come before the box, moving the ball, taking the key and opening the door, and each task names one or two of them, never all three. ARM-FM's RM pays every step of the level and the box. FARM's RM pays the box and, on 3 of the 7 tasks, one step before it. On the other 4 the steps appear only inside a rule. There FARM's RM gives no reward before the box and only penalises breaking the rule. Over the whole training run, ARM-FM's agents pick up the box in at least 95\% of the episodes on 20 of the 21 runs and in 70\% on the other. FARM's agents do so in at most 2.4\%. On the same level FARM learns the tasks that name every step (Appendix~\ref{app:res-controls}). ARM-FM's reward on steps the task does not name may explain the gap. No run tests it.
% END PICK H2H

\paragraph{Soft rules} Soft rules never decide success (Section~\ref{sec:eval-learned}), so a task with a goal counts as learned even when its agents break a soft rule. Of the 20 tasks with at least one soft rule, FARM's agents break no soft rule on 14. The other six are two tasks whose soft rule sets an order (Precedence) and four zombie-lane tasks, on which the environment itself sets off the soft rules (Section~\ref{sec:res-react}). One of the order tasks, \card{tpl-precedence-soft-2-goal}, counts as learned although FARM's agents break its soft rule once per episode (1.00). Those trained with no RM break it as often, and ARM-FM's less (0.67).

A soft rule can also be left unmet, when what it asks for never happens. FARM's agents leave a soft rule unmet on one task with a goal, \card{tpl-responded-existence-soft-1-goal}, on two of its three runs. Training with no RM leaves unmet, in every episode, a soft rule that asks for something to happen at some point. It does so on four tasks with a goal and on two tasks with only soft rules, \card{tpl-existence-soft-1-nogoal} and \card{tpl-responded-existence-soft-1-nogoal}, so it does not learn those two. FARM learns all eight tasks with only soft rules. Table~\ref{tab:res-soft}, in Appendix~\ref{app:res-soft}, gives the soft breaks and the soft rules left unmet on every soft-rule task outside the zombie lanes.

\subsection{A right RM is not enough}
\label{sec:res-not-enough}

This last part turns to the tasks that FARM does not learn. FARM builds a right RM for every one of the 60 tasks (Section~\ref{sec:res-built}), and it still does not learn 17 of them (Table~\ref{tab:res-h2h}). Of these 17, 16 score 0.00 in every run. The 17th, \cardid{tpl-response-1-nogoal-forced}, has a mean judged success of 0.62, with runs from 0.55 to 0.67. The eight shop tasks with a serving rule are the largest part, and Section~\ref{sec:res-shop} turns to them. Four facts, from these tasks and from side tests, show what else decides whether an agent learns:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{The reward before the goal.} On the seven \env{BlockedUnlockPickup} tasks that only ARM-FM learns, FARM's right RM pays little before the box, and its agents almost never reach it (Section~\ref{sec:res-60}).
    \item \textbf{The judge.} On \cardid{tpl-response-1-nogoal-forced} the policy does the task when its actions are sampled, though its action probabilities stay close to random. The judge takes the most likely action at every step, and that play gets stuck on some layouts.
    \item \textbf{The training time.} With three times the training, 4.5M steps, \cardid{tpl-response-1-nogoal-forced} is learned, at 0.80 to 0.85 of its judged episodes against 0.55 to 0.67 before, while \card{tpl-responded-existence-2-goal} stays at 0.00 in every run.
    \item \textbf{The size of the environment.} FARM learns \env{KeyCorridor} with rooms of size 3 and of size 4, the size of the 60, and not with rooms of size 5 or of size 6.
\end{itemize}
A right RM states what the task asks. Whether the agent learns it also depends on how much the RM pays on the way to the goal, how long the agent trains, how the judge plays the policy and how large the environment is.

\section{Reacting to events}
\label{sec:res-react}

After the limits of Section~\ref{sec:res-not-enough}, the last experiments turn to rules that ask the agent to answer an event. A rule that answers an event holds while the event never happens, so an agent that does nothing passes it. The environments built for this thesis therefore make things happen that the agent does not cause, and the agent has to react to them. This section reports what agents learn in three of them. In the service room a box falls next to the agent, and in the zombie lanes zombies walk in by themselves. The shop follows in Section~\ref{sec:res-shop}. Each part looks again at the tasks of the 60 that run in the environment and adds runs that change the environment or the rule.

The methods compared depend on the tasks. ARM-FM's RMs are built for the box-carry tasks of this section, so all three methods train on them. In the zombie lanes ARM-FM builds an RM only for the task that asks for two kills, and in the shop it builds none (Section~\ref{sec:res-armfm-machines}). The experiments on the zombie lanes and the shop therefore compare FARM with training with no RM, and ARM-FM appears only on that one lanes task.

\subsection{A falling box}
\label{sec:res-box}

The box-carry tasks form a small experiment on how an agent behaves when a rule can or cannot always be met. In the service room the agent carries a box to the exit, and the box falls by itself next to it (Section~\ref{sec:eval-cards}). The rule asks the agent to pick the box up on its very next move. Whether one move can reach the box depends on how the fall turns the agent. In the default setup the fall turns the agent to face the box. In the other setups it may leave the agent one turn short, when the box lands behind it or on a coin flip, or it does not turn the agent at all. Of the five box-carry tasks among the 60, FARM learns 4 (80\%, 95\% Wilson interval 38\% to 96\%), and ARM-FM and training with no RM learn 3 each (60\%, 23\% to 88\%), with intervals that overlap widely. Four more tasks add the coin-flip setup and make the rule soft, with 3 runs per method. Two scripted agents show how often one move can reach the fallen box. The \textit{plan} is the task's solving plan, which waits for each fall in the start corner, and the \textit{walker} takes the shortest route to the exit and picks the box up whenever it lies on the floor. Table~\ref{tab:res-box} gives, for each setup, the share of falls that their next move can pick up over 1000 starts, and what each method learns with the rule hard and soft. Appendix~\ref{app:res-boxcarry} names the task of each row.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrcc@{}}
\toprule
 & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Falls the next move picks up}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Learned}} \\
\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\cmidrule(l){4-5}
\textbf{The fall turns the agent} & \textbf{Plan} & \textbf{Walker} & \textbf{Hard rule} & \textbf{Soft rule} \\ \midrule
To face the box & 100\% & 100\% & $\bullet\,\bullet\,\bullet$ & -- \\
One turn short if the box is behind & 100\% & 77.4\% & $\bullet\,\circ\,\circ$ & $\bullet\,\bullet\,\bullet$ \\
Not at all & 71.1\% & 41.1\% & $\circ\,\circ\,\circ$ & $\circ\,\bullet\,\bullet$ \\
One turn short on a coin flip & 51.6\% & 52.7\% & $\circ\,\circ\,\circ$ & $\bullet\,\bullet\,\bullet$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption[A falling box]{The two share columns give, for the plan and the walker, the falls that the next move can pick up. Each mark gives FARM, ARM-FM and training with no RM, in that order. $\bullet$ marks a task learned, $\circ$ a task trained and not learned, and -- a task not run.}
\label{tab:res-box}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Recovering after a miss} With the hard rule, in the setup where the fall does not turn the agent, only the agents trained with no RM pick the box up after a miss. FARM's RM sends a missed pickup to the well, which ends the episode (Section~\ref{sec:final-well}). Its agent never trains on what follows a miss, so nothing rewards a second try. Of the 300 judged episodes of FARM's agents in this setup, 66 have a miss and end at it. Played again with the episode going on after the miss, all 66 run to the step limit and the agent never picks the box up. ARM-FM's RM does not end the episode at a miss, so its judged episodes go on, and in the 88 that have a miss its agents do not pick the box up either. The agents trained with no RM pick it up after the first miss in all 195 of their episodes that have one.

With the soft rule a miss does not end the episode, so the agent can still pick the box up and carry it to the exit. In FARM's RM the miss costs \loss{$-1$}. ARM-FM and training with no RM learn all three soft tasks, and FARM learns two. It misses the one where the fall does not turn the agent, with 1 of 3 runs passing. Training with no RM learns these soft tasks as well, so they show that a soft rule lets the agent recover, not what an RM adds.

\paragraph{Meeting the rule every time} What the methods learn with the hard rule follows the share of falls the next move picks up for the two scripted agents. All three methods learn it when the fall turns the agent to face the box, where both agents pick up every fall. FARM also learns it when a box behind leaves the agent one turn short, where the solving plan still picks up every fall. Where both agents miss part of the falls, with no turn or on a coin flip, no method learns the hard rule.

\subsection{The zombie lanes}
\label{sec:res-lanes}

In the zombie lanes the agent has to stop zombies that walk in by themselves, and the tasks say which lanes matter. This subsection first presents the environment. It then follows who learns as the lanes move closer, which lane FARM's agents guard first, and what changes when plants stun the agent. Four things define the environment of the 60 tasks (Figure~\ref{fig:res-lanes-env}):
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Board.} It is 11 cells wide and 11 high, counting its outer wall, and three lanes cross it, four rows apart. The top lane is the one to protect. Plants sprout on the grass between the lanes during the episode, at most three at a time, and a plant the agent steps on is trampled.
    \item \textbf{Zombies.} They enter at the right end of a random lane, with odds that rise from 4\% to 17\% per step, and walk one cell left every three steps.
    \item \textbf{Agent.} It starts on the middle lane and kills a zombie by moving onto it.
    \item \textbf{Episode.} It lasts 300 steps, a zombie that gets through does not end it, and the environment pays 1 for each zombie killed.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[!htb]
\centering
\IfFileExists{images/results/env-lanes.png}{\includegraphics[width=0.6\linewidth]{images/results/env-lanes.png}}{\tbd{image of the zombie lanes}}
\caption[The zombie lanes]{The zombie lanes of the 60 tasks during an episode. Zombies come in from the cave at the right end of a lane, and here one walks on each lane. The top lane, edged in yellow and with a shield on its gate at the left end, is the one to protect. The agent stands on the middle lane. Three living plants and one trampled plant lie on the grass between the lanes.}
\label{fig:res-lanes-env}
\end{figure}

A zombie that crosses the board alive gets through its lane, and the tasks count these breaches by lane. Seven tasks run in the lanes, and Appendix~\ref{app:res-lanes} gives the clauses FARM chose for each. Three tasks have a hard rule. \card{lanes-guard} asks the agent never to let a zombie through the top lane, \card{lanes-rules} adds soft rules against breaches in the other lanes and against stepping on plants, and \card{tpl-existence-two-5-nogoal} asks for at least two kills. The other four have only soft rules, and their runs are compared with an idle agent (Section~\ref{sec:eval-learned}). Training with no RM trains one agent per run on the kill reward, and that agent is judged on every task.

\paragraph{The distance between lanes} The distance between lanes is the number of rows from one lane to the next, four in the 60 tasks. Further runs move the lanes two rows and one row apart. The board keeps its width and gets shorter, 11 by 7 and 11 by 5 cells with its wall. Table~\ref{tab:res-lanes-distance} gives who learns each task at each distance. Its first panel holds the lanes as presented above, called the plain lanes from here on. Its second panel holds a last setup, where plants stun the agent. That setup also runs with the lanes three rows apart, on a board of 11 by 9 cells.

\begin{table}[!htb]
\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
\toprule
\multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\textbf{A. Plain lanes}} \\
\textbf{Task} & \textbf{4 rows} & \textbf{2 rows} & \textbf{1 row} \\ \midrule
\card{lanes-guard} & FARM & FARM & both \\
\card{lanes-rules} & FARM & FARM & both \\
\card{tpl-existence-two-5-nogoal} & both & no RM & -- \\
\card{tpl-absence-soft-1-nogoal} & both & both & both \\ \midrule
\multicolumn{4}{@{}l}{\textbf{B. Plants that stun}} \\
\textbf{Task} & \textbf{4 rows} & \textbf{3 rows} & \textbf{2 rows} \\ \midrule
\card{lanes-guard} & both & both & both \\
\card{lanes-rules} & no RM & no RM & both \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption[Who learns in the zombie lanes]{Each column is a distance between lanes, and each cell names the methods that learn the task, FARM, training with no RM (no RM) or both. -- marks a distance not run. A task with only soft rules counts as learned when its agents break or leave unmet fewer soft rules than an idle agent, every break counted (Section~\ref{sec:eval-learned}).}
\label{tab:res-lanes-distance}
\end{table}

Three more tasks, with only soft rules, run only with the lanes four rows apart. They are \card{tpl-existence-two-soft-1-nogoal}, \card{tpl-not-co-existence-soft-1-nogoal} and \card{tpl-precedence-soft-1-nogoal}, and both methods learn all three.

In the plain lanes, FARM learns the two tasks with a hard rule on the top lane, \card{lanes-guard} and \card{lanes-rules}, at every distance, and training with no RM only with the lanes one row apart. The task that asks for two kills goes the other way at two rows, where training with no RM learns it and neither FARM nor ARM-FM does. With the lanes four rows apart all three methods learn it. It is not run at one row, where a random agent already succeeds in about two of three episodes.

\paragraph{The top lane} FARM's agents guard the top lane first when the task makes that rule hard, as \card{lanes-guard} and \card{lanes-rules} do. The absence task, \card{tpl-absence-soft-1-nogoal}, instead asks the agent to let no zombie through any lane, with one soft rule for the top lane and one for the side lanes. With the lanes four rows apart, FARM's agents let 8.61 zombies per episode through the top lane, about as many as the idle agent's 8.66, and cut the side lanes from the idle agent's 17.52 to 10.13. Their RM penalises a breach in either kind of lane alike, and the agents cut the breaches where most of them happen. Training with no RM kills about twice as many zombies, 17.63 per episode against 7.95, and lets only 0.53 through the top lane. Both methods learn the task.

Adding the environment's kill reward to FARM's RM changes little, and in the plain lanes its agents learn the same tasks as with the RM alone, except \card{lanes-rules} with the lanes four rows apart, where 1 of 3 runs passes.

\paragraph{Plants} A last setup changes the plants and the end of the episode, and runs \card{lanes-guard} and \card{lanes-rules} with the lanes four, three and two rows apart. No plant sprouts. Instead 9, 6 and 3 plants stand on the grass from the start at these three distances, and a plant the agent steps on stuns it for two steps. A zombie through the top lane also ends the episode, for both methods. Both methods learn the guard task at every distance. On the rules task, training with no RM learns at every distance, and FARM only with the lanes two rows apart (Table~\ref{tab:res-lanes-distance}). Training with no RM was also run on the kill reward without the early end, three runs at each distance, and 1 of these 9 runs passes, with the lanes four rows apart.

The rules task also asks the agent not to step on plants, and here the two methods differ in what they can learn. The task states this rule, so FARM's RM gives a negative reward of \loss{$-1$} each time the agent steps on a plant (Appendix~\ref{app:res-lanes}). Training with no RM has no reward for plants and can learn to keep off them only from the stun. With the lanes two rows apart, where both methods learn the task, FARM's agents trample no plant in 82\% of the judged episodes, against 16\% for training with no RM. At that distance a zombie gets through a side lane in all 300 judged episodes of FARM's agents, and in 292 of the 300 of those trained with no RM.

In the plain lanes, FARM learns the two tasks with the hard top-lane rule at every distance. With only soft rules, as in the absence task with the lanes four rows apart, its agents do not guard that lane first. Where plants stun and the lanes are two rows apart, its agents keep off the plants in more episodes than those trained with no RM, and neither method stops the breaches in the side lanes. Across the three distances of that setup, where a zombie through the top lane ends the episode, training with no RM learns all 6 tasks and FARM 4.

\subsection{The shop}
\label{sec:res-shop}

The shop tests whether an agent learns to serve clients and also to answer two events, a siren and food that falls.

\paragraph{The environment} In the shop, clients come in and wait to be served, and the agent has three things to handle:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Serving.} The agent takes each order at the counter, fetches food from the pantry, cooks it at the stove and serves it.
    \item \textbf{Siren.} At times a siren rings, and the agent turns it off at a switch.
    \item \textbf{Falls.} Food that the agent carries can fall, and the task asks the agent to pick it up on its very next move.
\end{itemize}
Figure~\ref{fig:res-shop-env} shows the 5$\times$5 shop. The default shop is larger, with 8$\times$8 cells, and the shops of 7$\times$7, 5$\times$5 and 3$\times$3 cells keep the same task text and the same RM on a smaller floor. The pot that boils over in \card{shop-boil} is left out of these experiments.

\begin{figure}[!htb]
\centering
\IfFileExists{images/results/env-shop.png}{\includegraphics[width=0.6\linewidth]{images/results/env-shop.png}}{\tbd{image of the shop}}
\caption[The shop]{The 5$\times$5 shop, inside its wall. The client waits at the counter on the left, next to the register, with the order in a bubble above. The agent carries food. The stove is at the top, with the siren lamp beside it, and the switch that turns the siren off is at the bottom. The door is on the left and the pantry shelf on the right.}
\label{fig:res-shop-env}
\end{figure}

On the shop tasks of the training experiment, no agent serves an order (Section~\ref{sec:res-60}). The experiments of this subsection therefore build serving up in levels and change the size of the shop, to find how far an agent gets. ARM-FM builds no RM for the shop (Section~\ref{sec:res-armfm-machines}), so, as on most of the zombie-lane tasks, these experiments compare FARM with training with no RM only.

\paragraph{The levels} Serving has three levels, each with one step more than the level before. At the first, the agent delivers food to each client who comes in. At the second, it takes the client's order first. At the third, it also cooks the food before serving it. The siren and the falls then come on top of the third level, first the siren alone and then both events together. Table~\ref{tab:res-shop} lists the five levels. Each level is a task of its own, and FARM trains it on the RM built from that task, so no RM asks for a step that its level leaves out. The rules on the siren and on falling food wait for their event, and they ask nothing when it is off. Every RM of the table can therefore hold the hard rule on falling food, also at the levels where no food falls, and the siren level shares the RM of \card{shop-full} with the last level.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccclr@{}}
\toprule
 & \multicolumn{4}{c}{\textbf{Jobs at the level}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{FARM's RM}} \\
\cmidrule(lr){2-5} \cmidrule(l){6-7}
\textbf{Level} & \textbf{Order} & \textbf{Cook} & \textbf{Siren} & \textbf{Falls} & \textbf{Task} & \textbf{Size} \\ \midrule
1. Deliver & & & & & \cardid{shop-ladder-deliver} & 5 / 24 \\
2. + take the order & \faCheck & & & & \cardid{shop-ladder-order-deliver} & 9 / 110 \\
3. + cook & \faCheck & \faCheck & & & \card{shop-serve} & 17 / 406 \\
4. + siren & \faCheck & \faCheck & \faCheck & & \card{shop-full} & 33 / 2,094 \\

\caption[The levels of the shop]{Every level delivers, and a check marks the other jobs it holds. The size of an RM is given as states / transitions. The siren level and the last level share the RM of \card{shop-full}. Appendix~\ref{app:res-shop} gives the clauses of \card{shop-serve} and \card{shop-full}.}
\label{tab:res-shop}

The RMs grow with the jobs. Each added rule that waits for an answer nearly doubles the RM, from 5 states on delivering to 33 on the full shop. The largest RM of the shop is that of \card{shop-boil}, which adds the pot and has 65 states and 10,662 transitions.

\label{sec:res-curriculum}On a larger floor the agent needs more moves of its own between the serving steps that earn reward. A shop that grows within one run tests whether learning to serve on a small floor helps on a larger one. It grows from 3$\times$3 to 5$\times$5 and then to 7$\times$7. Only the environment changes from one stage to the next. The RM, the agent's policy and its count of steps carry on. A stage ends by a threshold, not by a schedule. Over its last 100 finished training episodes, the agent must serve 60\% of the orders that the solving scripted plan serves in that stage's environment. A stage with falling food also needs 90\% of the falls caught, and a stage with the siren 90\% of the sirens turned off. The experiments below that change the environment within one run follow this rule, with one exception given with Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-falls}. In all of them, the judge plays the last stage.

The rest of this subsection trains each level in shops of four sizes, adds the events to the two lower levels, and ends with a curriculum of tasks and with an experiment on serving with one event.

\paragraph{Each level in four shops} The first experiment trains each of the five levels in four shops, the 3$\times$3, 5$\times$5 and 7$\times$7 shops and one grown from 3$\times$3 to 7$\times$7 (Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-grid}). Each method trains 3 runs of 5M steps for each level and shop, 120 runs in all, and a task counts as learned when at least 2 of its 3 runs pass (Section~\ref{sec:eval-learned}). Food falls only at the last level.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}

\toprule
 & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{3$\times$3}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{5$\times$5}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{7$\times$7}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Grown}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Shops learned}} \\
\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5} \cmidrule(lr){6-7} \cmidrule(lr){8-9} \cmidrule(l){10-11}
\textbf{Level} & FARM & no RM & FARM & no RM & FARM & no RM & FARM & no RM & FARM & no RM \\ \midrule
1. Deliver & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & 4 of 4 & 4 of 4 \\

3. + cook & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\circ$ & $\circ$ & $\bullet$ & $\circ$ & 3 of 4 & 2 of 4 \\

\end{table}

Each of the three levels of serving is learned in at least one shop, and the levels with more jobs are learned in fewer shops. Both methods learn delivering and taking orders in every shop, and cooking in the 3$\times$3 and 5$\times$5 shops. At 7$\times$7, cooking is learned only by FARM and only when the shop grows, with 2 of 3 runs passing against 1 of 3 in the fixed 7$\times$7 shop. Training with no RM passes none of its 3 runs in the fixed 7$\times$7 shop and 1 of 3 in the grown one. Neither method learns the last two levels, cooking with the siren and cooking with both events, in any shop. Over the 20 tasks, FARM learns 11 and training with no RM 10, with 32 and 30 of their 60 runs passing. The two methods thus learn about as many tasks, and FARM's one task more is cooking in the grown shop.

\paragraph{Falling food on the two lower levels} At the last level of Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-grid}, the falls come together with cooking and the siren, so that table does not show what the falls do on their own. A second experiment therefore trains the two lower levels, delivering and taking orders, with food that falls. It trains them in the 3$\times$3, 5$\times$5 and 7$\times$7 shops, in the default 8$\times$8 shop and in a grown shop, with 3 runs per task and method in each shop, except that training with no RM was not run in the grown shop. This grown shop moves on once the agent serves 10 orders per episode, not by the 60\% rule above. Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-falls} marks the tasks learned in each shop.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l*{10}{c}@{}}
\toprule
 & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{3$\times$3}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{5$\times$5}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{7$\times$7}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{8$\times$8}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Grown}} \\
\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5} \cmidrule(lr){6-7} \cmidrule(lr){8-9} \cmidrule(l){10-11}
\textbf{Task} & FARM & no RM & FARM & no RM & FARM & no RM & FARM & no RM & FARM & no RM \\ \midrule
Deliver + falls & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\circ$ & $\bullet$ & $\circ$ & $\bullet$ & $\circ$ & $\bullet$ & $\circ$ & -- \\

\caption[Falling food on the two lower levels]{$\bullet$ marks a task learned, $\circ$ a task trained and not learned, and -- a shop not trained. The grown shop goes from 3$\times$3 to 7$\times$7 and is judged at 7$\times$7.}
\label{tab:res-shop-falls}
\end{table}

With falling food, FARM learns both tasks only in the 3$\times$3 shop. There all 6 of its runs pass (95\% Wilson interval 61\% to 100\%), and in the 5$\times$5 and 7$\times$7 shops none of 12 do (0\% to 24\%), nor any of 6 in the default 8$\times$8 shop. Without falls, FARM learns the same two levels in the fixed 5$\times$5 and 7$\times$7 shops (Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-grid}), and with falls it learns neither of them there. Over their first 100 training episodes, its runs in these two shops serve 0.00 orders per episode, against 0.01 to 0.05 at 3$\times$3. Growing the shop helps in part. In the grown shop, 3 of 6 runs pass (19\% to 81\%), against 0 of 6 in the fixed 7$\times$7 shop, and the two intervals overlap. Two of the three runs on taking orders pass and one of the three on delivering, so FARM learns taking orders with falls in the grown shop, and not delivering.

Training with no RM learns both tasks in every fixed shop, with 6 of 6 runs passing at 3$\times$3 and 5$\times$5, 5 of 6 at 7$\times$7 (44\% to 97\%) and 5 of 6 at 8$\times$8. These results do not show why the falls stop FARM and not training with no RM.

Delivering was also trained with both the siren and the falls, in the three smaller shops, and neither method learns it there, with 0 of 9 runs passing for each (0\% to 30\%). At 3$\times$3, training with no RM serves 21.6 orders per judged episode on this task, as many as without the siren, and every one of its 300 judged episodes fails with the siren rule left unmet.

\paragraph{A curriculum of tasks} The curriculum of tasks \cardid{shop-task-curriculum} trains one agent towards the full shop in stages. Its first stage is the cooking level in the 3$\times$3 shop, which both methods learn on its own (Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-grid}). The later stages add the falls, then the siren, then the 5$\times$5 shop and last the 7$\times$7 shop, by the rule above. FARM trains every stage under the RM of \card{shop-full}, whose rules on the events ask nothing when their event is off. The experiment asks whether serving first helps the agent with the events, and then with the larger shop.

Each run trains for 15M steps, against 5M for the tasks of Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-grid}, with 6 runs of FARM and 3 with no RM. Four of the six runs of FARM and two of the three with no RM meet the rule at the 3$\times$3 stage with the siren and the falls, and move on to the 5$\times$5 shop, where they stall. The other two runs of FARM stall at that 3$\times$3 stage, and the third run with no RM at the 3$\times$3 stage with the falls alone. No run enters the 7$\times$7 shop, and no run serves an order in its judged episodes, which the judge plays in the 7$\times$7 shop. Started from an agent that already cooks and serves, most runs thus cope with the siren and the falls at 3$\times$3 in training, and none at 5$\times$5. Passing a stage is a rule on training episodes, not judged success. Trained from the start in that same 3$\times$3 shop and judged, no run of either method passes (the last level of Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-grid}).

\paragraph{Serving with one event} The last experiment asks whether serving is learned with one event on top of it, the siren or the falls, when the event comes on only once the agent serves. Its four tasks are delivering and cooking, each with one of the two events, and each trains on the RM of an earlier task (Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-pairs}). In a fixed shop the event is off in the first stage and on in the second. The grown shop goes from 3$\times$3 without the event to 3$\times$3 with it, and then to 5$\times$5 and 7$\times$7. Each method trains 3 runs of 5M steps for each task in the four shops of Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-grid}, 96 runs in all. Delivering with the falls on from the start is part of Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-falls}, and cooking with the siren on from the start is the siren level of Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-grid}. The other two tasks have no earlier run.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{2pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll*{8}{c}@{}}
\toprule
 & & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{3$\times$3}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{5$\times$5}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{7$\times$7}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Grown}} \\
\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-6} \cmidrule(lr){7-8} \cmidrule(l){9-10}
\textbf{Task} & \textbf{FARM's RM} & FARM & no RM & FARM & no RM & FARM & no RM & FARM & no RM \\ \midrule
Deliver + falls & \cardid{shop-ladder-deliver} & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ \\
Deliver + siren & \cardid{shop-ladder-deliver-siren} & $\bullet$ & $\circ$ & $\bullet$ & $\circ$ & $\circ$ & $\circ$ & $\circ$ & $\circ$ \\
Cook + falls & \card{shop-serve} & $\bullet$ & $\circ$ & $\circ$ & $\circ$ & $\circ$ & $\circ$ & $\circ$ & $\circ$ \\
Cook + siren & \card{shop-full} & $\bullet$ & $\bullet$ & $\circ$ & $\bullet$ & $\circ$ & $\circ$ & $\circ$ & $\circ$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption[Serving with one event]{$\bullet$ marks a task learned and $\circ$ a task trained and not learned. FARM's RM names the task whose RM each task trains on. Cooking stands for the third level, which also takes the order. The grown shop goes from 3$\times$3 without the event to 7$\times$7 with it and is judged at 7$\times$7.}
\label{tab:res-shop-pairs}
\end{table}

Counted shop by shop, as in Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-grid}, FARM learns 8 of the 16 tasks (95\% Wilson interval 28\% to 72\%) and training with no RM 6 (18\% to 61\%), with 21 and 15 of their 48 runs passing. The two intervals overlap, so FARM's two tasks more do not set the methods apart. Both methods learn 5 of the tasks, delivering with the falls in all four shops and cooking with the siren at 3$\times$3. Only FARM learns 3, delivering with the siren at 3$\times$3 and 5$\times$5 and cooking with the falls at 3$\times$3. Only training with no RM learns 1, cooking with the siren at 5$\times$5, and neither method learns the other 7. Apart from delivering with the falls, no task is learned at 7$\times$7 or in the grown shop. Delivering with the falls and cooking with the siren were also trained with the event on from the start, and on both more is learned when the event comes on later. With the falls on from the start, FARM learns delivering only at 3$\times$3 (Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-falls}), and with falls that come on later it learns it in all four shops. Training with no RM learns it in every fixed shop either way. Neither method learns cooking with the siren on from the start in any shop (Table~\ref{tab:res-shop-grid}). When the siren comes on later, FARM learns it at 3$\times$3 and training with no RM at 3$\times$3 and 5$\times$5.

Training with no RM learns delivering with the siren in no shop. In 11 of its 12 runs the agent never turns the siren off in any judged episode. At 3$\times$3 its agents still deliver about 21 orders per episode, as many as FARM's, and the one agent that does turn the siren off leaves it ringing at the end of 68\% of its episodes. Its only reward is the shop's own (Section~\ref{sec:eval-training}), and this reward penalises a ring only once waiting clients have heard it for longer than three times the walk from the counter to the switch. For the agents that never turn the siren off and still deliver, this penalty adds up to between \loss{$-0.1$} and \loss{$-1.5$} per episode, against \gain{$+19$} to \gain{$+22$} for serving. The shop's reward thus asks for less than the task does, while in FARM's RM turning off every siren is a hard rule. Cooking with the siren at 5$\times$5 is the one task that only training with no RM learns, and its two runs that pass turn off nearly every ring.

Without the events, then, each level of serving is learned in at least one shop, and FARM also learns cooking at 7$\times$7 when the shop grows. With one event that comes on only once the agent serves, FARM learns 8 of the 16 tasks and training with no RM 6. Delivering with the falls is then learned by both methods in every shop, and cooking with one event only in the 3$\times$3 and 5$\times$5 shops. With both the siren and the falls, neither method learns serving under the learned rule of Section~\ref{sec:eval-learned}, in any shop and also after the curriculum of tasks.

\chapter{Conclusions}
\label{chap:conclusions}

The main objective of this thesis is a fully automated generation of RMs from tasks stated in plain language, on which agents learn the tasks. A further aim of the thesis is a curriculum for the shop. FARM addresses the main objective by changing one step of ARM-FM, the generation of the RM. A language model writes the task as clauses from a fixed catalogue of templates, each hard or soft, and fixed tools then compile the clauses into the RM and set the reward of each transition.

\keyidea{The RM follows the chosen clauses by construction, but whether the clauses say what the person meant rests on the generator and its critic.}

\noindent The main objective is \textbf{met}. No RM was edited by hand, and FARM builds 265 of the 266 RMs asked for (99.6\%, Section~\ref{sec:res-built}). Agents also learn more tasks with these RMs than in the other two methods. Of the 60 tasks of the training experiment, they learn:
\begin{itemize}
    \item 43 of 60 (72\%, 95\% Wilson interval 59\% to 81\%) with FARM's RMs;
    \item 29 of 60 (48\%, 36\% to 61\%) with the RMs of the rebuilt ARM-FM;
    \item 23 of 60 (38\%, 27\% to 51\%) with no RM.
\end{itemize}
\noindent A task whose RM was not built counts as not learned for that method (Section~\ref{sec:res-learning}). The claim is about tasks learned, not about how fast they are learned.

% PICK H2H: from here to END PICK H2H, cut as one block.
FARM's lead over ARM-FM comes mostly from tasks on which ARM-FM builds no RM. Of the 21 tasks that only FARM's agents learn, ARM-FM's RM was not built on 18. In the other direction, the 7 tasks that only ARM-FM's agents learn all sit on one level, \mbox{\env{BlockedUnlockPickup}}, where the agent moves a ball, takes a key and opens a door before it picks up a box. They name one or two of these three steps, never all three (Section~\ref{sec:res-60}). ARM-FM's RM pays for each of those steps, asked for or not, while FARM's keeps to the task and leaves little reward before the box. That difference in reward may explain these 7 tasks, but no run tests it.
% END PICK H2H

As for the RMs themselves, rewording a task seldom changes FARM's, and it changes ARM-FM's more often (Section~\ref{sec:res-clauses}). The rewordings were built and not trained, however, so this holds for the RMs and says nothing about learning.

Two things can still go wrong. First, the generator can add a rule of its own. It did so on one rewording of a shop task, and the scripted-plan check refused the RM (Section~\ref{sec:res-farm-machines}). Second, where the wording is ambiguous, the reading the generator takes decides the RM (Chapter~\ref{chap:limitations}).

% PICK SHOP: framing to confirm with the author
In the shop, however, no agent serves an order on the 11 shop tasks of the training experiment, whether it trains on FARM's RM, on the environment's reward or on both (Section~\ref{sec:res-shop}). ARM-FM trains none of these tasks, since none of its 24 shop RMs is built and 23 of them make the start state final (Section~\ref{sec:res-armfm-machines}).

This further aim, a curriculum for the shop, is met \textbf{in part}. With serving split into levels and no falling food, FARM and training with no RM both learn delivering and taking orders in every shop and cooking up to 5$\times$5. Only FARM learns cooking at 7$\times$7, and only in the shop that grows (Section~\ref{sec:res-curriculum}). Less is learned with the events. With falling food from the start, FARM learns delivering only at 3$\times$3. It learns taking orders there and in the grown shop, while training with no RM learns both in every fixed shop. When one event comes on only once the agent serves, FARM learns 8 of the 16 tasks and training with no RM 6. Both then learn delivering with the falls in every shop, and cooking with one event is learned only in the 3$\times$3 and 5$\times$5 shops. With the siren and the falls together, no method learns serving in any shop, also after the curriculum of tasks (Section~\ref{sec:res-shop}).

RM size stays open as well. FARM's RMs are larger than ARM-FM's and grow with the number of clauses, so the largest are hard to read. Making them smaller is work still to do (Chapter~\ref{chap:future-work}).

Table~\ref{tab:conc-summary} gathers these points as the advantages and disadvantages of FARM.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Advantages} & \textbf{Disadvantages} \\ \midrule
The RM follows the chosen clauses exactly and adds no step they do not state (Chapter~\ref{chap:farm}; Section~\ref{sec:res-machines}). & The model can still choose clauses that do not fit the task, and ambiguous wording decides the RM (Section~\ref{sec:res-clauses}; Chapter~\ref{chap:limitations}). \\ \midrule

\end{tabularx}

\label{tab:conc-summary}
\end{table}

\section{Scope and limits of the study}
\label{sec:scope}
The conclusions above hold within the bounds of the study. Some of these bounds come from how the comparison with ARM-FM is set up and from the models it uses, and others from the tasks that FARM can state.

FARM changes only one step of ARM-FM, the generation of the RM. The labelling functions, the state descriptions and their embeddings follow ARM-FM and sit outside the main comparison, so the comparison with ARM-FM is isolated to that step. The ARM-FM that FARM is compared with is a reconstruction, rebuilt from the paper and from a public repository of its first author. Its results may therefore differ from the original ones (Section~\ref{sec:critique}). Both methods also run on the same two models, \texttt{deepseek-v4.1-flash} as generator and \texttt{glm-5.3-flash} as critic, where the ARM-FM paper used GPT-4o. These models are small and cheap on purpose. They are not the strongest available, but they are enough to test the method and they keep it accessible. Whether the comparison holds with other models is not tested. FARM's generation alone is scored with ten other models, on 46 texts with three runs each (Section~\ref{sec:res-models}).

The other bounds concern the tasks. FARM states tasks with the eight Declare templates of IBM nl2ltl, read as LTLf over finite traces. A task that these templates cannot state is outside its reach (Section~\ref{sec:lim-conditions}), and a model that writes LTLf directly is future work (Section~\ref{sec:fw-ltlf}). The work also leaves out tasks that ask to optimise a quantity, such as \textit{Kill as many zombies as you can} or \textit{Reach the goal by the shortest path}, and behaviour that a person cannot put into words. A clause is met or broken over a whole run, so it cannot ask for as much of something as possible, and a behaviour with no words gives no clauses to write (Section~\ref{sec:lim-quantities}). The first group of bounds limits what the comparison with ARM-FM shows, and the second limits the tasks that FARM can state.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%                                 FUTURE WORK                                 %
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\chapter{Future work}
\label{chap:future-work}

One study of the Results chapter still waits for data, the study of other language models, which still lacks two small open-weight models (Section~\ref{sec:res-models}). Beyond it, the five directions below go past this thesis. Each one gives what is missing today, why it matters and a first step.

\section{Smaller RMs}
\label{sec:fw-size}
A FARM RM is the product of one automaton per clause. It is minimised by merging the states that behave the same, but it still grows with the number of clauses (Sections~\ref{sec:step4} and~\ref{sec:lim-size}).

The boil task of the shop, which has six hard rules, shows this growth. Each of these rules is either waiting for its answer or not, so the RM holds one state for each combination of the six, plus the well. That gives 65 states and 10,662 transitions (Section~\ref{sec:res-shop}). The RM is already minimised giving the conditions in which it was built, so none of its states can be merged without changing what it pays.

The first step in this direction could be to measure how the size grows with the number and the kind of rules, on tasks built for that purpose. Another more direct direction is presented in the next section. 

\section{A model that writes LTLf}
\label{sec:fw-ltlf}
The generator picks clauses from eight templates whose arguments are single declared facts. A task can therefore say no more than those templates state (Section~\ref{sec:lim-conditions}), and the generator cannot use other logical constructs to simplify the formulas of a task.

A further step is a generator that writes the LTLf formulas itself. Such a model could state negated conditions, alternatives and nested conditions. This way, it could give shorter formulas and smaller RMs, and state tasks that the templates cannot. In exchange, it would give up what the fixed catalogue gives, a known meaning, automaton and price for every clause. 

For example, a shop task with a chain of four steps: every client who arrives ($a$) has the order taken ($b$), then the food prepared ($c$), then the order served ($d$). With the templates, the generator states the chain link by link, as three Response clauses:
\[
\mathrm{G}\,(a \rightarrow \mathrm{F}\,b) \;\wedge\; \mathrm{G}\,(b \rightarrow \mathrm{F}\,c) \;\wedge\; \mathrm{G}\,(c \rightarrow \mathrm{F}\,d)
\]
Each clause is a promise of its own, so the RM keeps one state for every combination of promises still open, 8 states and 55 transitions. A model that writes LTLf can state the chain as a single promise, with each step nested inside the one before:
\[
\mathrm{G}\,\bigl(a \rightarrow \mathrm{F}\,(b \wedge \mathrm{F}\,(c \wedge \mathrm{F}\,d))\bigr)
\]
Its RM has 4 states and 15 transitions, one with nothing owed and one for each step still owed. The reward changes with it, however. With three clauses, the RM takes three units when the chain opens and pays one back at each later step. With the single formula, it takes one unit when the chain opens and pays it back when the chain closes, with nothing in between.

The rest of the pipeline should hold. Whether it does still needs testing. A first step is to ask such a model for formulas on tasks the catalogue cannot state, and to pass them through the same checks.

\section{A way to refuse}
\label{sec:fw-refusal}
The generator's answer is a list of clauses with no way to refuse. A task it should refuse therefore ends in failed attempts, or in clauses that come close and do not say what they leave out (Section~\ref{sec:lim-anomalies}).

A field for a refusal, with its reason, would let the model report a task that cannot be done, a fact the environment does not declare or a wording it cannot settle. A person could then fix the task or the environment description. 

The first step is to add the field  on the generator output schema, adjust the prompt and test it on tasks built to be refused.

\section{Counting}
\label{sec:fw-counting}
The templates count only to two, with ExistenceTwo, which asks for an event at least twice (Section~\ref{sec:step1}). Beyond two, ``at least $n$ times'' needs an LTLf formula (Section~\ref{sec:fw-ltlf}) that grows with $n$. But an \textbf{unbounded} count, such as ``one kill for each zombie that enters``, cannot be stated in LTLf.

A count would let an RM pay for each kill up to a fixed number, with no rewarding loop. Such an RM would come closer to the reward of the zombie lanes, where the environment pays for each kill (Section~\ref{sec:res-react}). 

The first step is to find how a count would be written as a clause and priced, if even possible at all with RMs, since their nature does not allow unbounded counting.

\section{The parts inherited from ARM-FM}
\label{sec:fw-inherited}
The parts that FARM takes from ARM-FM unchanged, the labelling function, the state descriptions and the training, can each be improved.

The labelling function is one case. A model writes its code, and its critic reads that code without seeing the labels it gives in the environment. The scripted-plan check catches some faults, such as the labelling function of one ARM-FM RM, which crashes (Table~\ref{tab:res-armfm-failures}). However, it catches them only on the plans it runs. 

The state descriptions are another. The agent sees its current RM state as a text embedding of that state's description, which has 768 values (Appendix~\ref{app:training-settings}). The 768 values do not reach the agent as they are. A small network of two layers first reduces them to 192. These then join the features that the agent takes from its observation. The training script of ARM-FM's first author does this \cite{armfm_code}. The paper itself does not describe the network.

Even after this reduction, 192 values are many beside those of the observation. With MiniGrid's usual view of 7$\times$7 cells, the agent takes 1,024 features from its observation, so the RM state is 192 of 1,216 values, and in XLand-MiniGrid it is 192 of 448. The small network itself adds about 185,000 weights to learn. The states of one RM also have descriptions that lie close together. Closeness here is the cosine similarity of two embeddings, which is 1 when they point the same way. The mean similarity between the embedding of a state and that of its nearest state in the same RM is 0.873, over the 82 RMs with at least two states that agents trained on in the training experiment. Any two states of one RM score 0.823 on average, against 0.639 for two states of different RMs.

Future work could therefore reduce the size of these embeddings and make their values tell the states of one RM apart better.

For the labelling function, a first step could be a critic that also sees the labels on sample episodes. For the state descriptions, it could be a network with smaller layers or another compression technique, such as principal component analysis (PCA) or singular value decomposition (SVD). A PCA of the 2,141 distinct state embeddings of the 417 RMs that FARM and ARM-FM built in the generation experiment finds that 32 components hold 80\% of the variance of the 768 values, and about 70 hold 90\%.

\section{Reflection on the Sustainable Development Goals}
This thesis is a technical work in reinforcement learning. It proposes FARM, a method that builds RMs from tasks written in plain language, and measures the RMs and the agents that learn with them in simulated environments. It does not address a social or environmental problem directly, so most goals do not apply. Its contribution is a research method, not a deployed system.

\subsection{SDG 9: Industry, innovation and infrastructure}
The work is research, so this is the goal it relates to most. Within it, the relation is highest with target 9.5, which asks to enhance scientific research and upgrade technological capabilities. The thesis contributes a method to one research problem, the writing of rewards for reinforcement learning. It also extends the open tools it builds on in public forks of IBM nl2ltl and FL-AT (Appendix~\ref{app:implementation}).

The intended benefit is reward design that needs no expert in reinforcement learning. People who train agents, and people who know a task but not reinforcement learning, could then state the task and get an RM that generates the reward (Chapter~\ref{chap:introduction}). The method also uses small and cheap language models on purpose, which keeps it within reach of groups without large computing budgets. These benefits are intended. No study with users was run, so they are not measured.

\subsection{SDG 8: Decent work and economic growth}
Writing and tuning a reward function by hand takes time and needs expertise in both the task and reinforcement learning. Target 8.2 asks for higher productivity through technological upgrading and innovation. FARM builds the RM from the task with no edit by hand, so it could save part of that work. The relation is low because the time saved is not measured.

\subsection{SDG 12: Responsible consumption and production}
This goal relates to the computing that the method needs. Target 12.2 asks for the efficient use of natural resources. Of the five steps of FARM, only the generator and the critic call a language model, and both models are small. Steps 2 to 4, the compiler, call none, since fixed tools build the RM from the clauses. The training runs of the evaluation still use computing power. The energy used by the work was not measured, so the relation is low.

\subsection{SDG 16: Peace, justice and strong institutions}
The goal includes accountable and transparent institutions. The work relates to it only indirectly, through the transparency of automated systems. In FARM each clause states its part of the task in one plain sentence. The RM follows the chosen clauses exactly, and a broken hard rule ends the episode in the well (Chapter~\ref{chap:farm}). A person can therefore read what an agent is rewarded for, and check it against the task, before the agent trains. The model can still choose clauses that do not fit the task (Chapter~\ref{chap:limitations}). This transparency therefore does not replace a review of the clauses.

%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%                      DEVELOPMENT PROCESS AND AI USAGE                       %
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\chapter{Development process and AI usage}
\label{app:dev-process}
Most of this work is built and checked through a workflow with AI agents: the system, its methodology and experiments, and this document. This appendix is about the AI used to build the work, not about the two language models inside FARM. The generator and the critic belong to the method and are described in Chapter~\ref{chap:farm}.

\section{How the work started}
\label{app:dev-start}
The work goes through four periods, the last of which is the main development:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{RMs in practice}: the work starts by studying what RMs are, with examples built and tried by hand. A first implementation of counterfactual experiences for RMs (CRM) \cite{icarte2022reward} shows how RMs work and what can be done with them.
    \item \textbf{Aims}: the aim of bringing AI closer to what people want (Section~\ref{sec:proposal}) is set against what other work does with RMs and what is feasible.
    \item \textbf{A first prototype}: with the goal framed and ARM-FM as the reference, a base prototype of the whole pipeline is built. It joins IBM's nl2ltl and FL-AT with a small post-process and the critic.
    \item \textbf{Iteration}: from the prototype on, the work runs in cycles of investigating, changing and testing. AI agents take the largest part in this period.
\end{enumerate}

\section{The workflow with AI agents}
\label{app:ai-workflow}
In the iterative period the developer talks to one main agent, which orchestrates other agents behind it. A change goes through seven steps:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Grounding}: the conversation first settles what is to be done. It covers the ideas behind the change, its aim, the tools and methods, what can and cannot be done, and where in the code it belongs. Mock-ups and first-hand tests come at this point.
    \item \textbf{Requirements that everyone understands}: planning sets down every requirement, in enough detail that the authors, student and supervisor, understand exactly what will happen.
    \item \textbf{Feasibility}: small ad hoc experiments show that the change is achievable, that the algorithms work and that the ideas hold. They are run by hand, or by an agent with temporary code.
    \item \textbf{Plan}: once everything is settled, an agent writes a plan and every reference is checked. The agent that codes then knows what to do, how to do it and what result it must reach.
    \item \textbf{Code, review and live tests}: every change is reviewed, tested live on real runs and compared with the references.
    \item \textbf{Reviewers of different providers}: several coding agents from different providers review the code independently, from different angles, and try to break it. They check that the code is robust and clean and gives the right results.
    \item \textbf{The human last}: a person checks the work last. The person checks the outputs rather than every line of the code, and asks the reviewers for clearer evidence where it is missing. Anything unclear is redone, checked again or compared with the standards and the goals.
\end{itemize}
The same workflow applies to this document. Agents draft text and figures from the authors' notes and the measured results, and the authors review them.

\section{Development tools}
\label{app:dev-tools}
Besides the AI agents, the work relies on four general tools and two tools built for the project. The general tools are LaTeX for this document, Python for the system, Visual Studio Code as the editor of the code, and Orca, an application that orchestrates coding agents. The two tools built for the project are the task settings page and the RM viewer.

\subsection{The task settings page}
A small local web page gathers the human input and the settings of a run (Figure~\ref{fig:dev-web-ui}). It is \texttt{app/app.py}, built with the Python library Dash, and it does four things:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Input}: it loads the environment description in Markdown, or a task list that names an environment and its tasks, and takes the tasks to build.
    \item \textbf{Settings}: it sets the number of attempts, the thinking level and the optional steps, such as the critic, the state descriptions and the local embeddings.
    \item \textbf{Run}: it shows the log, the warnings and the errors. At the end it shows the RM built, drawn by the viewer of Section~\ref{app:viewer}.
    \item \textbf{Saved RMs}: it also loads a saved RM to view it.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\IfFileExists{images/devprocess/web-ui.png}{\includegraphics[width=\linewidth,height=0.42\textheight,keepaspectratio]{images/devprocess/web-ui.png}}{\tbd{screenshot of the local web page}}
\caption[The local web page for the settings of a run]{One local web page holds the settings of a run and shows its result. It holds the generator and critic settings, the environment description and the tasks, and shows the compiled RM with its state graph.}
\label{fig:dev-web-ui}
\end{figure}

\subsection{The RM viewer}
\label{app:viewer}
The tool that helps most is a viewer that makes RMs easier to read (Figure~\ref{fig:dev-viewer}). It reads the text of an RM and draws it as an interactive graph. The graph appears in the web page, or in a stand-alone HTML or SVG file made by the \texttt{render-rm} command, and both use the same drawing code. To keep the graph readable, the viewer compacts the RM in three ways:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{One arrow per pair of states}: all transitions between the same two states share one arrow. Its label gives how many transitions it holds and their reward, or \textit{mixed} when the rewards differ. Its colour tells whether it costs, gains or does neither, as in the drawings of this thesis (Section~\ref{app:drawing}).
    \item \textbf{Propositions on demand}: the guards stay off the graph. Clicking an arrow opens a panel that lists each transition it holds, with its reward and its guard, the guard split into one chip per proposition.
    \item \textbf{A fixed layout}: the graph runs from the start at the top to the final state at the bottom. The well gets a lane of its own on the left, and states move off the arrows they are not part of.
\end{itemize}
Hovering an arrow highlights it, and hovering a state highlights the arrows that leave it. Unlike the drawings of this thesis, the viewer also draws the implicit stay of each state, the step that matches none of its transitions, as a loop labelled \texttt{else} with reward 0.
