\chapter{Implementation details}
\label{app:implementation}

Chapter~\ref{chap:farm} describes the method, and this appendix holds the implementation behind it. It gives the contributions of FARM, how it uses each tool, the format of its RMs, the models and settings of the runs, and the training values of the benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:eval-training}).

\section{Contributions}
\label{app:contributions}
FARM makes fifteen additions to the tools it builds on, IBM nl2ltl, FL-AT and MONA, and to the generation step of ARM-FM:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Prompt}: it offers all eight Declare templates, with the meaning, kind and formula of each. It serves any environment, because the propositions and their meaning come from the environment description (Section~\ref{sec:step1}).
    \item \textbf{Restricted answer}: each clause is a template, its propositions, a priority and one plain sentence. Only the first three reach the compiler, so tasks of one schema should get RMs of one shape (Sections~\ref{sec:step1} and~\ref{sec:task-schemas}).
    \item \textbf{Priority}: each clause is hard or soft, as the preference cues of the task set it. IBM's templates have no such priority (Section~\ref{sec:step1}).
    \item \textbf{FL-AT fork}: it drops MONA's first state only when it is redundant, and it returns the state of every clause in each product state (Sections~\ref{sec:step2} and~\ref{sec:step3}).
    \item \textbf{Final states and well}: the hard clauses set the final states and one well, in place of the accepting states and the rewards of FL-AT (Section~\ref{sec:step4}).
    \item \textbf{Rewards}: they count the stages still owed. A step closer pays, a new thing to do costs, and reaching the final state pays \gain{$+10$} (Section~\ref{sec:step4}).
    \item \textbf{Re-arming and folding}: soft rules are re-armed, so that every new break is penalised, and soft Absence clauses are folded and add no state to the RM (Section~\ref{sec:recurrent}).
    \item \textbf{Hard-break price}: a hard break costs more than breaking soft rules at every step up to the step limit. This price applies when the task has a soft clause (Section~\ref{sec:well-charge}).
    \item \textbf{Take-back}: the edge into the well takes back the progress already paid (Section~\ref{sec:well-charge}).
    \item \textbf{Merge and minimisation}: MONA's start state is merged with its idle successor, and the RM is minimised once its rewards are set (Sections~\ref{sec:start-merge} and~\ref{sec:step4}).
    \item \textbf{Loop check}: it refuses any RM with a loop whose rewards add up to more than zero (Section~\ref{sec:step4}).
    \item \textbf{Order check}: it checks that an RM pays the five episodes in order, from finished to hard break, when FARM prepares it for training (Section~\ref{sec:step4}).
    \item \textbf{Critic}: it reviews the clauses, not the RM text, and never edits the RM. Its model must differ from the generator's (Section~\ref{sec:step5}).
    \item \textbf{Compiler clues}: the critic gets each rule in plain words, the hard clauses that the others imply, and the events that every way to finish includes (Section~\ref{sec:step5}).
    \item \textbf{RM format}: ARM-FM's format is extended with explicit final and rejecting states and one event expression per transition (Section~\ref{sec:output-format}).
\end{itemize}

\section{IBM nl2ltl}
In Step~1 the LTLf formula of each clause comes from the templates of IBM's nl2ltl. FARM uses them through a fork (\url{https://github.com/MiquelGomezCorral/nl2ltl}) of \url{https://github.com/IBM/nl2ltl} \cite{nl2ltl_code}. It reuses only the template classes and the LTLf builders. The GPT engine of nl2ltl is bypassed, and FARM supplies its own engine in its place. That engine asks a language model for a list of clauses under a JSON schema, and then passes each clause to the Declare template classes of nl2ltl, which build the LTLf formula.

FARM talks to four model providers, OpenAI, OpenCode, Antigravity and a local server (vLLM or Ollama), through one engine each, and every engine returns the clauses under the same JSON schema. The study of other language models calls Haiku 5.5 and GPT-6 Luna through their makers' command-line tools, outside these engines, and its open-weight models through the local engine (Section~\ref{sec:res-models}). The generator and the critic are configured separately.

The template classes of nl2ltl cover all eight Declare templates of Table~\ref{tab:templates}. The GPT prompt of nl2ltl, by contrast, lets the model choose only five: Existence, ExistenceTwo, Response, \mbox{ChainResponse} and RespondedExistence.

\section{FL-AT}
Steps 2 and 3 run on FL-AT. FARM uses it through a fork (\url{https://github.com/MiquelGomezCorral/Flat}) of \url{https://github.com/Jamidd/Flat} \cite{flat_code}, which makes four main changes. The last two are the ones that Section~\ref{sec:step2} presents:
\begin{itemize}
    \item MONA runs in a subprocess with temporary files, where the original wrote a file in the working folder through \texttt{os.system}. Its output is parsed strictly, so an automaton that repeats a guard out of one state stops the build with an error.
    \item Proposition names are renamed with regular expressions, where the original split strings and evaluated labels.
    \item MONA's first state is removed when it is redundant (Section~\ref{sec:redundant}).
    \item The state tuples of the product are exported, so that each product state tells which state every clause is in.
\end{itemize}

\section{The generator prompt}
The prompt of the generator has three parts: a system message, a user message and a catalogue of the Declare templates. The catalogue gives, for each template, its arguments, its kind (goal or rule), its meaning in English and its LTLf formula in IBM's syntax. It states that only Existence and ExistenceTwo are goals and that every rule holds when nothing happens. Beside the catalogue, a second table maps thirteen typical wordings to templates.

The instructions for the priority list the preference cues that make a clause soft: ``if possible'', ``preferably'', ``ideally'', ``try to'', ``when you can'', ``it would be nice'' and, for a ban, ``try not to''. Every other clause is hard. A cue covers the step it qualifies, not the order word that leads into that step.

A step the task asks for is \textbf{lone} when no hard order puts it before a later step that must happen, that is, when no hard Precedence chain links it to a later step that has a hard Existence. Every lone step gets its own hard Existence, and no other step does. Three kinds of step are lone:
\begin{itemize}
    \item A step asked for on its own;
    \item The last hard step of an order;
    \item A step whose later step is only preferred, since a soft Existence forces nothing.
\end{itemize}

An order is written as a hard Existence of its last step and one Precedence for each pair of consecutive steps. The \runex{sequence} example is built this way: Existence(\texttt{at\_goal}), Precedence(\texttt{has\_key}, \texttt{door\_open}) and Precedence(\texttt{door\_open}, \texttt{at\_goal}), with no \mbox{Precedence}(\texttt{has\_key}, \texttt{at\_goal}) (Table~\ref{tab:running}).
% NOTE-OPEN: paste the prompt text, or an excerpt, from app/src/prompts/compiler-rm/ (declare-templates.md, reward-ltlf-creator.system.md, reward-ltlf-creator.user.md) at the commit of the frozen run.

\section{The post-process}
Two parts of the post-process of Step~4 need more detail than Chapter~\ref{chap:farm} gives: the count of stages and the minimisation of the RM. The order check of Section~\ref{sec:reward-assignment} also needs the step limit, and without one it records the order as not checked.

\subsection{Stages}
The stages that a state $u$ owes, $\Phi(u)$ (Section~\ref{sec:reward-assignment}), are counted in one of two ways, depending on whether the RM has a final state.

With a final state, the hard part of $\Phi(u)$ is the fewest events that take $u$ to a final state. Each step on the way counts the propositions that its guard needs true, and at least one.

To this hard part, each soft clause, a soft goal included, adds its own local count. That count is the longest path from the clause's state to an accepting state in its own automaton, with every cycle collapsed into one node. Its length is then multiplied by the chain weight of the clause. The chain weight is one plus the weights of the clauses that the event closing the clause makes pending, so a rule that hands off to another is worth the chain behind it. A violated state counts zero.

Without a final state, every clause, hard or soft, uses this local count.

\subsection{Minimisation}
Minimisation makes the RM smaller. After the rewards are set, states that pay the same, lead to the same states on every input and end an episode alike are merged into one. Minimisation is a setting, on by default.

\section{Retries and the critic}
A rejection by the critic starts a new attempt (Section~\ref{sec:step5}). Failures of providers and tools, and inputs too large for the critic, follow rules of their own:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Generator provider}: a passing failure of the generator's provider, such as a timeout or a rate limit, uses no attempt. The same attempt is rerun, up to ten times in a row.
    \item \textbf{Invalid generator answer}: it uses an attempt, like a rejection.
    \item \textbf{Critic}: a failed critic request, or a critic answer that cannot be read, is repeated on the same candidate, up to ten times.
    \item \textbf{Limits and permanent errors}: when either limit of ten runs out, or when a provider gives a permanent error, the run ends.
    \item \textbf{MONA or FL-AT}: a failure of MONA, or of FL-AT's translation of the formula into MONA's input, also ends the run instead of using an attempt.
    \item \textbf{Large inputs}: an RM text longer than 200{,}000 characters reaches the critic as a summary. A task with more than twelve propositions gets no compiler clues.
\end{itemize}

\section{State descriptions}
A LARM, the output of ARM-FM, is an RM with the code of its labelling functions and a description of each state (Section~\ref{sec:sota-armfm}). FARM builds these two parts the way ARM-FM does. After the RM is built, a \textbf{tagger} writes one description per state. It sends the states in batches of twenty, with up to three requests per batch. At a later commit than the final run's, it also sees the information that the critic received (Section~\ref{app:models-settings}).

\section{RM format}
The format of FARM differs from that of ARM-FM (Listing~\ref{lst:armfm-doorkey}) in four points:
\begin{itemize}
    \item There are no \texttt{else} self-loops. A state with no matching transition stays where it is and pays \texttt{DEFAULT\_REWARD}, which is 0.
    \item The states with a special role are listed explicitly. \texttt{FINAL\_\allowbreak STATES} is always written and may be empty. \texttt{REJECTING\_\allowbreak STATES} is left out when the RM has no well.
    \item One event expression combines propositions with \texttt{!} and \texttt{\&}, such as \texttt{has\_key\&\allowbreak !door\_open}, instead of a separate predicate such as \texttt{not\_has\_key}.
    \item \texttt{REWARD\_FUNCTION} lists only the transitions with a reward other than 0.
\end{itemize}

\section{Drawing the RMs}
\label{app:drawing}
The drawings of RMs in this thesis colour each arrow by the sign of what it pays. In the simplified drawings, all transitions between two states share one arrow, which is drawn in one of three ways:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{as a cost} when its rewards are negative or zero;
    \item \textbf{as a gain} when they are positive or zero;
    \item \textbf{neutral} only when it holds both signs or only zeros.
\end{itemize}
The label \textit{mixed} means that the rewards differ, not that the colour is neutral. The drawings follow the RM viewer of Section~\ref{app:viewer}, without its \texttt{else} loops.

\section{Models and settings}
\label{app:models-settings}
Unless stated otherwise, generation uses FARM's default settings: the OpenCode provider, \texttt{deepseek-v4.1-flash} as generator, \texttt{glm-5.3-flash} as critic and thinking level max. The generator has three attempts by default, and the benchmark gives ten (Section~\ref{sec:step5}).

The final run builds FARM's RMs with the code at commit \texttt{809dfe3} of the repository of this thesis, and ARM-FM's at that commit or at nearby ones where ARM-FM's code is the same. Its scripted-plan check includes one correction from commit \texttt{57a91cb}, the rule for a plan that breaks only soft rules (Section~\ref{app:eval-machines}). The running examples (Appendix~\ref{app:running}) and two more groups of FARM's RMs were built later, with the code of commit \texttt{881b22d}. They are the RMs of the delivering and taking-orders tasks of the shop, with and without the siren, and the RM of the box-carry tasks with a soft rule. There the tagger also sees the information that the critic received. The tagger writes only the state descriptions, so at both commits FARM's clauses, formulas and RMs come from the same generator, critic, prompts and compiler.

\subsection{Training settings of the benchmark}
\label{app:training-settings}
Every trained task of the benchmark trains with PPO. Table~\ref{tab:app-ppo} lists the training values of most tasks, those of the first row of Table~\ref{tab:eval-training}.

The state description of the current RM state is embedded into 768 values with \texttt{nomic-embed-text}. A small network reduces them to 192 before they join the observation.

\chapter{Evaluation details}
\label{app:eval-details}
Chapter~\ref{chap:evaluation} presents each experiment and each measure of the evaluation in short. This appendix holds the detail behind them: one whole task, the environments of the benchmark and how the 60 trained tasks come from it, the setting of each group, and the exact rules of the measures. Its three sections follow the three sections of that chapter.

\section{The benchmark tasks}
\label{app:eval-cards}
A task is easiest to understand whole. Table~\ref{tab:eval-card} shows one task from the templates group, with the RM that FARM built for it in the generation experiment. Its scripted plans, like those of every task, are made of commands such as going to the key or picking it up. A command may find an object or call a fixed scripted policy of the environment, so the actions a plan takes depend on the layout and on environment events.

\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.2cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Part of the task} & \textbf{Content} \\ \midrule
ID & \card{tpl-existence-1-goal}, templates group \\
Environment & MiniGrid \env{DoorKey} 8$\times$8 \\
Text & \textit{Pick up the key, then reach the goal.} \\
Goal & Yes \\
Expected clauses & Precedence(\texttt{has\_key}, \texttt{at\_goal}), hard: $(\neg\,\texttt{at\_goal}\ \mathrm{U}\ \texttt{has\_key}) \lor \mathrm{G}\,\neg\,\texttt{at\_goal}$ \newline Existence(\texttt{at\_goal}), hard: $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{at\_goal}$ \\
Other accepted answer & The expected clauses plus Existence(\texttt{has\_key}), all hard: $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{has\_key}$ \\
Rewordings & Three, such as ``Grab the key first, then make your way to the goal.'' \\
Scripted plans & One that solves the task: pick up the key, open the door, go to the goal. Two wrong ones that break Existence(\texttt{at\_goal}): ``Takes the key and opens the door, then waits and never reaches the goal.'' and ``Never takes the key and never reaches the goal.'' \\

States u0 (initial), u1, u2 (the well) and u3 (final): \newline u0 $\to$ u1, key picked up, goal not reached: $+1$ \newline u0 $\to$ u2, goal reached without the key: $-10$ \newline u0 $\to$ u3, goal reached with the key: $+12$ \newline u1 $\to$ u3, goal reached: $+11$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\caption[One task of the templates group and its RM]{A task holds its text, the answers accepted for it and its scripted plans. This task of the templates group is copied from its task file. The last row adds the RM that FARM built for it in the generation experiment, with its states and one arrow per pair of states with its reward.}
\label{tab:eval-card}
\end{table}

The task's environment is one of the four levels of the base group. These levels are taken from the tasks of ARM-FM in MiniGrid, and each asks for a list of steps:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{\env{DoorKey}} 8$\times$8, the environment of FARM's running examples. The agent takes a key, unlocks a door and reaches the goal.
    \item \textbf{\env{UnlockPickup}}. The agent takes the key, unlocks the door and picks up a box in the other room.
    \item \textbf{\env{BlockedUnlockPickup}}. As in \env{UnlockPickup}, but a ball blocks the door and has to be moved first.
    \item \textbf{\env{KeyCorridor}} S4R3\footnote{In MiniGrid's \env{KeyCorridor} names, S gives the room size and R the number of rows, so S6R3 is a larger layout than S4R3.}. The agent explores the side rooms of a corridor to find the key of a locked room, then opens that room and picks up the object inside.
\end{itemize}
\noindent The other groups reuse these levels or bring the environments presented in Section~\ref{sec:eval-cards}. The pilot group runs \env{DoorKey} and \env{UnlockPickup} on one fixed layout, and the complex group runs \env{KeyCorridor} S4R3, \env{BlockedUnlockPickup} and the service room. The forced-start levels start with a step already done, such as the key already held. In the shop, the agent can take orders, cook at the stove, serve at the counter, pick up fallen food and toggle a siren. Two environments run on timings of their own. In the box-carry group, the agent holds a box that falls by itself at a random step from 2 to 8. In the zombie lanes, the zombies walk one cell every three steps, the agent kills a zombie by moving onto it, and the tasks are about the zombies it kills or lets through.

A task with several accepted answers lists the expected clauses first, and a separate check asks whether the generator favours that answer. Six of the accepted answers were added after the generator's outputs were read. Three are another fair reading of the task's wording, and three add a rule the task already implies, so the RM teaches the same behaviour. It compares how often the generator picks the first-listed answer with a choice at random among the task's accepted answers (Appendix~\ref{app:res-choice}).

The 60 tasks of the training experiment come from the 126 tasks of the task files in six ways:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Tasks left out.} Of the 18 tasks that are never trained because an idle agent keeps their hard rules, 17 are template tasks and one is a complex task.
    \item \textbf{Template tasks.} The templates group holds variants of the eight Declare templates as hard or soft rules, with or without a goal; some train and others are checked only as RMs. Each of the 28 trained template tasks takes one of the eight Declare templates of Table~\ref{tab:templates}, as a hard or a soft rule, and tests that it becomes an RM an agent can learn from.
    \item \textbf{Complex tasks.} The complex group tests a long sequence, rules alone, a goal with one or several rules, and rules that react to environment events. It trains five of its six tasks: a long sequence, a goal with one rule, a goal with several rules, and two tasks whose rules react to events in the service room.
    \item \textbf{Pilot tasks.} The two pilot tasks train two base tasks again, on one fixed layout and with a smaller budget.
    \item \textbf{Repeated shop tasks.} Five shop tasks train a second time, with the environment's reward added (Section~\ref{sec:eval-training}).
    \item \textbf{Step-by-step tasks.} Six use the text of the ARM-FM paper, the four base tasks and the two pilot tasks that repeat two of them. Five more tasks are of the same kind, and together they give the 11 step-by-step tasks.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Just as some tasks repeat another, some tasks share an RM. A task that reuses another task's RM can still train. For example, two tasks of the box-carry group train on the RM of another task in a changed version of its environment. The five repeated shop tasks also train on the RMs of the shop tasks they repeat. In all, seven tasks train on an RM built for another task, the two box-carry tasks and the five repeated shop tasks, so the 60 tasks run on 53 FARM RMs.

\section{Empirical evaluation in a real environment}
\label{app:eval-learning}
The training experiment of Section~\ref{sec:eval-learning} depends on a rebuilt baseline, on the setting of each group and on rules that the chapter gives in short. This section completes each of them.

From the first author's repository, the rebuilt ARM-FM takes the prompts and RMs of the MiniGrid tasks. Among the parts that neither the repository nor the paper's appendices document, the reconstruction covers how the later rounds of the generator and the critic are framed.

The groups differ in their setting, and Table~\ref{tab:eval-training} lists them. In most groups, the agent of FARM and ARM-FM sees its current RM state as a text embedding of the state's description, as in ARM-FM (Section~\ref{sec:sota-armfm}). In every judged episode, the agent takes its most likely action at every step. The pilot tasks, for example, play one judged episode, because on their one fixed layout every judged episode would be the same. The PPO values behind the first setting, such as its learning rate, are listed in Table~\ref{tab:app-ppo}, in Appendix~\ref{app:training-settings}.

Shop (11 tasks) & \multicolumn{5}{l}{shop setting; values in \texttt{experiments/benchmark/configs/}} \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\caption{The training setting of each group. Most tasks share the first setting. The pilot tasks train for fewer steps, and the shop tasks have a setting of their own.}
\label{tab:eval-training}
\end{table}

A judged episode that the method's RM ends too early fails, whatever the rules say. Too early means that the RM ends the episode in a final state within $\max(1, \lceil 0.1L \rceil)$ actions, where $L$ is the median length of the solving plan on the task's layouts, or 0 when there is none.

A run of a task with no goal and only soft rules passes when three things hold, per episode, against the idle agent:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Its soft breaks and its soft rules left unmet are both no higher than the idle agent's;
    \item At least one of them is lower;
    \item The labels the judge reads change more often than the idle agent's, which shows that the agent acts.
\end{itemize}

Besides the learned count, each task reports two means over its runs, its judged success and its soft breaks per episode, and a shop task adds a third, its orders served per episode. The learned counts themselves are given over the 60 tasks and over four kinds of subset, in this order:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item ARM-FM's own kind of task, the step-by-step tasks, against the 49 tasks of new kinds;
    \item Whether the task has a goal, outside the shop and the step-by-step tasks;
    \item Three kinds of task by what they ask: the step-by-step tasks, tasks with hard rules but no order of steps and no soft rule, and tasks with at least one soft rule;
    \item Each group.
\end{enumerate}

The comparison on the tasks of the ARM-FM paper goes beyond these 60 tasks, and Section~\ref{sec:res-armfm-paper} gives its results. The paper's tasks include \env{UnlockToUnlock}, a level outside the training experiment.

\section{Qualitative evaluation of the Reward Machines}
\label{app:eval-machines}
Some measures of the generation experiment in Section~\ref{sec:eval-machines} rest on rules that the chapter only names. This section gives them in the order of that section.

In the model review, the reviewer, Claude Opus 5.5, reads one sheet per RM, and each sheet shows three things:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The RM's clauses beside the task's accepted answers;
    \item The results of the build checks and of the scripted-plan check;
    \item For every state, its role, its transitions, the clauses still owed there and its description.
\end{itemize}
\noindent From these, the reviewer gives each RM one of three verdicts, ok, unsure or anomaly.

The thinking-level study counts more strictly on a task that asks for steps in order. Clauses that also make an earlier step a goal of its own pass the task, but they are not counted as right.

In the scripted-plan check, each plan plays in the task's environment and stops at the first final state or at the well. A plan that breaks only soft rules counts as penalised when it receives a negative reward before it stops, or when it ends like the solving plan on its layout and is paid less in total. The total adds the rewards up without discount. The three more conditions that an RM with no final state must meet are:
\begin{itemize}
    \item An idle plan that keeps every rule does not enter the well, and both it and the solving plan end with nothing owed;
    \item A wrong or idle plan that breaks a hard rule for good ends in the well;
    \item Every wrong plan pays less than the solving plan, both with and without discount.
\end{itemize}

\section{How the generator chooses among accepted answers}
\label{app:res-choice}

A task may accept several answers. Table~\ref{tab:app-res-choice} measures which one the generator picks. Its rows count three sets of runs from the thinking-level study and the generation experiment:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Thinking-level study}: the runs of the default pair in the study (Appendix~\ref{app:res-thinking}) on its 11 tasks with several accepted answers, three runs per task and level.
    \item \textbf{Ordered tasks of the generation experiment}: FARM's 12 RMs of the 6 ordered tasks among the 15 tasks of Section~\ref{sec:res-clauses}, one per wording.
    \item \textbf{Not-both and order tasks of the generation experiment}: FARM's 24 RMs of the other 9 tasks, one for each of their 9 texts and 15 rewordings. Five of these tasks have three rewordings and four have the text only.
\end{itemize}

A run is right when its final clauses match an accepted answer of its task, as Section~\ref{sec:eval-built} defines it, with a stricter rule for ordered tasks. An ordered task is in the chain form when it is written as the Existence of its last step plus one Precedence for each pair of steps in a row (Appendix~\ref{app:implementation}). In the first row, a cell is one task at one thinking level with its three runs, and only the cells with at least two right runs count. The row on tasks whose answer changes counts the not-both and order tasks with four wordings, each wording run once.

Two points qualify the table. First, as a baseline for the first row, two right runs would agree by chance in about 27\% of the cells, assuming two accepted answers. The study also shows that, from low to max, the thinking level does not change the pick.

Second, random choice among the accepted answers gives 48\% of the RMs of the 15 tasks, since one of the tasks accepts three answers. The one-half bar is therefore the slightly stricter test.

% Target: Appendix "Supporting results", section "Prompt versions before and after"
% (from Results, Section sec:res-thinking; decided lines RB4, RB21, Q8, and E6 of the Evaluation file).
\section{Prompt versions before and after}
\label{app:res-prompts}

Two changes made during development are measured here: the versions of the generator prompt, and the compiler clues added to the critic.

The generator prompt went through twelve versions, v0 to v11, measured over 2,430 runs on the tuning set. From the first version to the last, the share of ordered tasks written in the chain form rises from 52\% (22 of 42, 95\% Wilson interval 38\% to 67\%) to 81\% (68 of 84, 71\% to 88\%). That is short of the 90\% set as its target. Over the same versions, wrong RMs fall from 14\% (18 of 126 runs, 9\% to 22\%) to 12\% (31 of 252, 9\% to 17\%).

Adding the compiler clues to the critic raises the share of labelled defects that the critic flags at its first call from 30\% (74 of 246, 95\% Wilson interval 25\% to 36\%) to 56\% (23 of 41, 41\% to 70\%). The measure covers 38 items: 28 wrong clause sets holding 41 defects, and 10 right ones. Through the full loop of generator and critic on 46 tasks, right RMs rise from 77\% (213 of 276, 72\% to 82\%) to 85\% (39 of 46, 72\% to 92\%).

% PICK NOISE
By a rule of thumb, a move of less than about 10 points in the chain-form share counts here as noise. For example, one prompt version scored 75.0\% and, run again an hour later, 70.2\% (63 and 59 of 84). Two other versions differ by 12 points. The changes in wrong RMs, and in right RMs through the full loop, are other measures and were not repeated.

% Target: Appendix "Supporting results", section "Cards that name every step"
% (from Results, Section sec:res-learning; decided line RB25).
\section{Tasks that name every step}
\label{app:res-controls}

On the \env{BlockedUnlockPickup} level the agent moves a ball out of the way, takes a key, unlocks a door and picks up a box. Two tasks on this level name all four steps (Table~\ref{tab:app-res-controls}). FARM's RM pays every step on them, and its agents learn both tasks.

This fits the reading that Section~\ref{sec:res-60} gives of the seven tasks that only ARM-FM learns. They name one or two of the three steps before the box, never all three, so on them FARM's RM leaves little reward before the box. No run rewords one of those tasks to test the reading. On one of them, \card{tpl-not-co-existence-soft-1-goal}, ARM-FM's judged success on the three runs is 0.95, 0.97 and 0.91.

The seven tasks are \card{cx-goal-one-rule}, \card{tpl-absence-1-goal}, \card{tpl-absence-soft-1-goal}, \card{tpl-not-co-existence-1-goal}, \card{tpl-not-co-existence-soft-1-goal}, \card{tpl-precedence-soft-1-goal} and \card{tpl-responded-existence-2-goal}. On \card{cx-goal-one-rule}, \card{tpl-precedence-soft-1-goal} and \card{tpl-responded-existence-2-goal}, FARM's RM pays one step before the box. On the two absence tasks and the two not-both tasks, the key and the door appear only inside a rule, and FARM's RM gives no reward before the box, only a negative reward for breaking the rule.

\section{Box-carry tasks}
\label{app:res-boxcarry}

In the box-carry environments a box falls into a cell next to the agent, and the task asks the agent to pick it up again. Five tasks run in these environments. All three methods learn \card{cx-box-carry}, \card{tpl-chain-response-1-goal-carry} and \card{tpl-chain-response-1-nogoal-carry}, with a judged success of 1.00 on every run. On \card{tpl-chain-response-1-goal-carry}, a fall turns the agent to face the box. The tasks \card{tpl-chain-response-1-goal-carry-backturn} and \card{tpl-chain-response-1-goal-carry-noturn} have the text of \card{tpl-chain-response-1-goal-carry} (Table~\ref{tab:app-res-boxcarry}). On the first, a box that falls behind turns the agent only to its side. On the second, the agent keeps its direction after a fall, so some falls cannot be picked up at the next move. On this task, the agents also drop the box when the first fall can be picked up. The runs do not show why.

Table~\ref{tab:res-box} adds four tasks outside the 60, one with the coin-flip setup and three with the rule made soft. Its rows, from top to bottom, hold the hard tasks \card{tpl-chain-response-1-goal-carry}, \card{tpl-chain-response-1-goal-carry-backturn}, \card{tpl-chain-response-1-goal-carry-noturn} and \cardid{tpl-chain-response-1-goal-carry-half}, and, from the second row, the soft tasks \cardid{tpl-chain-response-soft-1-goal-carry-backturn}, \cardid{tpl-chain-response-soft-1-goal-carry-noturn} and \cardid{tpl-chain-response-soft-1-goal-carry-half}.

\section{Examples of ARM-FM's RMs not built}
\label{app:res-armfm-examples}

Table~\ref{tab:res-armfm-failures} sorts the 114 RMs that ARM-FM did not build by the first problem the scripted-plan check finds. This section gives one task for each problem and what goes wrong on its RM. Appendix~\ref{app:cards} gives the text of each task.
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{The start state is final.} On \card{keygift-hard}, every episode ends at step 0.
    \item \textbf{Breaking a soft rule costs nothing.} On \card{tpl-chain-response-soft-1-goal}, a plan that breaks the soft rule loses nothing for it.
    \item \textbf{Breaking a hard rule reaches a final state.} On \card{tpl-chain-response-2-nogoal-carry}, a plan that waits nine moves after the fall before picking the box up reaches the final state at step 21.
    \item \textbf{The solving plan ends in no final state.} On \card{tpl-responded-existence-1-nogoal-forced~p1}, the solving plan ends in u1.
    \item \textbf{The labelling function crashes.} On \card{tpl-precedence-1-goal~p3}, an assertion fails in the code of its labelling function.
\end{itemize}

\section{The ARM-FM paper's own tasks}
\label{app:res-armfm-paper}

Section~\ref{sec:res-armfm-paper} trains the three methods on tasks of the ARM-FM paper. This section lists those tasks, trains ARM-FM twice more, each time with one of its parts removed, says where every value is kept and ends with the results task by task. The tasks are of four kinds:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{\env{DoorKey}.} \textit{Use the key to open the door, then reach the goal.} At 5$\times$5 and 16$\times$16 the layout is either new every episode or one fixed layout, and at 8$\times$8 there is only the fixed layout.
    \item \textbf{\env{UnlockToUnlock}.} A BabyAI level: \textit{Pick up the first key, open its matching door, pick up the second key, open its matching door, and then pick up the target ball.}
    \item \textbf{X1 to X5.} Each is one ruleset of XLand-MiniGrid, that is, fixed rules that turn objects into others plus a goal, on a new layout every episode. X1 makes a purple star and then a blue pyramid, and ends with the pyramid one tile right of the agent on the map. X2 makes a white key and then a grey star, and ends the same way with the star. X3 makes a green star and then an orange star, which the agent picks up. X4 makes a brown pyramid and then a blue ball, and ends with a step forward next to the ball. X5 makes a pink ball and ends the same way as X1. X1 also trains alone, X1 to X3 form the 3-task mix and X1 to X5 the 5-task mix.
    \item \textbf{ZA, ZB and ZC.} ZA makes an orange star by putting a blue key down next to a purple ball, and ends with the agent picking the star up. ZB turns a blue star and ZC a blue key into a blue pyramid, and both end the same way as X1. Each method trains on ZA and ZB at once and is judged on ZC, following its own RM of ZC. ZC also trains alone.
\end{itemize}

ARM-FM trains twice more on the 5-task mix, with three runs each time. Without the RM's reward, the environment alone pays, and the RM only gives the agent its state and ends no episode. Without the embeddings, the RM still pays. The agent no longer sees the RM state, which it otherwise reads as a text embedding of the state's description. Removing the RM's reward lowers ARM-FM's mean on all five tasks. The intervals are apart only on X2, and only just, at 0.017 (95\% interval 0.000 to 0.055) against 0.087 (0.058 to 0.115) for the full ARM-FM. Removing the embeddings lowers the mean on four of the five tasks, with overlapping intervals, and raises it on X2.

The folder \texttt{docs/paper/armfm-baseline-results/} of the repository of this thesis keeps every task's text, its expected clauses with their LTLf and both RMs, and the value of every run.

\subsection{Per-task results}

Figure~\ref{fig:res-armfm-paper} gives each method's judged success on the six tasks of MiniGrid and BabyAI, and Figure~\ref{fig:res-armfm-xland} on the tasks of XLand-MiniGrid. Table~\ref{tab:res-armfm-paper} gives their means.

\begin{figure}[!htb]
\centering
\input{images/results/fig-res-armfm-paper.tex}
\caption[Judged success on the paper's MiniGrid tasks]{FARM matches or exceeds ARM-FM on \env{DoorKey} and \env{UnlockToUnlock}, and training with no RM fails the three tasks trained for 5M steps. Each bar is a method's mean judged success over three runs, each dot one run, and the whisker the 95\% Student $t$ interval. On \env{DoorKey} 16$\times$16 fixed, a light bar is the share of runs judged successful, with its count above it and its 95\% Wilson interval as a dashed whisker.}
\label{fig:res-armfm-paper}
\end{figure}

On the three tasks trained for 5M steps, FARM matches or exceeds ARM-FM, and the intervals of the two are apart only on \env{UnlockToUnlock}, by 0.005. On \env{DoorKey} 16$\times$16 fixed, the first three runs gave FARM 2 successes and ARM-FM 3. With five more runs each, both succeed in 6 of 8 (75\%, 95\% Wilson interval 41\% to 93\%).

\begin{figure}[!htb]
\centering
\input{images/results/fig-res-armfm-xland.tex}
\caption[Judged success on the XLand-MiniGrid tasks]{FARM's lead over ARM-FM grows with the number of tasks trained at once. Each bar is a method's mean judged success over three runs, each dot one run, and the whisker the 95\% Student $t$ interval. The number under a task is its number in XLand-MiniGrid. ZC is the task of the last check, shown here trained alone.}
\label{fig:res-armfm-xland}
\end{figure}

FARM's lead over ARM-FM grows with the number of tasks trained at once. The lead is 0.05 to 0.08 on the single tasks, 0.21 to 0.28 in the 3-task mix and 0.39 to 0.52 in the 5-task mix. On X1, for example, FARM's mean goes from 0.85 alone to 0.80 in the 5-task mix, and ARM-FM's from 0.77 to 0.29. The intervals of the two are apart on the five tasks of the 5-task mix and on X3 in the 3-task mix, and they overlap on the other tasks. Against its paper, ARM-FM's 0.77 on X1 alone (95\% interval 0.63 to 0.91) is not clearly below the paper's value of about 0.88. In the 3-task mix, its interval on one task, 0.23 to 0.81, reaches the paper's value of about 0.80. On the zero-shot check, FARM reaches 0.69 (95\% interval 0.21 to 1.00) and ARM-FM 0.58 (0.38 to 0.78), so the two intervals overlap.

Four of the paper's other MiniGrid tasks are among the 60 tasks of Section~\ref{sec:res-60}. They are \env{DoorKey} 8$\times$8 on new layouts (\card{base-doorkey}), \env{BlockedUnlockPickup} (\card{base-blocked-unlock-pickup}), \env{UnlockPickup} (\card{base-unlock-pickup}) and \env{KeyCorridor} (\card{base-key-corridor}). This thesis trains \env{KeyCorridor} with rooms of size 4, where the paper's Table 3 names size 3 and the code of its authors uses size 6. \env{DoorKey} 6$\times$6 and the paper's tasks in Craftium and Meta-World were not trained in this comparison.

%                            IBM TEMPLATE EXAMPLES                            %
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\chapter{IBM template examples}
\label{app:templates}
This appendix shows what the compiler builds from each template alone, once hard and once soft, sixteen RMs in all. Each is the RM of a task with that one clause. The appendix goes with Table~\ref{tab:templates} of Step~1, which gives the eight Declare templates with their LTLf formulas and how to read them.

Each row shows one template, hard on the left and soft on the right, and its caption gives the plain sentence of each version. As in Section~\ref{sec:run-step4}, a double circle marks the final state, the well is grey and implicit stays are not drawn. These RMs are small, so each arrow also carries its propositions next to its reward. The colour of an arrow follows the sign of its reward.

Three rules of Chapter~\ref{chap:farm} account for the shapes:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Only a hard goal gives a final state.} Existence and ExistenceTwo are the goals. Each gives an RM with a final state when it is hard, and neither does when it is soft. A hard rule alone gives a rule-only RM, with no final state (Section~\ref{sec:rule-only}).
    \item \textbf{A broken hard rule leads to the well.} Absence, Precedence, ChainResponse and NotCoExistence have a violated state, so their hard RMs have a well. The hard Absence RM is drawn on the left of panel~(c) of Figure~\ref{fig:templates-alone-1}. \mbox{RespondedExistence} and Response have no violated state, so their hard RMs have no well. In them the trigger costs at once, and the answer pays it back.
    \item \textbf{A soft clause never ends the episode.} No soft RM has a well. A soft rule that can break is re-armed, so every new break costs again. A soft Absence is folded, so it adds no state and its break is a loop that costs \loss{$-1$} (Section~\ref{sec:recurrent}).
\end{itemize}

\IfFileExists{images/templates/all.tex}{\input{images/templates/all.tex}}{\tbd{the 16 single-template RMs}}

\chapter{MONA automata cleaning}
\label{app:mona}
MONA's automata can hold two states that do no work in an RM, and FARM removes both (Section~\ref{sec:redundant}). This appendix draws the two removals on one clause. Figure~\ref{fig:mona-stages} follows Absence(\texttt{key\_lost}), with the LTLf formula $\neg\,\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{key\_lost}$, through both removals in three panels:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Panel~(a)}: the automaton that MONA prints. Its start state S0 moves to S1 on every input.
    \item \textbf{Panel~(b)}: the same automaton after FARM removes S0.
    \item \textbf{Panel~(c)}: the RM after Step~4. That step has merged the ``no step yet'' start S1 into its idle successor S2, which moves exactly like S1 on every input, and has set the reward of every edge.
\end{itemize}
Panels~(a) and~(b) draw every transition of the automaton, and double circles mark accepting states. Panel~(c) follows the convention of Section~\ref{sec:run-step4}. In it, the rejecting well u1 is grey and is entered with reward \loss{$-10$}, and the implicit stay of u0 is not drawn.

The \mbox{\runex{careful key}} (Section~\ref{sec:run-step4}) holds this clause as a soft one. The figure keeps it hard to show the well, so the RM of that example differs from panel~(c).

\chapter{Running examples}
\label{app:running}
Chapter~\ref{chap:farm} follows three running examples step by step (Section~\ref{sec:running-examples}), and this appendix gathers their final versions. For each it gives the task, the clauses with their LTLf formulas, and the final RM as a simplified drawing and as text. Two of these parts are already in Chapter~\ref{chap:farm} and are not repeated: the text of the \runex{sequence} RM in Listing~\ref{lst:farm-example1} and the drawing of the \runex{careful key} in Figure~\ref{fig:ex3-rm}.

All three ran on the description of the \env{DoorKey} $8 \times 8$ environment, at commit \texttt{881b22d}, where the tagger sees more than in the final run (Section~\ref{app:models-settings}). Each was built at the first attempt, and the critic accepted each with \texttt{NO CHANGES NEEDED}. No RM was edited by hand.

The drawings follow the convention of Section~\ref{sec:run-step4}, and the well is drawn in grey.

\section{Sequence}
The \runex{sequence} orders three steps, and every break of that order leads to the well.

\textbf{Task.} \textit{Pick up the key, then open the door, then reach the goal.}

\textbf{Clauses.} All three are hard:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Existence(\texttt{at\_goal}), $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{at\_goal}$;
    \item Precedence(\texttt{has\_key}, \texttt{door\_open}), $(\neg\texttt{door\_open}\ \mathrm{U}\ \texttt{has\_key}) \lor \mathrm{G}\,\neg\texttt{door\_open}$;
    \item Precedence(\texttt{door\_open}, \texttt{at\_goal}), $(\neg\texttt{at\_goal}\ \mathrm{U}\ \texttt{door\_open}) \lor \mathrm{G}\,\neg\texttt{at\_goal}$.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{RM.} Five states and eleven transitions (Figure~\ref{fig:ex1-rm}). Listing~\ref{lst:farm-example1} gives the text, and Section~\ref{sec:output-format} what each transition pays. The start u0 owes three stages, u1 (key) two, u3 (key and door open) one and the final state u4 none. The well is u2.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[farm graph,
    rm gain/.style={draw=green!50!black, text=green!40!black},
    rm cost/.style={draw=red!70!black, text=red!60!black}]
\node[state, initial]            (u0) at (0,0)    {u0};
\node[state]                     (u1) at (2.8,0)  {u1};
\node[state]                     (u3) at (5.6,0)  {u3};
\node[state, accepting]          (u4) at (8.4,0)  {u4};
\node[state, fill=gray!25]       (u2) at (1.4,-2.4) {u2};
\path[->]
    (u0) edge[rm gain] node[above] {$+1$ (1)} (u1)
    (u1) edge[rm gain] node[above] {$+1$ (1)} (u3)
    (u3) edge[rm gain] node[above] {$+11$ (1)} (u4)
    (u0) edge[rm gain, bend left=35] node[above] {$+2$ (1)} (u3)
    (u0) edge[rm gain, bend left=50] node[above] {$+13$ (1)} (u4)
    (u1) edge[rm gain, bend right=30] node[below] {$+12$ (1)} (u4)
    (u0) edge[rm cost] node[left] {$-10$ (4)} (u2)
    (u1) edge[rm cost] node[right] {$-11$ (1)} (u2);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption[RM of the \runex{sequence}]{The RM of the \runex{sequence} example.}
\label{fig:ex1-rm}
\end{figure}

\section{Dropped key}
\label{app:ex2}
The \runex{dropped key} joins the goal with a hard rule that reacts to an event: a dropped key must come back at the next step.

\textbf{Task.} \textit{Reach the goal, and if you drop the key, pick it up right away.}

\textbf{Clauses.} Both are hard: Existence(\texttt{at\_goal}), $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{at\_goal}$; ChainResponse(\texttt{key\_lost}, \texttt{has\_key}), $\mathrm{G}\,(\texttt{key\_lost} \rightarrow \mathrm{X}\,\texttt{has\_key})$.

\textbf{RM.} Five states and ten transitions, nine of them with a reward (Figure~\ref{fig:ex2-rm} and Listing~\ref{lst:ex2-rm}). Section~\ref{sec:rewards-example} below gives the stages of each state and what each transition pays.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[farm graph,
    rm gain/.style={draw=green!50!black, text=green!40!black},
    rm cost/.style={draw=red!70!black, text=red!60!black},
    rm zero/.style={draw=gray!70!black, text=gray!60!black}]
\node[state, initial]        (u0) at (0,0)      {u0};
\node[state]                 (u1) at (0,-3.2)   {u1};
\node[state, accepting]      (u2) at (3.0,-1.6) {u2};
\node[state]                 (u3) at (6.2,-1.6) {u3};
\node[state, fill=gray!25]   (u4) at (3.0,-4.8) {u4};
\path[->]
    (u0) edge[rm cost, bend right=20] node[left] {$-1$ (1)} (u1)
    (u1) edge[rm gain, bend right=20] node[right] {$+1$ (1)} (u0)
    (u0) edge[rm gain] node[above right] {$+11$ (1)} (u2)
    (u0) edge[rm zero, bend left=20] node[above] {$0$ (1)} (u3)
    (u1) edge[rm gain] node[below right] {$+12$ (1)} (u2)
    (u1) edge[rm gain, bend right=25] node[below, pos=0.4] {$+1$ (1)} (u3)
    (u1) edge[rm cost] node[below left] {$-10$ (2)} (u4)
    (u3) edge[rm gain] node[above] {$+11$ (1)} (u2)
    (u3) edge[rm cost] node[right] {$-10$ (1)} (u4);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption[RM of the \runex{dropped key}]{The RM of the \runex{dropped-key} example.}
\label{fig:ex2-rm}
\end{figure}

\noindent\begin{minipage}{\linewidth}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=rm,caption={The RM of the \runex{dropped-key} example.},label={lst:ex2-rm}]
REWARD_MACHINE:
STATES: u0, u1, u2, u3, u4
INITIAL_STATE: u0
FINAL_STATES: u2
REJECTING_STATES: u4
DEFAULT_REWARD: 0
TRANSITION_FUNCTION:
(u0, !at_goal&key_lost) -> u1
(u0, at_goal&!key_lost) -> u2
(u0, at_goal&key_lost) -> u3
(u1, !at_goal&!has_key) -> u4
(u1, !at_goal&has_key&!key_lost) -> u0
(u1, at_goal&!has_key) -> u4
(u1, at_goal&has_key&!key_lost) -> u2
(u1, at_goal&has_key&key_lost) -> u3
(u3, !has_key) -> u4
(u3, has_key&!key_lost) -> u2
REWARD_FUNCTION:
(u0, !at_goal&key_lost, u1) -> -1
(u0, at_goal&!key_lost, u2) -> 11
(u1, !at_goal&!has_key, u4) -> -10
(u1, !at_goal&has_key&!key_lost, u0) -> 1
(u1, at_goal&!has_key, u4) -> -10
(u1, at_goal&has_key&!key_lost, u2) -> 12
(u1, at_goal&has_key&key_lost, u3) -> 1
(u3, !has_key, u4) -> -10
(u3, has_key&!key_lost, u2) -> 11
\end{lstlisting}
\end{minipage}

\textbf{Product.} Figure~\ref{fig:run-product2} draws the product of the two clause automata as FL-AT builds it. Its seven states pair the two states of Existence with the four of ChainResponse. Step~4 turns them into five, because the start merges with its idle successor and the two states where the rule is broken become the well u4.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering

\node[state, initial] (p0) at (-1.6,0) {p0};

\node[state] (p2) at (2.2,-4.0) {p2};

    (p0) edge coordinate[pos=0.5] (e01) (p1)

    (p1) edge[bend left=25] coordinate[pos=0.5] (e13) (p3)

    (p3) edge[loop above] coordinate (e33) (p3)
    (p3) edge[bend right=15] coordinate[pos=0.5] (e34) (p4)

    (p4) edge coordinate[pos=0.5] (e45) (p5)
    (p5) edge[loop above] coordinate (e55) (p5)

\node[lab] at (e01) {$\neg\,\texttt{at\_goal} \land$ \\ $\neg\,\texttt{key\_lost}$ / $1$};
\node[lab] at (e02) {$\neg\,\texttt{at\_goal} \land$ \\ $\texttt{key\_lost}$ / $0$};
\node[lab, above left] at (e03) {$\texttt{at\_goal} \land$ \\ $\neg\,\texttt{key\_lost}$ / $2$};
\node[lab, above right] at (e04) {$\texttt{at\_goal} \land$ \\ $\texttt{key\_lost}$ / $1$};
\node[lab, above] at (e11) {$\neg\,\texttt{at\_goal} \land$ \\ $\neg\,\texttt{key\_lost}$ / $1$};

\subsection{The rewards step by step}
\label{sec:rewards-example}
The \runex{dropped-key} RM has no soft clause, but it shows four kinds of reward of Section~\ref{sec:reward-assignment}: progress, a step back, completion and a hard break. This part follows them step by step.

\textbf{What each state owes.} Table~\ref{tab:ex2-todo} gives the stages that each state still owes.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{State} & \textbf{Still to do} & \textbf{Stages} \\ \midrule
u0, start & reach the goal & 1 \\
u1 & pick up the key at the next step, then reach the goal & 2 \\
u3 & pick up the key at the next step & 1 \\
u2, final & nothing & 0 \\
u4, well & the key was not picked up in time & -- \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption[Stages owed by the states of the \runex{dropped key}]{The stages that each state of the \runex{dropped-key} RM still owes.}
\label{tab:ex2-todo}
\end{table}

\textbf{What each step pays.} A step pays the change in stages, and the step that reaches the final state adds the completion reward:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Dropping the key}, from u0 to u1, adds a stage and costs \loss{$-1$}.
    \item \textbf{Picking it up again}, from u1 back to u0, closes that stage and pays \gain{$+1$}.
    \item \textbf{Reaching the goal} pays \gain{$+10$} plus the progress of the step: \gain{$+11$} from u0, and \gain{$+12$} from u1 when the key comes back in the same step.
    \item \textbf{Reaching the goal in the step that drops the key}, from u0 to u3, closes the goal but owes the key, so it pays 0. Picking up the key in u3 then pays \gain{$+11$}.
    \item \textbf{A step without the key after a drop} breaks the hard rule. Every edge into the well u4 pays \loss{$-10$} and takes nothing back, because neither u1 nor u3 is ahead of the start (Section~\ref{sec:reward-assignment}, \emph{Taking progress back}).
\end{itemize}

\textbf{What five episodes total.} Table~\ref{tab:ex2-traces} adds up five traces.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Trace} & \textbf{Rewards} & \textbf{Total} \\ \midrule
Reach the goal & $11$ & $11$ \\
Drop the key, pick it up, reach the goal & $-1,\ 1,\ 11$ & $11$ \\
Drop the key, pick it up with the goal & $-1,\ 12$ & $11$ \\
Drop the key and leave it & $-1,\ -10$ & $-11$ \\
Nothing happens & -- & $0$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption[Rewards of the \runex{dropped key} on five traces]{The rewards that the \runex{dropped-key} RM pays on five traces. Idle steps pay nothing and are left out.}
\label{tab:ex2-traces}
\end{table}

Every trace that does the task totals \gain{$+11$}, with or without a drop. A drop that is repaired therefore costs nothing in the end. Leaving the key on the floor totals \loss{$-11$}, below a trace in which nothing happens. Finished, not started and hard break therefore keep the order of Section~\ref{sec:reward-assignment}.

\section{Careful key}
\label{app:ex3}
The \runex{careful key} joins the goal with a preference, which costs reward when it is broken but never ends the episode.

\textbf{Task.} \textit{Reach the goal, ideally without ever losing the key.}

\textbf{Clauses.} Existence(\texttt{at\_goal}), hard, $\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{at\_goal}$; Absence(\texttt{key\_lost}), soft, $\neg\,\mathrm{F}\,\texttt{key\_lost}$.

\textbf{RM.} Two states and three transitions (Listing~\ref{lst:ex3-rm}). Figure~\ref{fig:ex3-rm} draws it, and Section~\ref{sec:run-step4} explains what each transition pays. The RM has no well.

\noindent\begin{minipage}{\linewidth}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=rm,caption={The RM of the \runex{careful-key} example.},label={lst:ex3-rm}]
REWARD_MACHINE:
STATES: u0, u1
INITIAL_STATE: u0
FINAL_STATES: u1
DEFAULT_REWARD: 0
TRANSITION_FUNCTION:
(u0, !at_goal&key_lost) -> u0
(u0, at_goal&!key_lost) -> u1
(u0, at_goal&key_lost) -> u1
REWARD_FUNCTION:
(u0, !at_goal&key_lost, u0) -> -1
(u0, at_goal&!key_lost, u1) -> 11
(u0, at_goal&key_lost, u1) -> 10
\end{lstlisting}
\end{minipage}
